**Core principle:** Entities must disclose information that enables users of financial statements to evaluate: (1) the significance of financial instruments for the entity's financial position and performance; and (2) the nature and extent of risks arising from financial instruments, and how the entity manages those risks.

**Key rules:**
- **Classes of financial instruments:** Disclosures are made by class — a grouping appropriate to the nature of the information and characteristics of the instruments. Entities must reconcile classes to line items in the statement of financial position.
- **Structure of the standard, because the disclosure inventory hangs off it:**
  - **IFRS 7.6** — disclosures are made **by class** of financial instrument, grouped to suit the nature of the information and the characteristics of the instruments, with **sufficient information to permit reconciliation to the line items presented in the statement of financial position**. Classes are an entity-determined layer *below* the IFRS 9 measurement categories, not the same thing
  - **IFRS 7.7 (Part A)** — enable users to evaluate the **significance** of financial instruments for financial position and performance (paragraphs 8–30). **IFRS 7.31 onwards (Part B)** — the **nature and extent of risks** and how they are managed
  - **IFRS 7.8** — carrying amounts of each IFRS 9 category, on the face or in the notes; 8(a) now splits FVTPL financial assets into (i) those designated at initial recognition or subsequently under IFRS 9.6.7.1, (ii) those measured as such under the IFRS 9.3.3.5 election, (iii) those under the **IAS 32.33A** election, and (iv) those mandatorily at FVTPL. 8(b)–(d) are deleted
  - **IFRS 7.19A–19B (added by IFRS 18, from 2027)** — for **puttable instruments classified as equity** under IAS 32.16A–16B: summary quantitative data, the objectives, policies and processes for managing the repurchase or redemption obligation, and changes from the prior period. **19B** requires disclosure of the amount reclassified **into and out of** financial liabilities and equity, with the **timing and reason**, for IAS 32.16A–16B puttables and for IAS 32.16C–16D instruments imposing an obligation to deliver a pro rata share of net assets only on liquidation
- **Investments in equity instruments designated at FVOCI (IFRS 7.11A–11B, amended May 2024):** **11A** requires, **for each class** of designated investment: (a) which investments have been designated; (b) the reasons for using the presentation alternative; (c) fair value at the end of the period; (d) **dividends recognised during the period, split between investments derecognised during the period and those held at the end of the period**; (e) any transfers of the cumulative gain or loss **within equity** during the period, with the reason; and (f) the **fair value gain or loss presented in OCI** during the period, **again split between derecognised and held investments**. **11B** requires, for investments derecognised during the period: the reasons for disposal; fair value at the date of derecognition; the cumulative gain or loss on disposal; and transfers of the cumulative gain or loss within equity. The held/derecognised split is the new requirement — it exists precisely because FVOCI equity gains are never recycled, so users otherwise cannot see realised versus unrealised performance.
- **Financial instruments with contingent features (IFRS 7.20B–20D, new from 1 January 2026):**
  - **20B:** the 20C information is given **by class of financial assets measured at amortised cost or FVOCI, and by class of financial liabilities measured at amortised cost**. Note the symmetry — **liabilities are in scope**, which surprises issuers of sustainability-linked bonds: their own coupon step-ups trigger these disclosures even though the SPPI test applies only to assets
  - **20C:** to enable users to understand the effect of contractual terms that could change the amount of contractual cash flows on the occurrence or non-occurrence of a contingent event **that does not relate directly to changes in basic lending risks and costs**, disclose (a) a qualitative description of the nature of the contingent event; (b) quantitative information about the **possible changes to contractual cash flows** — for example, the **range** of possible changes; and (c) the **gross carrying amount** of financial assets and the **amortised cost** of financial liabilities subject to those terms
  - **20D:** the standard's own example is a class of financial liabilities at amortised cost whose contractual cash flows change if the entity achieves a **reduction in its carbon emissions**
  - **IFRS 19 equivalents:** the contingent-feature disclosures map to **IFRS 19.56A and 56B(a)–(c)**, and the nature-dependent electricity disclosures (below) to **IFRS 19.64A–64C**, for eligible subsidiaries electing the reduced-disclosure regime
- **Contracts referencing nature-dependent electricity (IFRS 7.5B–5D, 30A–30C, from 1 January 2026):**
  - **5B / 30A** — for contracts to **buy** nature-dependent electricity within IFRS 9.2.3A that fall **outside** IFRS 9 by virtue of IFRS 9.B2.7–B2.8, a **single note** must enable users to understand the effects on the amount, timing and uncertainty of future cash flows and on financial performance: (a) contractual features exposing the entity to variability in the underlying amount of electricity and to the risk of having to buy electricity in a delivery interval in which it cannot use it; (b) unrecognised commitments at the reporting date — estimated future cash flows from buying electricity in time bands the entity judges appropriate, and qualitative information about how the entity assesses whether a contract might become **onerous under IAS 37**; and (c) for the period, on the same basis used for the net-purchaser assessment, the costs of electricity purchased **showing separately how much was unused at the time of delivery**, the proceeds from sales of unused electricity, and the costs of purchases made to offset those sales
  - **5C / 30B** — for contracts designated in a cash flow hedging relationship under IFRS 9.6.10.1, disaggregate the IFRS 7.23A terms-and-conditions information **by risk category**
  - **5D / 30C** — cross-reference from the single note to any other notes carrying information about other nature-dependent electricity contracts
  - **IFRS 7.44OO:** if IFRS 9 comparatives are not restated, **no comparatives** are provided for the 30A–30C disclosures (nor for IFRS 19.64A–64C)
- **Offsetting disclosures (IFRS 7.13A–13F):**
  - **13A** — scope: **all** recognised financial instruments **set off** under **IAS 32.42**, *and* recognised financial instruments **subject to an enforceable master netting arrangement or similar agreement, irrespective of whether they are actually set off**. This second limb is the one preparers miss: an ISDA-governed derivative book that fails the IAS 32.42 offset test is still fully within 13A
  - **13C** — the quantitative table, **separately for recognised financial assets and for recognised financial liabilities**: (a) gross amounts; (b) amounts set off under IAS 32.42; (c) net amounts presented in the statement of financial position; (d) amounts subject to an enforceable master netting arrangement **not otherwise included in (b)**, split between (i) amounts relating to instruments that fail some or all of the IAS 32.42 criteria and (ii) financial collateral, including cash collateral; and (e) the net amount after deducting (d) from (c). Presented **in a tabular format** unless another format is more appropriate
  - **13D** — the 13C(d) amount for an instrument is **capped at its 13C(c) amount**. Over-disclosing collateral so that column (e) goes negative is a mechanical error this paragraph exists to prevent
  - **13E** — a **narrative description** of the rights of set-off underlying the 13C(d) amounts. **13F** — cross-reference between notes where the information is spread across more than one
- **Transfers of financial assets (IFRS 7.42A–42H):**
  - **42A** — three requirements routinely missed: these disclosures **supplement** the other IFRS 7 disclosures; they **must be presented in a single note**; and they must be given for **all** transferred assets not derecognised and for **any** continuing involvement existing at the reporting date, **irrespective of when the transfer occurred**. There is no cut-off for legacy securitisations, and scattering the disclosures across the securitisation, borrowings and derivatives notes does not comply
  - **42C** — **continuing involvement** means retaining any contractual right or obligation inherent in the transferred asset, or obtaining any new one. Expressly **not** continuing involvement: normal representations and warranties about fraudulent transfer and concepts of reasonableness, good faith and fair dealing; forward, option and other contracts to reacquire the asset at **fair value**; and a **pass-through arrangement** meeting IFRS 9.3.2.5(a)–(c)
  - **42D** — for assets **not derecognised in their entirety** (repos, securities lending, factoring with retained credit risk, most on-balance-sheet securitisations), disclose for **each class**: the nature of the transferred assets; the nature of the risks and rewards to which the entity remains exposed; the nature of the **relationship between the transferred assets and the associated liabilities**, including restrictions on use of the transferred assets; where counterparties have **recourse only to the transferred assets**, a schedule of the fair value of the assets, the fair value of the liabilities and the **net position**; and, where the entity recognises the assets only to the extent of its continuing involvement, the total carrying amount before transfer, the amount still recognised, and the carrying amount of the associated liabilities
  - **42E** — for assets **derecognised in their entirety** with continuing involvement, disclose **for each type of continuing involvement**: the carrying amount of the recognised assets and liabilities representing it **and the line items in which they sit**; their fair value; the amount that best represents the **maximum exposure to loss**, with information showing **how it is determined**; the undiscounted cash outflows that would or may be required to repurchase the assets; a **maturity analysis** of those outflows; and qualitative information explaining and supporting all of it
  - **42G** — additionally, for each type of continuing involvement: the **gain or loss recognised at the date of transfer** (B38); income and expenses recognised in the period and cumulatively; and, where total transfer proceeds are **unevenly distributed** across the period, when the greatest transfer activity occurred, the amount recognised from it, and the total proceeds in that part of the period — a specific anti-window-dressing requirement
  - **42H** — a **catch-all** for any additional information needed to meet the 42B objectives. Where a regulator has challenged securitisation or factoring disclosure, 42H is usually the hook
- **Significance disclosures (Part A):**
  - **Statement of financial position:** carrying amounts by IFRS 9 category (amortised cost, FVOCI — debt, FVOCI — equity, FVTPL); net gains/losses by category
  - **Fair value:** For instruments not measured at fair value, disclose fair value if practicable; for all instruments measured at fair value, apply the three-level hierarchy (IFRS 13)
  - **Fair value hierarchy (IFRS 13 cross-reference):**
    - Level 1: Quoted prices in active markets for identical assets/liabilities
    - Level 2: Inputs other than Level 1 that are observable (directly or indirectly)
    - Level 3: Unobservable inputs — entity's own assumptions
    - Disclose transfers between Level 1 and Level 2; explain Level 3 movements with reconciliation
  - **Income statement and equity:** Interest income/expense; fee income/expense; impairment losses; gains/losses on derecognition
  - **Hedge accounting disclosures:**
    - Risk management strategy and how it relates to the hedging instrument
    - Hedging instruments: amounts, carrying values, line items in balance sheet, change in fair value
    - Hedged items: carrying amounts, accumulated OCI (cost of hedging reserve), change in value used for hedge effectiveness
    - Hedge effectiveness: sources of ineffectiveness, amounts of ineffectiveness in P&L, hedge ratio
    - Reconciliation of hedging reserve movements
  - **Credit risk:**
    - Maximum exposure, collateral and credit enhancements held
    - For financial assets at amortised cost and FVOCI (debt): gross carrying amount, loss allowance, credit quality analysis using credit risk grades
    - **ECL staging disclosures:**
      - Stage 1 (12-month ECL): amounts at each balance sheet date
      - Stage 2 (lifetime ECL — not credit-impaired): amounts
      - Stage 3 (lifetime ECL — credit-impaired): amounts, interest revenue using the net carrying amount
      - POCI (purchased or originated credit-impaired): separate disclosure
    - Reconciliation of gross carrying amounts and loss allowances (opening to closing)
    - Write-off policy, collateral and other credit enhancements obtained during the period
    - Modifications: how modified assets were assessed, carrying amount of assets modified while in Stage 2/3
    - Assumptions and inputs used in ECL models (including forward-looking information used)
  - **Paragraph anchors for the credit risk disclosures:** `35A` scope of 35F–35N, with two carve-outs (for simplified-approach trade receivables, contract assets and lease receivables, 35J(a) applies only where the assets were **modified while more than 30 days past due**; and 35K(b) does not apply to lease receivables); `35B` the disclosure objective; **`35C` cross-referencing** to a management commentary or risk report is permitted **only** where that document is available to users **on the same terms and at the same time** as the financial statements — this is the paragraph that legitimises "Pillar 3 by cross-reference", and the condition banks most often fail; `35D`–`35E` aggregation judgement and additional information; **`35F`** credit risk management practices, including **explicitly** how and where the IFRS 9.5.5.10 low-credit-risk simplification is used and **whether and how the 30-days-past-due presumption has been rebutted**, plus the entity's **definitions of default and the reasons for selecting them**; **`35H`** the loss allowance reconciliation **in a table**, by class, showing separately 12-month ECL, lifetime ECL not credit-impaired, lifetime ECL credit-impaired, simplified-approach items and POCI assets — **including the total undiscounted expected credit losses at initial recognition on assets originated during the period**; `35I` how significant changes in gross carrying amounts contributed to changes in the loss allowance; `35J` modification disclosures; `35K` maximum exposure and collateral; **`35L` the contractual amount outstanding on assets written off during the period that remain subject to enforcement activity**; `35M` gross carrying amounts by **credit risk rating grade**; and `35N` permitting a **provision matrix** basis for simplified-approach items
  - **Liquidity risk:**
    - Maturity analysis of financial liabilities (including derivatives) showing contractual undiscounted cash flows
    - Description of how liquidity risk is managed
  - **Market risk:**
    - Sensitivity analysis for each type of market risk (interest rate, currency, equity price, commodity price): showing the effect on P&L and equity of a reasonably possible change
    - Alternatively: Value-at-Risk (VaR) analysis with specific disclosures about methodology and parameters
    - Concentrations of risk
  - **Capital disclosures:** objectives, policies and processes for managing capital. The requirement is cross-referenced, not located in IFRS 7: it sits in **IAS 1.134–136** until IFRS 18 is applied, and in **IFRS 18.126–129** for periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Quantitative and qualitative disclosures for credit risk, liquidity risk, and market risk
- Fair value disclosures including the level hierarchy and any transfers between levels
- Collateral information — pledged as security and held as collateral
- Compound financial instruments with multiple embedded derivatives
- Defaults and breaches of loan agreements
- Financial assets transferred but not derecognised (e.g., securitisations, repo agreements): nature of continuing involvement, carrying amounts, associated liabilities
- Netting arrangements: gross amounts, amounts offset, net amounts

**Common pitfalls:**
- Inadequate description of ECL methodology — disclosures must explain forward-looking macroeconomic factors, not just state that they are used
- Omitting the reconciliation of loss allowances (movement table from opening to closing)
- Insufficient granularity in the maturity analysis for liquidity risk — using contractual maturities only, without also providing behavioural maturities where relevant
- Failing to disclose Level 3 sensitivity analysis or the valuation techniques for Level 3 instruments
- Boilerplate sensitivity analyses that do not reflect actual risk exposure
- Not disclosing concentrations of credit risk by geography, industry, or counterparty where material
- Overlooking disclosures for transferred financial assets (repos, securities lending, factoring with retained risk)

- Confining the 13A–13F offsetting disclosures to instruments actually set off in the statement of financial position; 13A extends to **every** enforceable master netting arrangement, offset or not
- Reporting 13C(d) collateral in excess of the 13C(c) net amount, contrary to 13D
- Treating factoring with recourse or a repo as outside 42D because the asset "has been sold" — the test is derecognition, not legal transfer
- Disclosing 42E maximum exposure to loss without explaining **how it is determined**, which the paragraph requires
- Omitting the 42G uneven-distribution disclosure for entities that transact heavily around period ends
- Defining classes (IFRS 7.6) so broadly that they cannot be reconciled to statement of financial position line items, which the paragraph expressly requires
- Cross-referencing credit risk disclosures to a Pillar 3 or risk report that is **not** available on the same terms and at the same time as the financial statements (35C)
- Omitting the 35H requirement to disclose **total undiscounted ECL at initial recognition** on assets originated during the period, or 35L written-off amounts still subject to enforcement activity
- For FVOCI equity designations, giving aggregate OCI movements without the 11A(d)/11A(f) split between investments **held at period end** and those **derecognised during the period**
- Omitting the 20B–20D contingent-feature disclosures for **liabilities** — issuers of sustainability-linked bonds are in scope even though they run no SPPI test

**Related standards:** IFRS 9 (measurement and recognition — IFRS 7 disclosures mirror the IFRS 9 categories), IFRS 13 (fair value measurement and hierarchy), IFRS 18 (presentation from 2027; capital disclosures at IFRS 18.126–129), IFRS 19 (reduced-disclosure equivalents at IFRS 19.56A–56B and 64A–64C), IAS 1 (capital disclosures and presentation until IFRS 18 applies), IAS 7 (supplier finance arrangements — the companion disclosures at IAS 7.44F–44H), IAS 32 (financial instrument definitions; the offset criteria at IAS 32.42), IFRIC 16 (hedges of net investments)

---

## IFRS 8 — Operating Segments

**Scope:** Applies to the separate or consolidated financial statements of an entity whose debt or equity instruments are traded in a public market, or that files (or is in the process of filing) its financial statements with a securities commission or other regulatory organisation for the purpose of issuing any class of instrument in a public market. Parent entities that present consolidated financial statements apply IFRS 8 only at the consolidated level (not required for the separate financial statements). Entities that are not publicly accountable are encouraged but not required to apply IFRS 8.

**Status and amendments:** Issued **November 2006**, replacing IAS 14 *Segment Reporting*; effective for annual periods beginning on or after **1 January 2009**. EU-endorsed 21 November 2007, OJ 22 November 2007, EU effective 1 January 2009. Subsequently amended by *Annual Improvements 2010–2012* (December 2013 — adding the 22(aa) aggregation-judgement disclosure), *Amendments to References to the Conceptual Framework* (March 2018), *Disclosure of Accounting Policies* (February 2021), and consequentially by **IFRS 18** (April 2024, effective 1 January 2027). **No active IASB project affects IFRS 8.**

**IFRIC agenda decision — *Disclosure of Revenues and Expenses for Reportable Segments (IFRS 8)*.** Originally published **July 2024**; **updated in January 2026** to replace IAS 1 references with IFRS 18 references, and **finalised by the Committee in March 2026**. Two determinations that change practice:
- **Which specified amounts must be disclosed (IFRS 8.23(a)–(i)).** An entity discloses them for each reportable segment where they are either **included in the measure of segment profit or loss reviewed by the CODM — even if not separately provided to or reviewed by the CODM** — **or** regularly provided to the CODM even if **not** included in that measure. The frequent misreading, that an amount is only disclosable if the CODM looks at that line specifically, is wrong: inclusion in the reviewed measure is sufficient.
- **'Material items' in IFRS 8.23(f).** From 2027 the reference is to material items of income and expense disclosed under **IFRS 18.42** (previously IAS 1.97). In applying it, an entity uses the definition of **material information** in IFRS 18 Appendix A and IFRS 18.B2, assesses materiality **in the context of the financial statements taken as a whole**, applies **IFRS 18.41–43** on aggregation and disaggregation, considers **nature or magnitude — qualitative or quantitative factors, or both**, and considers the circumstances in **IFRS 18.B79**. The Committee expressly observed that **23(f) does not require every item of income and expense to be disclosed by segment**; judgement is applied against the IFRS 8 core principle. No standard-setting project was added.

**Core principle:** An entity must report financial and descriptive information about its reportable operating segments, based on internal information used by the chief operating decision maker (CODM) to allocate resources and assess performance — the **management approach**.

**Key rules:**
- **Operating segment definition:** A component of an entity that:
  1. Engages in business activities from which it may earn revenues and incur expenses
  2. Whose operating results are regularly reviewed by the CODM
  3. For which discrete financial information is available
  - A segment may be a single product/service line or encompass multiple products/services; start-up activities that have not yet earned revenues are still operating segments
- **CODM:** The function (not necessarily a single individual) that allocates resources and assesses segment performance — could be the CEO, executive committee, or board
- **The CODM judgement in full (IFRS 8.5, 7–10):**
  - **7** — the CODM is a **function, not a title**: allocating resources to, and assessing the performance of, the operating segments. Often the CEO or COO, but it may be a group of executive directors or others
  - **8** — where the CODM uses **more than one set** of segment information, other factors may identify the single set that constitutes the operating segments, including the nature of each component's business activities
  - **9** — the **segment manager** test. Where the paragraph 5 characteristics apply to more than one set of components but there is only one set for which **segment managers are held responsible**, that set constitutes the operating segments. Segment manager is likewise a function, not a title; the CODM may also be the segment manager for some segments
  - **10** — in a **matrix organisation** where the CODM regularly reviews both sets of components and financial information exists for both, the entity determines which set constitutes the operating segments **by reference to the core principle in paragraph 1**. There is no product-over-geography default
- **Aggregation (IFRS 8.12) requires three things, and the first is usually omitted:** aggregation must be **consistent with the core principle of IFRS 8**; the segments must have **similar economic characteristics**; and they must be similar in **each** of (a) the nature of the products and services, (b) the nature of the production processes, (c) the type or class of customer, (d) the methods used to distribute products or provide services, and (e) if applicable, the nature of the regulatory environment. Paragraph 12 offers the benchmark that segments with similar economic characteristics would be expected to have **similar long-term average gross margins**.
- **A separate and looser rule for sub-threshold segments (IFRS 8.14):** operating segments that individually fail the quantitative thresholds may be **combined** into one reportable segment only if they have **similar economic characteristics and share a majority of the paragraph 12 criteria**. "Majority", not "each" — the distinction between paragraph 12 and paragraph 14 is routinely collapsed.
- **Disclosure of the aggregation judgement (IFRS 8.22(aa)):** where aggregation has been used, disclose **the judgements management made in applying the paragraph 12 criteria**, a brief description of the segments aggregated, and **the economic indicators assessed** in concluding that they share similar economic characteristics. Generic boilerplate here is the single most common regulator challenge on IFRS 8.
- **Quantitative thresholds:** A segment is reportable if ANY of the following applies:
  - Revenue (including intersegment): ≥ 10% of combined revenue of all operating segments
  - Reported profit or loss: absolute amount ≥ 10% of the greater of (a) combined profits of all profitable segments or (b) combined losses of all loss-making segments
  - Assets: ≥ 10% of combined assets of all operating segments
- **75% test:** The total external revenue attributable to reportable segments must be at least 75% of total external revenue. If not met, additional segments must be identified as reportable (even if below the 10% thresholds) until the 75% threshold is reached.
- **All other segments (IFRS 8.16):** non-reportable segments and other business activities are combined in an "all other segments" category **separately from other reconciling items** in the paragraph 28 reconciliations, and **the sources of the revenue in that category must be described**.
- **Continuing significance (IFRS 8.17):** a segment reportable in the prior period that no longer meets the thresholds continues to be reported separately **if management judges it to be of continuing significance**.
- **Newly reportable segments (IFRS 8.18):** prior-period comparatives are **restated** as a separate segment even though the thresholds were not met then, unless the information is not available and the cost to develop it would be excessive.
- **Practical limit (IFRS 8.19):** above **ten** reportable segments, the entity **should consider whether a practical limit has been reached**.
- **Measurement:** Segment information is reported on the basis used internally by the CODM — this may not conform to IFRS. Differences between segment totals and IFRS-compliant totals must be reconciled.
- **Restatement on reorganisation (IFRS 8.29):** on a change in internal organisation that changes the composition of reportable segments, earlier-period corresponding information **including interim periods** is restated **unless the information is not available and the cost to develop it would be excessive** — and that determination is made **for each individual item of disclosure**, not for the restatement as a whole. The entity must disclose **whether** it has restated; if it has not, it discloses current-period segment information on both the old and the new basis. The same wording governs IFRS 8.18, 32 and 33.

**Disclosure requirements:**
- General information: factors used to identify operating segments, types of products/services in each segment
- **IFRS 8.23:** a measure of **profit or loss is required for every reportable segment**. A measure of **total assets and total liabilities** is required **only if such amounts are regularly provided to the CODM** — segment assets are not always required, and segment liabilities that *are* regularly provided must not be omitted
- **IFRS 8.24:** investment in equity-accounted associates and joint ventures, and additions to non-current assets other than financial instruments, deferred tax assets, net defined benefit assets and rights under insurance contracts, disclosed **if** included in the measure of segment assets reviewed by the CODM or otherwise regularly provided to it
- **IFRS 8.27 — an explanation of the measurements**, at minimum: the basis of accounting for intersegment transactions; the nature of the differences between segment measures and the entity's profit or loss before income tax and discontinued operations; the nature of the differences between segment and entity assets, and between segment and entity liabilities; the nature of any changes from prior periods in measurement methods and the effect of those changes; and the nature and effect of any **asymmetrical allocations** to segments
- Specific items if included in the measure of segment profit or loss reviewed by the CODM, or otherwise regularly provided to it: revenue (external and intersegment), depreciation and amortisation, **material items of income and expense (IAS 1.97 until IFRS 18 applies; IFRS 18.42 from 1 January 2027)**, interest income and expense (separately, unless the CODM reviews net interest), income tax expense, and significant non-cash items other than depreciation
- Basis of measurement for each reported segment measure
- **Reconciliations** (mandatory):
  - Total segment revenues to entity revenue
  - Total segment profit/loss to entity profit/loss before tax (and discontinued operations)
  - Total segment assets to entity assets
  - Total segment liabilities to entity liabilities (if segment liabilities reported)
  - Any other material segment items to corresponding entity amounts
- **Entity-wide disclosures** (required even for a single-segment entity):
  - Revenues from external customers: by product/service line
  - Revenues from external customers: by geographic area (country of domicile; any individual foreign country that is material)
  - Non-current assets: by geographic area (country of domicile; any individual material foreign country) — excludes financial instruments, deferred tax, post-employment benefit assets, and rights arising under insurance contracts
  - **Major customers (IFRS 8.34):** if any single external customer accounts for **≥ 10%** of the entity's revenue, disclose that fact, **the total revenue from each such customer**, and the segment(s) reporting it. The customer's identity and the per-segment split need not be disclosed. **A group of entities known to the reporting entity to be under common control is a single customer.** Whether a **government** and the entities known to be controlled by it constitute a single customer is a matter of **judgement**, informed by the degree of economic integration between them
  - The IFRS 8.32–33 entity-wide disclosures are subject to a "not available and the cost to develop would be excessive" exemption, **which must itself be disclosed** if used; IFRS 8.33 also requires disclosure of **the basis of attribution** of revenue to countries

**Common pitfalls:**
- Misidentifying the CODM — leads to incorrect segment identification; the CODM function is key, not the individual's title
- Aggregating segments that have different economic characteristics without sufficient justification
- Reporting segment information in a way that does not match what the CODM actually reviews — the management approach should be applied faithfully, not rationalised after the fact
- Failing to provide the 75% reconciliation test check before finalising the number of reportable segments
- Omitting entity-wide disclosures for a single-reportable-segment entity
- Not restating prior period comparatives when the segment structure changes
- Disclosing segment liabilities inconsistently — if regularly reviewed by CODM, they must be disclosed

- Aggregating without the **core principle** condition in IFRS 8.12, or applying paragraph 14's "majority of criteria" test to segments that do meet the quantitative thresholds
- Disclosing a bare statement that aggregated segments "have similar economic characteristics" without the **economic indicators assessed** required by IFRS 8.22(aa)
- Reading IFRS 8.23 as requiring disclosure only of amounts the CODM reviews line by line; the March 2026-finalised agenda decision holds that inclusion in the **reviewed measure of segment profit or loss** is itself sufficient
- From 2027, continuing to drive IFRS 8.23(f) off IAS 1.97 rather than **IFRS 18.42** and the IFRS 18.41–43 aggregation requirements — or swinging to the opposite error of disclosing **every** income and expense item by segment, which the agenda decision says 23(f) does not require
- Disclosing segment assets because they are "available" rather than because they are **regularly provided to the CODM** (IFRS 8.23), and inconsistently omitting segment liabilities that are
- Measuring the 75% test against combined segment revenue rather than **entity** revenue (IFRS 8.15)
- Treating a government's controlled entities as separate customers without performing the IFRS 8.34 judgement
- Making the IFRS 8.29 "not available / cost excessive" assessment wholesale rather than **item by item**, and failing to disclose whether restatement occurred

**Related standards:** IAS 1 (material items of income and expense until IFRS 18 applies), IFRS 18 (IFRS 18.42 material items; the aggregation and materiality framework at 41–43, Appendix A, B2 and B79; segment evidence of a specified main business activity at IFRS 18.B36), IAS 36 (goodwill allocation — the IFRS 8.5 **operating** segment ceiling, before aggregation), IFRS 6 (E&E impairment ceiling, also an operating segment), IFRS 3 (business combinations may create new segments), IFRS 5 (discontinued operations and their interaction with segment disclosures), IFRS 19 (IFRS 8 disclosures are **not** reduced for entities applying IFRS 19)

---

## IFRS 9 — Financial Instruments

**Scope:** Applies to all financial instruments except: interests in subsidiaries, associates, and joint ventures (IFRS 10, 11, IAS 27, IAS 28 — unless designated at FVTPL for venture capital/similar entities); rights and obligations in leases (IFRS 16 — except for impairment of lease receivables and derecognition of lease liabilities); employee benefit plan rights and obligations (IAS 19); equity instruments issued by the entity (IAS 32); insurance contracts (IFRS 17 — except for certain financial guarantee contracts meeting the IFRS 9 definition, at issuer's election); certain loan commitments (not measured at FVTPL); financial instruments, contracts, and obligations under share-based payment transactions (IFRS 2). Applies to financial guarantee contracts and commitments to provide a loan at below-market interest rate.

**Status and amendments:** Issued July 2014; effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2018. Amendments in force or issued as at 28 August 2026:

| Amendment | Issued | Effective (annual periods beginning on or after) | Early application | EU / UK status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Prepayment Features with Negative Compensation | Oct 2017 | 1 Jan 2019 | permitted | endorsed |
| Interest Rate Benchmark Reform (Phase 1) | Sep 2019 | 1 Jan 2020 | permitted | endorsed |
| Interest Rate Benchmark Reform — Phase 2 | Aug 2020 | 1 Jan 2021 | permitted | endorsed |
| **Amendments to the Classification and Measurement of Financial Instruments** (IFRS 9 and IFRS 7, and consequentially IFRS 19) | **30 May 2024** | **1 Jan 2026** | **permitted, but may be split — see below** | EU-endorsed 27 May 2025, OJ 28 May 2025; UK adopted 15 Apr 2025 |
| **Annual Improvements—Volume 11** (IFRS 9 and IFRS 7 items) | 18 Jul 2024 | **1 Jan 2026** | permitted | EU-endorsed 9 Jul 2025, OJ 10 Jul 2025; UK adopted 11 Feb 2025 |
| **Contracts Referencing Nature-dependent Electricity** (IFRS 9 and IFRS 7, and consequentially IFRS 19) | **18 Dec 2024** | **1 Jan 2026** | permitted | EU-endorsed 30 Jun 2025, OJ 1 Jul 2025; UK adopted 23 Jul 2025 |

**No further amendments to IFRS 9 had been issued as at 28 August 2026.** The last effective-date paragraph in the standard is IFRS 9.7.1.15; the IFRS 9 landing page's amendment history ends at December 2024; the EFRAG register of 23 July 2026 lists no IFRS 9 or IFRS 7 amendment endorsed after, or pending since, the December 2024 package; and every IFRS 9 project on the IASB work plan sits at a *future* milestone.

**The split early-application election — never write "early application permitted" without this qualification.** IFRS 9.7.1.13 gives an early adopter exactly **two** options and no others: (a) apply **all** the May 2024 amendments at the same time and disclose that fact; or (b) apply **only** the amendments to the Application Guidance to Section 4.1 (classification of financial assets) and disclose that fact. Two asymmetries follow. The **derecognition / electronic-payment amendments cannot be early-applied on their own** — they arrive only as part of option (a), so an entity wanting the electronic-payment relief early must also take the SPPI clarifications and reassess its whole classification population. And an entity taking option (b) **must nonetheless apply IFRS 7.20B, 20C and 20D at the same time** (IFRS 7.44LL): SPPI clarifications without the electronic-payment option is permitted; SPPI clarifications without the contingent-feature disclosures is not.

**Transition disclosure:** IFRS 9.7.2.49 requires, for financial assets whose measurement category changed on applying the amended Application Guidance to Section 4.1, disclosure of the previous measurement category and carrying amount immediately before applying the amendments. Relief from the IAS 8.28(f) quantitative transition information is given by IFRS 7.44MM (classification and measurement) and IFRS 7.44PP (nature-dependent electricity).

**Note on vocabulary:** "Stage 1 / Stage 2 / Stage 3" is market shorthand. **IFRS 9 never uses those words** — the requirements are expressed as lifetime ECL where credit risk has increased significantly since initial recognition (IFRS 9.5.5.3) and 12-month ECL otherwise (5.5.5). The staging language is used below because it is universal, but a preparer searching the standard for it will not find it, and IFRS 7.35H's populations are the ones the disclosures must tie to.

**Core principle:** Financial instruments must be classified and measured on the basis of the entity's business model for managing financial assets and the cash flow characteristics of the financial asset. Impairment is based on expected (not incurred) credit losses. Hedge accounting may be applied when hedging relationships meet specified criteria and reflect the entity's risk management activities.

---

### Part A: Classification and Measurement

#### Financial Assets — Classification Decision Tree

**Step 1 — SPPI Test (Solely Payments of Principal and Interest):**

Assess whether the contractual cash flows are solely payments of principal and interest on the principal amount outstanding.
- **Principal:** fair value of the financial asset at initial recognition
- **Interest:** consideration for the time value of money, credit risk, other basic lending risks (e.g., liquidity risk), and a profit margin
- **Fails SPPI if:**
  - Cash flows are linked to an equity index or commodity price
  - Leverage in cash flows (e.g., inverse floater, leveraged interest rate)
  - Non-recourse features that significantly limit cash flows (depends on instrument; some non-recourse pass the SPPI test)
  - Prepayment or extension terms that are not symmetric and not solely compensation for early termination (unless the *de minimis* or *solely compensation* exception applies)
  - Contractually linked instruments (CLOs, CDOs) unless assessed using a look-through to the underlying pool and the pool itself passes SPPI
- **Modified time value of money:** If interest is reset periodically but the reset frequency does not match the tenor, assess whether the difference is *de minimis* or whether the instrument behaves like a basic lending arrangement (benchmark comparison)
- **The de minimis and non-genuine screens (IFRS 9.B4.1.18):** a contractual cash flow characteristic does **not** affect classification if it could have only a *de minimis* effect. The test is applied **twice** — the possible effect **in each reporting period** *and* **cumulatively over the life of the instrument**. Separately, a characteristic that could have a more-than-de-minimis effect is still ignored if it is **not genuine** — affecting cash flows only on an event that is *extremely rare, highly abnormal and very unlikely to occur*. The two screens are sequential, not alternatives: de minimis first, then non-genuine.
- **Contingent features not related to basic lending risks — the "not significantly different" test (IFRS 9.B4.1.10A, added May 2024, effective 1 January 2026).** Where a contingent feature produces cash flows consistent with a basic lending arrangement **both before and after** the change, but the **nature of the trigger** does not relate directly to changes in basic lending risks and costs — the canonical case being an interest-rate step-down if the debtor hits a contractually specified carbon-emissions reduction — the asset is SPPI **if and only if, in all contractually possible scenarios, the contractual cash flows would not be significantly different from those of an otherwise identical instrument without the contingent feature.** The assessment may be qualitative; a quantitative assessment is required only where the qualitative one is inconclusive.
  - **This is the single most important 2026 change for lenders with ESG-linked loan books, and there is no ESG exemption.** The assessment is of *what the entity is being compensated for*, not *how much* (IFRS 9.B4.1.8A). Cash flows are inconsistent with a basic lending arrangement if they compensate for risks or market factors that are not basic lending risks or costs — a share of the debtor's revenue or profit is the standard example — **even if such terms are common in the entity's market**. An interest rate indexed to a market-determined carbon price index fails SPPI; an interest rate adjusted by a fixed number of basis points on the debtor achieving its own emissions target can pass, subject to the B4.1.10A test. Note also the direction-and-magnitude condition in B4.1.8A: a change is inconsistent with a basic lending arrangement if it is **not aligned with the direction and magnitude of the change in basic lending risks or costs**.
- **Prepayment and early-termination compensation (IFRS 9.B4.1.11(b), B4.1.12, B4.1.12A):** B4.1.12A (the October 2017 amendments) settles the negative-compensation question — irrespective of the event causing early termination, a party may **pay *or* receive** reasonable compensation, including where it is the party choosing to terminate. B4.1.12 gives the separate premium/discount relief: an asset acquired or originated at a premium or discount to par, whose prepayment amount substantially represents par plus accrued interest plus reasonable compensation, and whose prepayment feature has insignificant fair value at initial recognition, remains eligible for amortised cost or FVOCI.
- **Non-recourse (IFRS 9.B4.1.16, B4.1.16A, B4.1.17, B4.1.17A):** the May 2024 amendments enhanced the description of "non-recourse" and added B4.1.17A, a list of factors an entity **may** need to consider when looking through to the underlying assets. Non-recourse features do **not** automatically fail SPPI; the entity assesses the link between the particular underlying assets or cash flows and the instrument's contractual cash flows, and how that link is affected by other contractual arrangements such as subordinated debt or equity issued by the debtor.
- **Contractually linked instruments (IFRS 9.B4.1.20–B4.1.26):** CLI treatment applies **only** to transactions with non-recourse features where a **waterfall payment structure creates concentrations of credit risk and results in a disproportionate allocation of cash shortfalls** between tranches. **B4.1.20A (added May 2024)** carves out transactions that *appear* to have those characteristics but do not — for example secured lending arrangements where the sponsor holds the junior instrument in the SPE — which are assessed under the ordinary B4.1.7–B4.1.19 requirements instead. Applying the CLI look-through to structures that are not CLI transactions is a common error.
- **Subordination is not automatically fatal (IFRS 9.B4.1.19):** a subordinated instrument can still be SPPI where the debtor's non-payment is a breach of contract and the holder retains a contractual right to unpaid principal and interest even in bankruptcy. A general-creditor trade receivable qualifies.

**Step 2 — Business Model Test:**

Determine the business model in which the financial asset is held:

| Business Model | Description | Classification |
|---|---|---|
| Hold to collect | Objective is to collect contractual cash flows; sales are incidental | Amortised cost (if SPPI passes) |
| Hold to collect and sell | Objective is both collecting cash flows and selling assets | FVOCI — debt instrument (if SPPI passes) |
| Other / trading / residual | Objective is primarily to realise fair value gains; any other model | FVTPL (regardless of SPPI) |

- Business model is assessed at a portfolio/group level, not instrument-by-instrument
- Frequency, volume, and timing of past sales are considered; a single sale does not change the business model; a change of business model is rare and requires significant changes in operations
- **The fair value option for financial assets (IFRS 9.4.1.5):** even if SPPI passes and the business model is hold-to-collect, an entity may designate a financial asset at FVTPL if doing so eliminates or significantly reduces an accounting mismatch — irrevocable at initial recognition. This is the **fair value option**, not an OCI election; the OCI election for equity instruments (IFRS 9.5.7.5) is a separate mechanic, described in the next bullet.
- **Equity instruments:** Cannot pass SPPI; always FVTPL unless irrevocably elected at initial recognition to measure at FVOCI (only for equity instruments that are not held for trading); FVOCI equity gains/losses are never recycled to P&L — only dividend income is recognised in P&L

#### Financial Assets — Measurement Summary

| Category | Initial measurement | Subsequent measurement | Impairment |
|---|---|---|---|
| Amortised cost | Fair value + directly attributable transaction costs | EIR method; foreign exchange gains/losses in P&L | ECL (3-stage model) |
| FVOCI — debt | Fair value + transaction costs | Fair value; changes in OCI; EIR, FX, and impairment in P&L | ECL in P&L; OCI balance is cumulative FV change less P&L items |
| FVTPL | Fair value (transaction costs expensed) | Fair value; all changes in P&L | No separate impairment |
| FVOCI — equity (irrevocable election) | Fair value + transaction costs | Fair value; changes in OCI (never recycled); dividends in P&L | No separate impairment |

#### Financial Liabilities — Classification

- **Default:** Amortised cost using EIR method
- **FVTPL (mandatory):** Held for trading (including derivatives that are liabilities); contingent consideration in a business combination (IFRS 3)
- **FVTPL (designated — Fair Value Option):** Permitted if: eliminates or significantly reduces accounting mismatch; financial liability is part of a group managed on a FV basis with a documented strategy; hybrid instrument with embedded derivative
  - **Own credit risk:** For FVO liabilities, changes in fair value attributable to the entity's own credit risk are presented in OCI (not P&L), unless this creates or enlarges an accounting mismatch — then all changes remain in P&L
- **Financial guarantee contracts (IFRS 9.4.2.1(c)):** measured at the **higher of** (i) the loss allowance determined under **IFRS 9 Section 5.5** and (ii) the amount initially recognised (5.1.1) less, when appropriate, the cumulative income recognised **in accordance with the principles of IFRS 15**. Neither IAS 37 nor straight-line "amortisation" is the measurement basis.
- **Embedded derivatives:** Only separate from a host financial liability that is not measured at FVTPL if: (a) economic characteristics not closely related to host; (b) a separate instrument with the same terms would be a derivative; (c) host is not measured at FVTPL. For financial asset hosts — the whole instrument is assessed for SPPI; if fails SPPI it is FVTPL in its entirety.

#### Derecognition of Financial Liabilities Settled through an Electronic Payment System

**An accounting policy option, added May 2024, effective 1 January 2026 (IFRS 9.B3.3.8–B3.3.10).** The default remains settlement-date accounting (IFRS 9.B3.1.2A). By election, an entity may deem a financial liability (or part of one) that will be settled in cash using an electronic payment system to be **discharged before the settlement date**, if and only if it has initiated the payment instruction **and all three** of the following hold:

1. the entity has **no ability** to withdraw, stop or cancel the payment instruction;
2. the entity has **no practical ability to access the cash** to be used for settlement as a result of the instruction; and
3. **settlement risk associated with the electronic payment system is insignificant.**

**B3.3.9** defines (3): settlement risk is insignificant where completion follows a standard administrative process and the time between initiation and cash delivery is short. It is **not** insignificant if completion is subject to the entity's own ability to deliver cash on the settlement date. **B3.3.10:** the election must be applied **to all settlements made through the same electronic payment system** — a per-system policy, not a per-transaction choice.

Three practical points: the relief is for **financial liabilities only** (a receivable settled electronically is still derecognised on the settlement date); it matters most at period-end for entities running large payment runs on the last working day, because it moves the trade-payables/cash reduction into the earlier period and changes reported liquidity ratios; and the trigger is *initiation of the payment instruction*, not authorisation, with the "no ability to withdraw" test being legal and operational rather than a policy statement.

#### Contracts Referencing Nature-dependent Electricity (December 2024 amendments)

Issued 18 December 2024; effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after **1 January 2026**, early application permitted with disclosure of that fact (IFRS 9.7.1.15); EU-endorsed 30 June 2025, OJ 1 July 2025. Arose from an IFRS IC request about applying the own-use scope exception in IFRS 9.2.4 to physical power purchase agreements for wind and solar generation.

- **Definition (IFRS 9.2.3A):** contracts that expose an entity to variability in the **underlying amount** of electricity because the source of generation depends on **uncontrollable natural conditions** (e.g. weather). The definition covers both physical contracts to buy or sell such electricity and financial instruments that reference it. **IFRS 9.2.3B prohibits applying the relief by analogy** to any other contract, item or transaction — a deliberately sealed carve-out.
- **Own use — the net purchaser test (IFRS 9.2.8, B2.7–B2.8).** The problem: pay-as-produced PPAs oblige the buyer to take and pay for whatever is generated, and market design may force it to sell unused volumes, which on a literal reading defeats the own-use exception and drags the contract into IFRS 9 as a derivative at FVTPL.
  - **B2.7:** such forced sales of unused electricity are **not necessarily inconsistent** with the contract being held in accordance with expected usage requirements. The entity is within own-use if it **has been, and expects to be, a net purchaser of electricity for the contract period** — buying sufficient electricity to offset the sales of any unused electricity **in the same market in which it sold that electricity**.
  - **B2.8:** the assessment uses reasonable and supportable information available without undue cost or effort about past, current and expected future electricity transactions over a **"reasonable amount of time"**, identified by reference to the seasonal cycle of the natural conditions and the entity's own operating cycle. For the **backward-looking** leg, "a reasonable amount of time" **shall not exceed 12 months**. This is a look-back cap, not a look-forward cap.
  - The IFRS 9.2.5 irrevocable FVTPL designation for own-use contracts remains available.
- **Hedge accounting (IFRS 9.6.10.1–6.10.2).** Where such a contract is designated as the **hedging instrument** in a hedge of forecast electricity transactions, the entity may designate as the hedged item a **variable nominal amount** of forecast electricity transactions aligned with the variable amount of nature-dependent electricity expected to be delivered by the referenced generation facility. **6.10.2** supplies the critical presumption: where the cash flows of the hedging instrument are conditional on the occurrence of the designated forecast transaction, that forecast transaction is **presumed to be highly probable** for IFRS 9.6.3.3 purposes. Without it a variable-volume renewable PPA could almost never support a cash flow hedge.
- **Transition (IFRS 9.7.2.51–7.2.53):** the scope and own-use paragraphs are applied **retrospectively using facts and circumstances at the date of initial application**, which must be the beginning of a reporting period (not necessarily an annual one); prior periods need not be restated, and restatement is permitted **only if achievable without hindsight**, with any difference taken to opening retained earnings. At the date of initial application an entity may **irrevocably designate at FVTPL under 2.5** a contract that would fall outside IFRS 9 as a result of B2.7–B2.8 — a one-off re-opening of a window otherwise available only at contract inception. The hedge-accounting paragraphs apply **prospectively** to relationships designated on or after that date, and an existing relationship using such an instrument may be discontinued at that date **provided the same instrument is redesignated** under 6.10.1–6.10.2.
- Disclosure: see IFRS 7.5B–5D and 30A–30C above.

---

### Part B: Impairment — Expected Credit Loss (ECL) Model

**Scope of ECL:** Applies to financial assets at amortised cost, financial assets at FVOCI (debt), lease receivables (IFRS 16), contract assets (IFRS 15), loan commitments not at FVTPL, and financial guarantee contracts.

#### The 3-Stage ECL Model

**Stage 1 — Performing (12-month ECL):**
- Apply to financial instruments where credit risk has **not significantly increased** since initial recognition (or where credit risk is low at reporting date)
- Recognise a loss allowance equal to **12-month ECL** — the portion of lifetime ECL from default events expected within 12 months of the reporting date
- Interest revenue: calculated on **gross carrying amount** (i.e., before deducting the loss allowance) using the EIR

**Stage 2 — Underperforming (Lifetime ECL — not credit-impaired):**
- Apply when credit risk has **significantly increased** since initial recognition but the asset is **not yet credit-impaired**
- Recognise **lifetime ECL** — expected credit losses from all possible default events over the expected life of the instrument
- Interest revenue: still calculated on **gross carrying amount**
- **Significant increase in credit risk (SICR):** Assessed based on change in risk of default (not change in loss given default or exposure):
  - **SICR is assessed on the change in the risk of a *default occurring*, not on the change in the amount of ECL (IFRS 9.5.5.9).** The comparison is the risk of a default occurring at the reporting date against the risk of a default occurring **at the date of initial recognition** — not against the prior reporting date, and not against an absolute threshold
  - **The 30-day presumption (IFRS 9.5.5.11) — three points routinely got wrong:** it is a **rebuttable** presumption of SICR, rebuttable on reasonable and supportable information available without undue cost or effort; where forward-looking information is available without undue cost or effort, an entity **cannot rely solely on past-due information** — past-due status is a backstop, not a primary trigger; and where the entity determines SICR occurred **before** 30 days past due, the presumption simply does not apply. It is a floor on lateness, not a permission to wait
  - **Do not confuse it with the 90-day default backstop (IFRS 9.B5.5.37).** The entity must apply a definition of default **consistent with the one used for internal credit risk management**, considering qualitative indicators such as financial covenants. There is a **rebuttable presumption that default does not occur later than 90 days past due**, rebuttable only with reasonable and supportable information that a more lagging criterion is more appropriate. 30 days = SICR presumption; 90 days = default presumption; different paragraphs, different rebuttal evidence, different consequences
  - Quantitative: change in PD (probability of default) — compare current lifetime PD to lifetime PD at origination
  - Qualitative indicators: significant deterioration in external credit ratings, past-due status (30 days past due = rebuttable presumption of SICR), watchlist, covenant breach, forbearance
  - Forward-looking information must be incorporated
  - **Low credit risk simplification (IFRS 9.5.5.10, B5.5.22–B5.5.24):** an entity **may assume** no significant increase in credit risk if the instrument is determined to have low credit risk **at the reporting date** (e.g. investment-grade equivalent). It is optional and applied instrument by instrument; it is a simplification, not a category. The IASB's July 2024 post-implementation review **declined** to add more prescriptive guidance on its use, on the ground that this would make the requirements rule-based and reduce alignment with internal credit risk management

**Stage 3 — Credit-impaired (Lifetime ECL — credit-impaired):**
- Apply when the financial asset is **credit-impaired** — one or more events have had a detrimental impact on estimated future cash flows
- **Credit impairment indicators (objective evidence of impairment):**
  - Significant financial difficulty of issuer or borrower
  - Breach of contract (default or delinquency in interest/principal payments)
  - Lender granting a concession that would not otherwise be considered (forbearance/restructuring)
  - Probability that borrower will enter bankruptcy or financial reorganisation
  - Disappearance of an active market for the financial asset
  - Purchase or origination at a deep discount that reflects incurred credit losses (POCI)
- Recognise **lifetime ECL**
- Interest revenue: calculated on **net carrying amount** (gross carrying amount less loss allowance) using the EIR — net interest approach

**POCI (Purchased or Originated Credit-Impaired) Assets:**
- Credit-impaired at initial recognition
- Interest revenue calculated using a credit-adjusted EIR applied to the amortised cost (net of allowance)
- Only cumulative changes in lifetime ECL since initial recognition are recognised as a loss allowance (changes can be positive — loss allowance gain)
- Never migrates to Stage 1 or 2

#### ECL Measurement — Key Inputs

- **PD (Probability of Default):** Probability of default over the measurement horizon
- **LGD (Loss Given Default):** 1 minus the recovery rate; reflects collateral, security, and seniority
- **EAD (Exposure at Default):** Contractual cash flows outstanding at the time of default, including expected drawdowns on undrawn commitments
- **ECL = PD × LGD × EAD** (simplified; discounted at the original EIR or credit-adjusted EIR for POCI)
- **Forward-looking information:** ECL must reflect unbiased, probability-weighted information about past events, current conditions, and forecasts of future economic conditions. Entities must consider multiple economic scenarios; point-in-time PDs (not through-the-cycle)
- **Measurement objective (IFRS 9.5.5.17):** ECL must reflect (a) an unbiased and **probability-weighted** amount determined by evaluating a **range of possible outcomes**; (b) the time value of money; and (c) reasonable and supportable information available without undue cost or effort about past events, current conditions and **forecasts of future economic conditions**. A single "most likely" scenario does not satisfy (a) where the loss distribution is non-linear.
- **Credit loss definition (IFRS 9 Appendix A; B5.5.28–B5.5.29):** the difference between all contractual cash flows due and all cash flows the entity **expects to receive**, discounted at the relevant effective interest rate. **Cash shortfalls from any cause are included** — the IASB rejected in the 2024 PIR the argument that only credit-deterioration-driven shortfalls should be captured. It also stressed the **ordering**: apply the other IFRS 9 requirements to establish the gross carrying amount first, then apply the impairment requirements to that gross carrying amount.
- **Maximum period (IFRS 9.5.5.19–5.5.20).** The maximum period is the maximum **contractual** period (including extension options) over which the entity is exposed to credit risk — not a longer behavioural period. **5.5.20 is the exception preparers miss:** for instruments containing **both a loan and an undrawn commitment** where the entity's contractual ability to demand repayment and cancel the commitment does not limit its exposure to the contractual notice period — revolving credit facilities, credit cards, overdrafts — ECL is measured over the period the entity is **actually** exposed. This is the source of most bank ECL model complexity for retail revolvers.
- **Collective assessment (IFRS 9.B5.5.5):** instruments may be grouped on the basis of **shared credit risk characteristics** for SICR and loss-allowance purposes. Grouping must be re-assessed as characteristics change; static pools are a recurring audit finding.
- **The IASB's own worked examples (IFRS 9 Illustrative Examples)** are the fastest route through a disputed judgement: Example 1 significant increase in credit risk; Example 4 public investment-grade bond (the low-credit-risk exemption in operation); Example 5 responsiveness to changes in credit risk; Example 6 comparison to maximum initial credit risk; Example 7 counterparty assessment; Example 8 12-month ECL using an explicit PD approach; Example 9 12-month ECL using a loss-rate approach; **Example 10 revolving credit facilities** (the 5.5.20 exception worked through); Example 11 modification of contractual cash flows; **Example 12 provision matrix**; Example 13 debt instrument at FVOCI.
- **Write-offs:** When there is no reasonable expectation of recovery; reduce gross carrying amount; may still pursue enforcement activity
- **Simplified approach — exactly who is eligible (IFRS 9.5.5.15–5.5.16):**

  | Instrument | Lifetime ECL from day 1 |
  |---|---|
  | Trade receivables / contract assets in scope of IFRS 15 with **no** significant financing component (or where the entity uses the IFRS 15.63 practical expedient) | **Mandatory** |
  | Trade receivables / contract assets in scope of IFRS 15 **with** a significant financing component | **Accounting policy election** |
  | Lease receivables in scope of IFRS 16 | **Accounting policy election** |

  Note that **contract assets without a significant financing component are mandatory, not elective**. Where elected, the policy must be applied to **all** such items, but **may be applied separately to trade receivables and to contract assets**, and separately to **finance and operating lease receivables**; IFRS 9.5.5.16 confirms the three populations are selected independently. Loan receivables, debt securities and intercompany loans are **never** eligible for the simplified approach.
  - A **provision matrix** is a practical expedient, not the approach: IFRS 9.B5.5.35 permits practical expedients only where consistent with the principles in 5.5.17, and IFRS 7.35N permits the credit-risk-exposure disclosure for simplified-approach items to be given on a provision-matrix basis

#### Modification vs Derecognition

**Financial assets:**
- **Modification without derecognition (IFRS 9.5.4.3):** recalculate the **gross carrying amount** as the present value of the renegotiated or modified contractual cash flows discounted at the **original effective interest rate** (or the credit-adjusted EIR for POCI assets, or the revised EIR under 6.5.10 where IBOR-reform relief applies) and recognise a **modification gain or loss in profit or loss**. **Any costs or fees incurred adjust the carrying amount of the modified asset and are amortised over its remaining term** — they do not go to profit or loss.
- **IFRS 9 contains no 10% test for financial assets.** The substantiality assessment for assets is qualitative — a change in currency, a change in counterparty, the introduction of features that cause the asset to fail SPPI. Applying the liability 10% test to assets by analogy is a common and incorrect shortcut.
- **SICR after modification (IFRS 9.5.5.12):** for a modified-but-not-derecognised asset, SICR is assessed by comparing **(a) the risk of a default occurring at the reporting date based on the modified contractual terms** with **(b) the risk of a default occurring at initial recognition based on the *original, unmodified* terms.** The origination benchmark is **not reset** by the modification. The past-due counter restarts on the new payment schedule, but the SICR comparison point does not move.
- **Derecognition on modification** gives a new asset; if it is credit-impaired on initial recognition, POCI accounting applies (credit-adjusted EIR; only cumulative changes in lifetime ECL since initial recognition recognised as a loss allowance — IFRS 9.5.5.13–5.5.14, which expressly permits recognising an **impairment gain** even where lifetime ECL is below the amount built into the initial estimate).

**Financial liabilities:**
- **Trigger (IFRS 9.3.3.2):** an exchange between an existing borrower and lender of debt instruments with **substantially different terms**, or a **substantial modification** of the terms of an existing financial liability **or a part of it**, is accounted for as an extinguishment of the old liability and recognition of a new one — "*whether or not attributable to the financial difficulty of the debtor*". **There is no troubled-debt-restructuring carve-out in IFRS.**
- **The 10 per cent test (IFRS 9.B3.3.6):** terms are substantially different if the discounted present value of the cash flows under the **new** terms, **including any fees paid net of any fees received**, discounted at the **original effective interest rate**, is at least **10 per cent different** from the discounted present value of the **remaining** cash flows of the original liability. In determining fees paid net of fees received, a **borrower includes only fees paid or received between the borrower and the lender**, including amounts paid or received by either party on the other's behalf. **Third-party fees — legal costs, adviser arrangement fees — are excluded from the test**, even though they may still be accounted for as costs.
- **The qualitative companion.** The 10% test is necessary but not, in practice, sufficient: a change in currency, the introduction of an equity-conversion feature, or a switch between fixed and floating can be substantial even where the threshold is not breached. Treat a qualitative assessment as a required companion. *(This is established practice rather than an explicit IFRS 9 requirement — and the IASB is codifying it: see the pipeline table below.)*
- **Accounting for the two outcomes (IFRS 9.3.3.3, B3.3.6A):**
  - *Extinguishment*: the difference between the carrying amount extinguished and the consideration paid (including non-cash assets transferred or liabilities assumed) goes to **profit or loss**; **any costs or fees incurred are recognised as part of that gain or loss**
  - *Non-substantial modification*: **any costs or fees incurred adjust the carrying amount of the liability and are amortised over the remaining term of the modified liability.** The standard does not, in that paragraph, address the day-one catch-up on remeasuring the liability at the original EIR — but the accepted reading, and the one the IASB is codifying in the Amortised Cost Measurement project, is that the liability is remeasured to the present value of the modified cash flows at the **original** EIR with an **immediate gain or loss in profit or loss**. This is the single most frequently mis-applied requirement in the standard: many entities incorrectly adjust the EIR prospectively instead
- **Partial repurchase (IFRS 9.3.3.4):** allocate the previous carrying amount between the part that continues to be recognised and the part derecognised on the basis of **relative fair values at the repurchase date**; the difference between the carrying amount allocated to the part derecognised and the consideration paid for it goes to profit or loss.
- **Debtor released but retaining a guarantee (IFRS 9.B3.3.7):** recognise a new financial liability at the **fair value of the guarantee obligation**, and a gain or loss equal to proceeds paid less (carrying amount of the original liability less fair value of the new liability).
- **Own liability held in a consolidated fund (IFRS 9.3.3.5):** an entity operating an investment fund, or issuing direct-participating insurance contracts and holding the underlying items, may **irrevocably elect, instrument by instrument**, not to derecognise its own financial liability repurchased into that fund, and instead account for the repurchased instrument as if it were a financial asset at FVTPL.

---

### Part C: Hedge Accounting

**Overview:** Hedge accounting is a voluntary election that aligns the timing of gain/loss recognition in profit or loss for a hedging instrument with the hedged item. IFRS 9 hedge accounting more closely reflects risk management activities than IAS 39.

**Two distinct IAS 39 elections survive, and they are independent — do not describe them as one:**
1. **IFRS 9.7.2.21 — the full IAS 39 hedge accounting policy choice.** On first applying IFRS 9, an entity **may choose as its accounting policy to continue to apply the hedge accounting requirements of IAS 39 instead of Chapter 6 of IFRS 9**. The policy must be applied to **all** its hedging relationships — it is all-or-nothing. An entity making this choice must also apply **IFRIC 16 without** the amendments that conform that Interpretation to Chapter 6.
2. **IFRS 9.6.1.3 — the macro fair value hedge carve-out.** *For a fair value hedge of the interest rate exposure of a portfolio of financial assets or financial liabilities, and only for such a hedge*, an entity may apply the IAS 39 requirements while otherwise applying IFRS 9 Chapter 6 to all its other hedges. It must then also apply the IAS 39 portfolio fair value hedge requirements and designate as the hedged item a portion that is a **currency amount** (IAS 39.81A, 89A, AG114–AG132).

**What electing IFRS 9.6.1.3 commits you to.** IAS 39.AG114(a)–(i) is a **prescribed nine-step procedure**, not a principle: identify the portfolio; analyse it into repricing time periods based on **expected, not contractual, repricing dates** (AG117 requires the expected repricing date to be estimated at inception **and throughout the term**); decide the amount to hedge and designate **an amount of assets *or* liabilities — expressly not a net amount** (AG114(c)); designate the hedged interest rate risk; designate hedging instruments per repricing period; assess prospective effectiveness; measure the change in fair value of the hedged item attributable to the hedged risk and recognise it in **one of the two IAS 39.89A balance sheet line items** without allocating it to individual assets or liabilities; measure the hedging instrument's fair value change; and recognise the difference as ineffectiveness in profit or loss. **The core-deposit constraint is AG118:** because IFRS 13 requires the fair value of a financial liability with a demand feature to be **not less than the amount payable on demand**, discounted from the first date it could be required to be paid, such an item **cannot qualify for fair value hedge accounting for any time period beyond the shortest period in which the holder can demand payment**. Demand deposits therefore cannot form part of the designated hedged item under the IASB text — they can only be used to *determine* the amount of assets designated. AG126 governs ineffectiveness where estimates of repricing dates change; AG132 permits migrating a portfolio hedge previously accounted for as a cash flow hedge into this approach, prospectively.

**The EU "carve-out" is a separate thing.** IFRS 9.6.1.3 and the IAS 39 requirements above are the **IASB's** portfolio-hedge accommodation, available to every IFRS reporter. The European Union separately endorsed a **modified version of IAS 39** that relaxes two of those constraints — the AG114(c) prohibition on designating a net amount, and the AG118 demand-deposit limitation — for core-deposit hedging. That modification is a European Commission endorsement carve-out, **not** an IASB provision, and an entity asserting compliance with IFRS Accounting Standards **as issued by the IASB** cannot rely on it. It remains in force: EFRAG's Endorsement Status Report of 23 July 2026 still records the hedge accounting part of IAS 39 as not endorsed.

**Neither election is removed automatically by the macro hedging project.** The December 2025 *Risk Mitigation Accounting* Exposure Draft **asks respondents** whether the development of risk mitigation accounting justifies withdrawing IAS 39's remaining hedge accounting requirements. **No decision had been taken at 28 August 2026**, and both elections remain fully available.


#### Qualifying Criteria (all must be met)

**IFRS 9.6.4.1** — a hedging relationship qualifies only if **all** of the following are met:

1. The hedging relationship consists only of eligible hedging instruments and eligible hedged items
2. Formal designation and documentation of the hedging relationship at inception: the entity's risk management objective and strategy, the type of hedge, identification of the hedging instrument and the hedged item, the hedged risk, and how effectiveness will be assessed
3. The hedging relationship meets all of the following effectiveness requirements (IFRS 9.6.4.1(c)):
   - Economic relationship exists between the hedging instrument and hedged item (both respond to the same risk)
   - Credit risk does not dominate the value changes from the economic relationship
   - The hedge ratio reflects the actual quantities used for risk management (not set to achieve an accounting outcome)

#### Eligible Hedging Instruments

- Derivatives measured at FVTPL (the most common)
- Non-derivative financial assets or liabilities at FVTPL, but only for currency risk hedges
- A proportion (not a time component alone) of a derivative may be designated
- Options: can designate intrinsic value only (time value excluded from designation and deferred in OCI as "cost of hedging") — avoids P&L volatility from time value decay; similarly, forward element of forward contracts and currency basis spread may be excluded

#### Eligible Hedged Items

- A recognised asset or liability
- An unrecognised firm commitment (except for foreign currency risk)
- A highly probable forecast transaction
- A net investment in a foreign operation (IAS 21)
- An aggregated exposure (a hedged item combined with a derivative)
- A group of items (if individually eligible); a net position may be designated only for foreign currency risk (with certain conditions)
- Portions: a risk component (if separately identifiable and reliably measurable), a nominal portion, a layer component of a hedged item

#### Types of Hedge Relationships

**Fair Value Hedge:**
- Hedges exposure to changes in fair value of a recognised asset/liability or firm commitment attributable to a particular risk
- **Accounting:** Changes in fair value of the hedging instrument are recognised in P&L; the carrying amount of the hedged item is adjusted for the gain/loss attributable to the hedged risk (fair value hedge adjustment) and that adjustment also recognised in P&L
- When the hedge is discontinued, any remaining fair value hedge adjustment on the hedged item (if it is a financial instrument at amortised cost) is amortised to P&L over the remaining life using the EIR

**Cash Flow Hedge:**
- Hedges exposure to variability in cash flows attributable to a particular risk associated with a recognised asset/liability or highly probable forecast transaction
- **Accounting:**
  - The effective portion of the gain/loss on the hedging instrument is recognised in OCI (the cash flow hedge reserve — CFHR)
  - The ineffective portion is recognised immediately in P&L
  - When the hedged forecast transaction occurs and affects P&L: reclassify the CFHR to P&L (recycling)
  - If the hedged item results in the recognition of a non-financial asset or liability: either reclassify when the asset/liability affects P&L, or include in the initial cost of the non-financial asset/liability (basis adjustment — applicable only for non-financial items; irrevocable election at inception)
  - If the forecast transaction is no longer expected to occur: reclassify CFHR to P&L immediately

**Net Investment Hedge:**
- Hedges the currency risk on a net investment in a foreign operation
- **Accounting:** Effective portion of the hedging instrument's gain/loss recognised in OCI (translation reserve); reclassified to P&L only on disposal or partial disposal of the foreign operation
- Can use monetary items (e.g., intercompany loans) or derivatives as hedging instruments; borrowing in the functional currency of the foreign operation also qualifies

#### Hedge Effectiveness Assessment

- Prospective: assess whether an economic relationship exists; done at inception and on an ongoing basis (at a minimum at each reporting date)
- Hedge ratio: must be the same as that actually used for risk management (IFRS 9.6.4.1(c)(iii))
- **Rebalancing (IFRS 9.6.5.5, B6.5.7–B6.5.21)** is required **only** where the relationship ceases to meet the hedge-ratio effectiveness requirement **and the risk management objective for that designated relationship remains the same**.
