Rebalancing is a **continuation** of the existing relationship, not a re-designation. If the risk management objective has changed, rebalancing is not available and discontinuation is mandatory
- **Discontinuation (IFRS 9.6.5.6)** is prospective, and applies **only** when the relationship (or part of it) ceases to meet the qualifying criteria **after taking any rebalancing into account**. Voluntary de-designation is prohibited. Two specific non-events: a **replacement or rollover** of the hedging instrument is not an expiration or termination if it is part of, and consistent with, the documented risk management objective; and **novation to a central counterparty** required by law or regulation is not a termination, provided both parties clear with the **same** central counterparty and other changes are limited to those necessary to effect the replacement
- **Costs of hedging (IFRS 9.6.5.15–6.5.16):** option time value, the forward element of a forward contract, and foreign currency basis spread may each be **excluded from the designation**, with the deferred amounts held in a separate component of equity (the "cost of hedging reserve") and released differently for transaction-related and time-period-related hedged items

#### IFRIC Interpretations Relevant to IFRS 9

- **IFRIC 16 — Hedges of a Net Investment in a Foreign Operation:** clarifies (1) the nature of the hedged risk — hedge accounting applies **only** to the exchange difference between the functional currency of the foreign operation and the functional currency of **a parent**, never a presentation currency; (2) that the hedging instrument may be held by **any entity in the group**, and the hedged risk may be designated against the functional currency of **any** parent in the chain; and (3) what is reclassified to profit or loss on disposal. The same net assets may qualify **only once** in the consolidated financial statements. An entity electing IAS 39 hedge accounting under IFRS 9.7.2.21 applies IFRIC 16 **without** the IFRS 9 conforming amendments.
- **IFRIC 19 — Extinguishing Financial Liabilities with Equity Instruments.** More than a one-liner, because the partial-extinguishment mechanics are where preparers go wrong:
  - **IFRIC 19.5** — equity instruments issued to a creditor are **consideration paid** for IFRS 9.3.3.3 purposes; the liability is removed when, and only when, it is extinguished under IFRS 9.3.3.1
  - **IFRIC 19.6–19.7** — measure the equity instruments at **their own fair value**; only if that cannot be reliably measured, measure them to reflect the fair value of the **liability extinguished** — and in measuring that liability's fair value where it includes a demand feature, **IFRS 13.47 is not applied**
  - **IFRIC 19.8 and 19.10 — partial extinguishment.** The entity must assess whether part of the consideration relates to a **modification of the liability that remains outstanding** and, if so, **allocate** the consideration between the part extinguished and the part remaining. The amount allocated to the remaining liability then feeds the **IFRS 9.B3.3.6 substantial-modification assessment** of that remaining liability; if it is substantially modified, extinguishment accounting applies to it too. Debt-for-equity restructurings routinely collapse this two-stage test into one
  - **IFRIC 19.9, 19.11** — the gain or loss is the difference between the carrying amount extinguished and the consideration paid, recognised in profit or loss, with the equity recognised **at the date the liability is extinguished**; present the gain or loss as a **separate line item** in the statement of profit or loss **or disclose it in the notes**

---


#### IASB pipeline affecting IFRS 9, IFRS 7 and IAS 32 (as at 28 August 2026)

| Project | Status | What it will change |
|---|---|---|
| **Risk Mitigation Accounting** (formerly Dynamic Risk Management / macro hedging) | Exposure Draft published **3 December 2025**; comment deadline **extended in May 2026 to 30 November 2026**. Classified a Major Project; next milestone **Exposure Draft Feedback, 28 February 2027** | A new **optional** model for entities managing **repricing risk on a net basis**. Scope is set by business and risk management activities, not industry. Measurement of the underlying portfolios and the designated derivatives is **unchanged**; instead, recognition of fair value changes on the designated derivatives is **deferred through a "risk mitigation adjustment"**. Demand deposits and some pipeline transactions become eligible underlying items, removing the need for proxy hedging. Applied **prospectively** |
| **Withdrawal of IAS 39** | The ED **explicitly seeks feedback** on whether risk mitigation accounting justifies withdrawing IAS 39's remaining hedge accounting requirements. **No decision taken** | If withdrawn, both the IFRS 9.7.2.21 policy election and the IFRS 9.6.1.3 portfolio carve-out disappear. Until then **both remain fully available** |
| **Amortised Cost Measurement** | Active; **pre-Exposure Draft**. Next milestone **Exposure Draft, 31 December 2027** | Codifies modification and derecognition. Tentative decisions: a *modification* is "a change in contractual terms that changes the nature, timing, amounts or uncertainty of contractual cash flows"; a **substantial modification of a financial asset is accounted for as derecognition**; substantiality is to be determined by a **holistic analysis** of qualitative **and** quantitative factors, with the **10% test as a supplementary tool rather than decisive in isolation**; and IFRS 9.B5.4.5 is to require an entity to **adjust the EIR** on a re-estimation of contractual cash flows providing time-value or credit-risk consideration. The project also absorbs the contingent-settlement-provision measurement issues moved out of FICE |
| **FICE** (Financial Instruments with Characteristics of Equity) | Active; redeliberation. Next milestone **Final Amendments, 30 September 2027**. **No amendments issued** | Tagged against IAS 32, IFRS 7, IFRS 18, IAS 1 and the Conceptual Framework — see IAS 32 |
| **Post-implementation Review of IFRS 9 — Hedge Accounting** | Started Q1 2026; **Request for Information expected 30 September 2026**. The RFI will contain **separate questions** on the IFRS 9 hedge accounting requirements and the related IFRS 7 disclosures | Third and final phase of the IFRS 9 PIR |
| **Credit risk disclosures** | Research pipeline, added May 2024 following the impairment PIR; medium priority; no ED | Targeted improvements to IFRS 7 credit risk disclosures, including **reducing the burden for entities with no significant credit risk exposure** |
| **Financial guarantee contracts** | **Low priority**; deferred to the next agenda consultation | No action expected before then |

**Post-implementation Review of IFRS 9 — Impairment: concluded 4 July 2024.** The IASB concluded the impairment requirements are **working as intended** and **declined** requests to add application guidance on the objective of SICR, the low-credit-risk exemption, or collective SICR assessment, on the ground that doing so would risk making the requirements rule-based and reduce alignment with entities' internal credit risk management. **The practical implication: there is no forthcoming relief on ECL model judgement — the 2024 PIR closed that door.**

#### Recent IFRS Interpretations Committee agenda decisions on IFRS 9

- **Determining and Accounting for Transaction Costs — published January 2026.** Whether costs directly attributable to the origination or issuance of a financial instrument but incurred **before entering into the contractual arrangement** can be "incremental" and so meet the Appendix A definition of transaction costs. The Committee found **no diversity with a material effect** and added no project; feedback indicated such costs **are not precluded** from being incremental and are recognised in the statement of financial position, **often as prepayments or other assets**, until the instrument is recognised.
- **Embedded Prepayment Option — published January 2026.** Whether, in IFRS 9.B4.3.5(e)(ii), "the entity" means the **lender** or the reporting entity (borrower). No diversity with material effect, no project; stakeholders read the requirement as referring to **the lender**. The distinction drives whether a prepayment option embedded in a **financial liability** is separated and measured at FVTPL while the host stays at amortised cost.
- **Physical Settlement of Contracts to Buy or Sell a Non-financial Item — March 2019, updated January 2026 for IFRS 18.** Where a contract fails the IFRS 9.2.4 own-use exception, is accounted for as a derivative at FVTPL, is **not** designated in a hedging relationship, and is ultimately **physically settled**, IFRS 9 **neither permits nor requires** an additional entry reversing the accumulated fair value gain or loss against inventory (purchase contract) or revenue (sale contract). Such an entry would negate the requirement to account for the contract as a derivative. Presentation follows IFRS 18.B70–B76 and disclosure IFRS 7.20(a)(i). **For these purposes there is no gain or loss on the derivative caused by settlement.**
- **Classification of Gains and Losses on a Derivative Managing a Foreign Currency Exposure (IFRS 18) — April 2026.** Nominally an IFRS 18 decision, but it is the authoritative guidance on where **undesignated risk-management derivatives** land in profit or loss. IFRS 18.B72 requires gains and losses on a derivative **not designated as a hedging instrument but used to manage identified risks** to be classified in the **same category as the income and expenses affected by the risks the derivative is used to manage** — unless doing so would require **grossing up** or involve **undue cost or effort**, in which case **all** gains and losses go to the **operating** category. On the fact pattern (a net FC20 exposure hedged externally through a treasury entity), the external derivative's gain or loss goes to the **financing** category because classifying the net exposure in a single category does not require grossing up. Only the **external** derivative is analysed: transfers of risk **within** the reporting entity do not change the group's risk exposure.

---

**Disclosure requirements:**
- See IFRS 7 above — IFRS 7 contains all disclosure requirements for IFRS 9 instruments
- Specifically for IFRS 9 impairment: ECL methodology, assumptions, and inputs; staging movement tables; write-off and recovery policies; forward-looking information used
- Specifically for hedge accounting: see IFRS 7 hedge accounting disclosure requirements above

**Common pitfalls:**
- **Classification:** Applying SPPI at entity level rather than instrument level; failing to assess modified time value of money features; incorrectly treating equity investments as debt (e.g., preference shares with mandatory redemption)
- **Business model:** Using the wrong level of aggregation; changing classification based on a single sale rather than a genuine business model change; confusing intent (SPPI concern) with business model
- **ECL — SICR:** Using only backward-looking or lagging indicators; failing to incorporate macroeconomic forecasts; mechanically applying the 30-day presumption without considering lead indicators
- **ECL — forward-looking:** Single-scenario ECL that ignores non-linearity; using through-the-cycle PDs rather than point-in-time
- **ECL — staging:** Moving assets back to Stage 1 too quickly after cure; insufficient cure period evidence
- **ECL — provision matrix:** Using industry-average loss rates without adjustment for entity-specific and forward-looking factors
- **Hedge accounting — designation:** Hedging the benchmark rate component without meeting the separately identifiable and reliably measurable criteria; designating a net position for non-FX risks
- **Hedge accounting — discontinuation:** Voluntarily discontinuing to manage volatility (prohibited under IFRS 9)
- **Own credit risk on FVO liabilities:** Forgetting to present the own credit adjustment in OCI rather than P&L
- **Derecognition:** Failing to properly analyse pass-through arrangements and retained risks/rewards; partial derecognition errors in transfers

- Treating "Stage 1 / 2 / 3" as IFRS 9 terminology and then failing to trace the disclosures back to the actual populations in IFRS 7.35H(a)–(c)
- Conflating the **30-day SICR presumption** (5.5.11) with the **90-day default presumption** (B5.5.37) — different paragraphs, different rebuttal evidence, different outcomes
- Resetting the SICR origination benchmark on modification; IFRS 9.5.5.12 compares default risk on the **modified** terms against default risk at **initial recognition on the original** terms
- Applying the **10% test to financial assets**. It exists only for liabilities (B3.3.6)
- On a **non-substantial** liability modification, adjusting the EIR prospectively instead of remeasuring the liability at the **original** EIR with an immediate P&L catch-up
- Including **third-party** fees in the 10% test numerator; B3.3.6 limits the fees to those between borrower and lender
- Assuming an **ESG-linked loan** automatically fails SPPI, or automatically passes. Neither: run B4.1.10A's "not significantly different in all contractually possible scenarios" test, then B4.1.18's de minimis and non-genuine screens
- Applying the **CLI look-through** to structures that are not CLI transactions (B4.1.20A)
- Applying the nature-dependent electricity relief **by analogy** to other commodity contracts — expressly prohibited by IFRS 9.2.3B — or failing the **12-month look-back cap** in B2.8, or assessing net purchaser status across a **different market** from the one in which unused electricity was sold
- Applying the electronic-payment derecognition option **selectively within a payment system**; B3.3.10 requires consistency across all settlements through the same system
- Treating IFRS 9.7.2.21 and 6.1.3 as the same election, or assuming that electing IAS 39 hedge accounting under 7.2.21 still lets you apply the IFRS 9-conformed version of IFRIC 16 — it does not
- Relying on the **EU IAS 39 carve-out** while asserting compliance with IFRS as issued by the IASB
- Ignoring 5.5.20 for revolving facilities and measuring ECL only over the contractual notice period
- Measuring a financial guarantee contract at an IAS 37 amount rather than the higher of the Section 5.5 loss allowance and the initial amount less cumulative IFRS 15 income (4.2.1(c))

**Related standards:** IFRS 7 (all disclosures for financial instruments), IFRS 13 (fair value measurement; the day-one gain restriction is in IFRS 9.B5.1.2A, not IFRS 13), IFRS 15 (contract assets within ECL scope), IFRS 16 (lease receivables within ECL scope; lessee derecognition of lease liabilities via IFRS 9.2.1(b)(ii) and 3.3.3), IFRS 17 (insurance contracts — the IFRS 17.7(e) financial guarantee election), IFRS 18 (classification of derivative gains and losses, B70–B76), IFRS 19 (reduced disclosures at IFRS 19.56A–56B and 64A–64C), IAS 12 (deferred tax on fair value movements in OCI for FVOCI instruments), IAS 21 (foreign currency — interaction with hedge accounting and FVOCI), IAS 28 (equity method — but IFRS 9 applies **first** to long-term interests forming part of the net investment, IAS 28.14A), IAS 32 (equity versus liability classification — a prerequisite for IFRS 9), IAS 39 (predecessor standard; still applicable under the two elections above), IFRIC 16 (net investment hedge), IFRIC 19 (extinguishing liabilities with equity)

---

## IFRS 10 — Consolidated Financial Statements

**Scope:** Applies to all entities that control one or more other entities (subsidiaries). Excludes investment entities (which measure subsidiaries at fair value through profit or loss under IFRS 9), post-employment benefit plans, and entities within the scope of IFRS 17.

**Status and amendments:** Issued May 2011; effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2013 (EU effective 1 January 2014; endorsed 11 December 2012, OJ 29 December 2012). Amended by *Transition Guidance* (June 2012); *Investment Entities* (October 2012, EU effective 1 January 2014); *Sale or Contribution of Assets between an Investor and its Associate or Joint Venture* (September 2014 — amended IFRS 10.25–26 and added **IFRS 10.B99A**; **applies prospectively to transactions in annual periods beginning on or after a date to be determined by the IASB, i.e. indefinitely deferred since December 2015**; earlier application permitted with disclosure of that fact; never EU-endorsed); *Investment Entities: Applying the Consolidation Exception* (December 2014, EU effective 1 January 2016); and *Annual Improvements Volume 11 — Determination of a 'De Facto Agent'* (issued 18 July 2024, amending IFRS 10.B74; effective **1 January 2026**, earlier application permitted; EU effective 1 January 2026).

**A printing convention that misleads.** The annual edition **prints the deferred 2014 text** in IFRS 10.25(b), 26 and B99A, with the deferral carried only in the effective-date paragraphs and in a cross-reference to the Basis for Conclusions. Reading the standard text alone will lead you to conclude those requirements are in force. **They are not** — see the IAS 28 entry for where the Equity Method project is taking them.

**Core principle:** A parent must present consolidated financial statements that combine its financials with those of its subsidiaries, reflecting the group as a single economic entity. Control is the sole basis for consolidation.

**Key rules:**
- **Control model (three elements — all must be present):**
  - Power over the investee (existing rights that give the ability to direct relevant activities)
  - Exposure, or rights, to variable returns from involvement with the investee
  - Ability to use power to affect the amount of those returns
- **The three elements of control (IFRS 10.7)** — power (10–14), exposure or rights to variable returns (15–16), and the ability to use power to affect returns (17–18) — must be present **simultaneously**. IFRS 10.8 requires reassessment whenever facts and circumstances indicate a change in any of the three. **There is no percentage threshold anywhere in the model**: the requirements are a single basis for consolidation requiring a holistic, qualitative assessment of all legal, contractual and other facts and circumstances.
- **Power (IFRS 10.10):** existing rights giving the **current ability** to direct the relevant activities. Where two or more investors each unilaterally direct *different* relevant activities, power belongs to the one directing the activities that **most significantly** affect returns (IFRS 10.13).
- **Substantive versus protective rights:**
  - Substantiveness is judged on all facts and circumstances against the non-exhaustive factor list in **IFRS 10.B23**, which includes whether the holder **would benefit** from exercising the right
  - **Protective rights (IFRS 10.B26–B27)** relate to fundamental changes or exceptional circumstances; a holder of only protective rights can neither have power nor prevent another party from having it. Not every contingent right is protective
  - Franchise arrangements are the standard's worked case (**IFRS 10.B30–B31**): distinguish decisions that significantly affect the franchisee's returns from decisions that protect the brand
- **De facto control — the actual mechanics (IFRS 10.B38, B41–B46).** IFRS 10.B41: an investor with less than a majority of voting rights has sufficient rights when it has the **practical ability to direct the relevant activities unilaterally**. IFRS 10.B42 sets out what to consider:
  - (a) the size of the investor's holding **relative to the size and dispersion of other holdings**
  - (b) potential voting rights held by the investor, other vote holders or other parties
  - (c) rights arising from other contractual arrangements
  - (d) any additional facts and circumstances indicating the ability to direct at the time decisions need to be made, **including voting patterns at previous shareholders' meetings**
  - **IFRS 10.B45:** factors (a)–(c) alone may not be conclusive; where they are not, the investor considers additional facts and circumstances and applies IFRS 10.B18–B20, **giving greater weight to the evidence of power in B18 than to the indicators in B19–B20**. The fewer votes held, the more reliance is placed on those additional facts
  - **IFRS 10.B46 is the decision rule preparers most often miss: if, having considered B42(a)–(d), it is not clear that the investor has power, the investor does not control the investee.** Ambiguity resolves **against** consolidation
  - The standard's own examples make the point: 45% against eleven 5% holders is **not conclusive** either way (Example 7); 35% where 75% of votes were cast at recent meetings indicates **no** practical ability to direct unilaterally (Example 8)
- **Principal versus agent (IFRS 10.B58–B72):** IFRS 10.B60 requires all four factors to be considered **together**, weighted by facts and circumstances — the scope of decision-making authority; rights held by other parties, notably substantive removal rights; remuneration; and exposure to variability of returns from **other** interests held in the investee. IFRS 12.9(c) requires disclosure of the significant judgements in reaching the conclusion.
- **De facto agents (IFRS 10.B73–B75):** no contractual arrangement is required; the determination requires **judgement**, considering the nature of the relationship and how the parties interact (B73). Where a de facto agency exists, the investor aggregates the agent's decision-making rights and its indirect exposure to variable returns through the agent with its own.
  - **Annual Improvements Volume 11 amendment, effective 1 January 2026.** IFRS 10.B74 now draws a distinction the old text did not: a party **is** a de facto agent when *the investor* has the ability to direct that party to act on its behalf; a party **might also be** a de facto agent when *those that direct the activities of the investor* have that ability. The pre-amendment text used the conclusive "is" for both limbs, which contradicted the judgement requirement in B73 and could force two sister entities under common control each to conclude it controlled the same investee — impossible, since **only one party at most can control an investee**
- **Relevant activities:** Activities that significantly affect the investee's returns (e.g., operating and capital decisions, appointment of key management)
- **Potential voting rights:** included only if **substantive**, assessed under IFRS 10.B22–B24 and listed as a factor at IFRS 10.B42(b), with detailed guidance at IFRS 10.B47–B50. **"Currently exercisable" is not the test** — IFRS 10.B23 requires judgement across all facts and circumstances, including whether the holder **would benefit** from exercise. **IFRS 10.B85: a change in market conditions alone does not change the control conclusion** unless it changes one of the three elements or the overall principal/agent relationship — an option moving in or out of the money is not, by itself, a consolidation event.
- **Structured entities:** Assessed on substance; contractual and non-contractual involvement both considered
- **Investment entities exception:**
  - **Definition (IFRS 10.27)** — three limbs, all required: (a) obtains funds from one or more investors to provide them with investment management services; (b) commits to investors that its business purpose is to invest funds **solely** for capital appreciation, investment income, or both; (c) measures and evaluates the performance of **substantially all** its investments on a fair value basis. Application guidance at B85A–B85M
  - **Business purpose evidence (IFRS 10.B85B):** offering memoranda, publications, corporate or partnership documents, and how the entity presents itself. An entity presenting itself as investing to **jointly develop, produce or market products with its investees** has a business purpose inconsistent with the definition
  - **Investment-related services (IFRS 10.B85C):** an investment entity may provide investment advisory, management, support and administrative services to third parties as well as investors, directly or through a subsidiary, **even if those activities are substantial**, provided it continues to meet the definition
  - **Typical characteristics (IFRS 10.28):** more than one investment; more than one investor; investors that are not related parties; ownership interests in the form of equity or similar interests. **Absence of any of these does not necessarily disqualify the entity**, but it triggers the additional disclosure in **IFRS 12.9A**
  - **Consequence (IFRS 10.31):** an investment entity does **not** consolidate its subsidiaries and does **not** apply IFRS 3 on obtaining control; it measures each investment in a subsidiary at **FVTPL under IFRS 9**. This holds even where the subsidiary is itself an investment entity holding the underlying portfolio
  - **Sole exception (IFRS 10.32):** a subsidiary that is **not itself an investment entity** and whose **main purpose and activities** are providing services relating to the investment entity's investment activities is consolidated under IFRS 10.19–26, with IFRS 3 applied to its acquisition. The IFRS IC (March 2014) confirmed that an intermediate holding company established purely for **tax optimisation**, in which there is **no activity**, is not providing investment-related services and is therefore measured at FVTPL, not consolidated
  - **The exception does not travel up the group (IFRS 10.33):** a parent of an investment entity consolidates **all** entities it controls, **including those controlled through an investment entity subsidiary**, unless the parent is itself an investment entity. A non-investment-entity parent cannot inherit fair value measurement from an investment-entity subsidiary. (The IAS 28.36A election is the only analogous relief available to an investor — see IAS 28.)
  - **Change of status (IFRS 10.29–30, B100–B101):** reassess if facts and circumstances indicate a change in any of the three definitional elements or the typical characteristics; account for a change **prospectively** from the date it occurs. On **ceasing** to be an investment entity (B100), apply IFRS 3 to each previously fair-valued subsidiary using the date of change of status as the deemed acquisition date and the subsidiary's fair value at that date as deemed consideration. On **becoming** an investment entity (B101), cease consolidating (except an IFRS 10.32 service subsidiary) and apply IFRS 10.25–26 as though control had been lost at that date — a full remeasurement gain or loss
- **Consolidation procedures:**
  - Combine like items of assets, liabilities, equity, income, and expenses
  - Eliminate intragroup balances, transactions, income, expenses, and unrealised profits/losses
  - Non-controlling interests (NCI) presented within equity, separately from the parent's equity (IFRS 10.22). Note the **IAS 32 override** (IFRS IC, November 2013): puttable instruments issued by a subsidiary and classified as equity in the subsidiary's own financial statements under IAS 32.16A–16D are **classified as financial liabilities in the consolidated financial statements** (IAS 32.AG29A) — the IAS 32 exception does not travel up to the parent's consolidated statements
  - **IFRS 10.B94:** attribute profit or loss, each component of OCI, and total comprehensive income between the owners of the parent and NCI **even if this results in NCI having a deficit balance**. There is no "stop at zero" rule for NCI. **IFRS 10.B95:** where a subsidiary has outstanding cumulative preference shares classified as equity and held by NCI, compute the parent's share of profit or loss after adjusting for those dividends, whether or not declared
  - Uniform accounting policies for like transactions in similar circumstances (IFRS 10.19); consolidate from the date control is obtained until the date it is lost (IFRS 10.20)
  - **Reporting dates — the default is a common reporting date, not a three-month window (IFRS 10.B92–B93).** The subsidiary prepares additional financial information as at the parent's reporting date **unless it is impracticable to do so**. Only where impracticable may the parent use the subsidiary's most recent financial statements, adjusted for significant transactions and events in the intervening period, with a maximum gap of three months, and with the period lengths and the gap consistent from period to period
  - **IAS 21.45 carve-out on elimination:** an intragroup monetary asset or liability cannot be eliminated against its counterpart **without showing the effect of currency fluctuations**, so the exchange difference survives consolidation. The April 2026 IFRS IC agenda decision on classifying that difference under IFRS 18 holds that **two readings of IFRS 18.B65 are both reasonable** — the operating category as the IFRS 18.52 default, or the category in which the intragroup loan's income and expenses would have been classified before elimination (falling back to operating where that involves undue cost or effort, IFRS 18.B68). The entity chooses one as an IAS 8 accounting policy and discloses it
- **Changes in ownership without loss of control (IFRS 10.23, B96):** equity transactions. Adjust the carrying amounts of the controlling and non-controlling interests for the change in relative interests, and recognise **directly in equity, attributed to the owners of the parent**, any difference between that adjustment and the fair value of consideration paid or received. **No gain, no loss, no goodwill adjustment.** Two traps confirmed by agenda decisions: transaction costs on an NCI purchase or disposal are **equity transaction costs, not expenses** (July 2009); and where **non-cash consideration** is given for NCI, the difference between the fair value and carrying amount of the transferred asset is a **derecognition gain or loss in profit or loss**, while only the NCI/fair-value difference goes to equity under B96 (January 2013).
- **Loss of control (IFRS 10.25–26, B97–B99):**
  - **B97 — the anti-structuring rule.** Multiple arrangements are accounted for as a **single** transaction where indicated by: entry at the same time or in contemplation of each other; a single transaction designed to achieve an overall commercial effect; the occurrence of one being dependent on another; or one arrangement not being economically justified on its own but being so in combination. This is why staged disposals cannot be engineered into a series of equity transactions
  - **B98** — derecognise the subsidiary's assets (including goodwill) and liabilities at carrying amount, and the carrying amount of NCI (including OCI components attributable to them); recognise the fair value of consideration received, any distribution of subsidiary shares to owners in that capacity, and **any retained investment at its fair value at the date control is lost**; reclassify or transfer accumulated OCI per B99; recognise the resulting difference as a gain or loss in profit or loss attributable to the parent
  - **B99** — account for amounts previously in OCI **as if the parent had directly disposed of the related assets or liabilities**: recycle to profit or loss what would recycle (the FX translation reserve), transfer directly to retained earnings what would transfer (an IAS 16 revaluation surplus)
  - **IFRS 10.25(b)** — the remeasured value at the date control is lost is the **fair value** on initial recognition of a financial asset under IFRS 9, or the **cost** on initial recognition of an investment in an associate or joint venture
- **The deferred restriction — IFRS 10.B99A, printed but not in force.** Where a parent loses control of a subsidiary **that does not contain a business** as a result of a transaction involving an equity-accounted associate or joint venture, the gain or loss determined under B98–B99 would be recognised in profit or loss **only to the extent of unrelated investors' interests**, with the remainder eliminated against the carrying amount of the investment. **Its effective date is deferred to "a date to be determined by the IASB"; early application is permitted with disclosure.** In May 2026 the IASB tentatively decided to **withdraw** the 2014 amendments and instead give investors an accounting policy choice — see IAS 28.

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Significant judgements and assumptions used in determining whether control exists
- Information about subsidiaries with material NCI: name, place of incorporation, proportion of ownership and voting rights held by NCI, summarised financial information
- Nature and extent of significant restrictions on access to group assets or settlement of group liabilities
- Consequences of changes in the parent's ownership interest that did not result in loss of control
- Nature of risks associated with interests in consolidated structured entities

**Common pitfalls:**
- Failing to consolidate entities where control exists without a majority of voting rights (de facto control)
- Overlooking substantive removal rights held by other parties (which could negate power)
- Incorrect assessment of relevant activities in structured entities — focusing on legal form rather than economic substance
- Not reassessing control when facts and circumstances change
- Treating NCI as a liability rather than as equity — but note the reverse trap: **puttable NCI instruments that are equity in the subsidiary's own statements are financial liabilities in the consolidated statements** (IAS 32.AG29A; IFRS IC, November 2013)
- Omitting elimination of upstream and downstream intragroup transactions (including unrealised profit in inventory)
- Misapplying the investment entity exception — all three criteria must be satisfied simultaneously

- Treating "currently exercisable" as the test for potential voting rights, or re-running the control assessment because an option moved in or out of the money (IFRS 10.B85)
- Concluding de facto control from relative block size alone. IFRS 10.B41 requires the practical ability to direct **unilaterally**, and **IFRS 10.B46 requires the investor not to consolidate where, after considering B42(a)–(d), power is not clear**
- Stopping at IFRS 10.B42(a)–(c) without considering past voting patterns and the B18–B20 evidence and indicators, which B45 requires where (a)–(c) are inconclusive
- Two entities under common control each concluding they control the same investee via the de facto agent guidance — the Volume 11 change to IFRS 10.B74 (effective 1 January 2026) exists to prevent exactly this
- Capping NCI at zero when a subsidiary is loss-making; IFRS 10.B94 requires attribution to NCI even into a deficit balance
- Recognising a gain in profit or loss on an NCI transaction. IFRS 10.23 and B96 make it an equity transaction — but the derecognition gain on **non-cash consideration given** still goes to profit or loss
- Defaulting to a three-month reporting-date gap; IFRS 10.B92 requires coterminous information unless it is **impracticable** to produce
- Failing to consolidate through an investment-entity subsidiary — IFRS 10.33 requires a non-investment-entity parent to consolidate everything it controls
- Consolidating a "tax optimisation" intermediate holding subsidiary of an investment entity as if it provided investment-related services (IFRS IC, March 2014 — it has no activity, so it does not)
- Treating IFRS 10.B99A as effective. It is printed in the standard but deferred, and is on course to be withdrawn rather than activated

**Related standards:** IAS 27 (separate financial statements; IAS 27.11A forces the investment-entity measurement through), IAS 28 (associates and joint ventures; the deferred IFRS 10/IAS 28 conflict), IFRS 11 (joint arrangements), IFRS 12 (disclosure — IFRS 12.9, 9A, 9B, 12, 13), IFRS 3 (business combinations), IFRS 9 (measurement of subsidiaries by an investment entity), IFRS 18 (classification of the intragroup FX difference, B65–B68), IAS 21 (IAS 21.45 — the exchange difference survives elimination), IAS 32 (IAS 32.AG29A — puttable NCI instruments)

---

## IFRS 11 — Joint Arrangements

**Scope:** Applies to entities that have rights and obligations in joint arrangements — arrangements over which two or more parties have joint control. Does not apply to arrangements where joint control does not exist (those are assessed under IFRS 10 or IAS 28).

**Status and amendments:** Issued May 2011; effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2013 (EU effective 1 January 2014; endorsed 11 December 2012, OJ 29 December 2012). Amended by *Transition Guidance* (June 2012); *Accounting for Acquisitions of Interests in Joint Operations* (issued 6 May 2014, adding **IFRS 11.21A and B33A–B33D**; EU effective 1 January 2016); and *Annual Improvements 2015–2017 Cycle* (December 2017, clarifying that previously held interests are **not** remeasured when a party to a joint operation obtains joint control). **No amendments to IFRS 11 were issued or under development at 28 August 2026** — but the IASB's May 2026 tentative decision to amend **IFRS 10** for restricted gain recognition on loss of control of a non-business subsidiary is directly adjacent to IFRS 11.B34–B35.

**Core principle:** An entity must classify its joint arrangements as either a joint operation or a joint venture based on the rights and obligations of the parties, and account for each type using the appropriate method.

**Key rules:**
- **Joint control is a two-step test (IFRS 11.7–9), not one.** First (IFRS 11.8) establish that all the parties, or a group of them, **control the arrangement collectively** — they must act together to direct the relevant activities. Only then (IFRS 11.9) ask whether decisions about those relevant activities require the **unanimous consent** of the parties that control collectively. Skipping step one is the commonest error: unanimous consent among parties who do not collectively control does not create joint control.
  - **IFRS 11.10:** no single party controls the arrangement alone, and a party with joint control can block any other party or group from controlling it
  - **IFRS 11.11:** an arrangement can be a joint arrangement **even though not all its parties have joint control**. The standard distinguishes joint operators and joint venturers from parties that merely participate
- **Two types of joint arrangements:**
  - **Joint operation:**
    - Parties (joint operators) have rights to the assets and obligations for the liabilities of the arrangement
    - Accounting: recognise own assets/liabilities (including share of jointly held assets/liabilities), own revenues/expenses, and share of revenues/expenses earned/incurred jointly — line-by-line
  - **Joint venture:**
    - Parties (joint venturers) have rights to the net assets of the arrangement
    - Structured via a separate vehicle where legal form does confer separation, contractual terms do not override, and relevant activities are not solely dependent on joint venturers
    - Accounting: equity method (same as associates under IAS 28)
- **Classification is an ordered assessment (IFRS 11.14, 15–17, B14–B15), and the order matters — each later step can only move a classification *towards* joint operation:**
  1. **Not structured through a separate vehicle → joint operation** (IFRS 11.B16–B21). No further analysis
  2. **Separate vehicle → legal form** (IFRS 11.B22–B24). A legal form that does not confer separation between the parties and the vehicle gives a joint operation
  3. **Terms of the contractual arrangement** (IFRS 11.B25–B28) — these can **override** the legal form, for example by giving the parties rights to the assets and obligations for the liabilities
  4. **Other facts and circumstances**, "when relevant" (IFRS 11.B29–B33). **IFRS 11.B30:** an arrangement in a vehicle whose legal form confers separation, with contractual terms silent on rights and obligations, is nevertheless a **joint operation** where other facts and circumstances give the parties rights to the assets and obligations for the liabilities
  - **The output test (IFRS 11.B31):** where the activities are primarily designed to provide **output to the parties**, that indicates the parties have rights to substantially all the economic benefits of the assets. Parties typically secure this by preventing the arrangement from selling output to third parties
  - **The funding test (IFRS 11.B32):** where the arrangement's liabilities are in substance satisfied by the cash flows the parties provide through their purchases of output, and the parties are **substantially the only source of cash flows** sustaining operations, that indicates the parties have an obligation for the liabilities
  - **IFRS IC, March 2015 (reproduced at IFRS 11.B29):** the "other facts and circumstances" assessment must focus on whether those facts and circumstances create **enforceable** rights to the assets and obligations for the liabilities. Design, purpose, business need and past practice are relevant only through that lens
  - Example 5 in the standard is the canonical fact pattern: a 50/50 incorporated entity manufacturing materials for the parties' own processes, all output taken by the parties, no third-party sales without approval, priced to break even — legal form and contractual terms point to a joint venture, but the other facts and circumstances make it a **joint operation**
- **Joint operation accounting (IFRS 11.20–23):** recognise own assets including the share of assets held jointly; own liabilities including the share of liabilities incurred jointly; revenue from the sale of own share of output; share of revenue from the arrangement's sale of output; and own expenses including the share of expenses incurred jointly. **IFRS 11.23:** a party that participates in but does not have joint control of a joint operation applies the same accounting **if it has rights to the assets and obligations for the liabilities**; otherwise it applies the standards applicable to its interest.
  - **Output share is not necessarily ownership share.** Where a joint operator's contracted output purchase differs from its ownership interest, understanding *why* they differ drives the IFRS 11.20 answer (IFRS IC, March 2015)
  - **Primary responsibility.** The liabilities a joint operator recognises include those for which it has **primary responsibility**, determined by assessing all contractual agreements and the governing law. Where the operator has primary responsibility it recognises the **whole** liability and separately assesses whether to recognise a right to recover from the other operators — it must settle even if recovery fails (IFRS IC, March 2019)
- **Acquiring an interest in a joint operation that is a business (IFRS 11.21A, B33A–B33D):**
  - Apply all **IFRS 3** business-combination principles that do not conflict with IFRS 11, **to the extent of the acquirer's share**, and give the IFRS 3 disclosures. **B33A** spells out what that includes: fair value measurement of identifiable assets and liabilities; expensing acquisition-related costs; recognising deferred tax on initial recognition (except on goodwill); recognising the excess of consideration over the share of identifiable net assets as **goodwill**; and annual IAS 36 impairment testing of the CGU to which that goodwill is allocated. This applies to both the **initial** and any **additional** interest
  - **B33B:** the requirements also apply on the **formation** of a joint operation if, and only if, an **existing business is contributed** on formation by one of the participating parties. They do not apply where all parties contribute only assets or asset groups that are not businesses
  - **B33C:** where a joint operator increases its interest in a joint operation that is a business, **previously held interests are not remeasured** if joint control is retained
  - **B33D:** IFRS 11.21A and B33A–B33C do **not** apply where the parties sharing joint control are under the common control of the same ultimate controlling party both before and after, and that control is not transitory
  - Where the assets acquired are **not** a business, IFRS 3.2(b) requires a cost-based approach and existing assets are generally not remeasured (IFRS IC, January 2016)
- **Separate financial statements (IFRS 11.26):** a joint operator accounts for its interest in a joint operation the **same way** (IFRS 11.20–22) in its separate as in its consolidated financial statements. Per the IFRS IC (March 2015), it does **not** additionally recognise its shareholding in the separate vehicle at cost under IAS 27 or at fair value under IFRS 9 — that would double-count. A joint venturer applies IAS 27.10.
- **Proportionate consolidation is prohibited** for joint ventures
- **Transactions between the entity and a joint operation (IFRS 11.B34–B35):** a joint operator selling or contributing assets to the joint operation is transacting with the other parties, so it recognises gains and losses **only to the extent of the other parties' interests**. Where such a transaction evidences a reduction in net realisable value or an impairment, the operator recognises those losses **in full** (B35). The IFRS IC (July 2016) flagged that B34–B35 can be read as conflicting with IFRS 10's requirement to remeasure a retained interest on loss of control, and recommended the IASB consider it alongside the IAS 28 sale-or-contribution question; **it remains unresolved at 28 August 2026**.

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Nature, extent, and financial effects of interests in joint arrangements (name, place of business, ownership interest, nature of relationship)
- Summarised financial information for material joint ventures (balance sheet, income statement, including fair value if quoted)
- Risks associated with interests in joint ventures (commitments, contingent liabilities)
- For joint operations: the nature of the arrangement and the entity's share

**Common pitfalls:**
- Applying equity method to joint operations (must use line-by-line recognition)
- Classifying a joint venture as a joint operation based solely on legal form without considering contractual terms and other facts
- Assuming that a separate vehicle automatically creates a joint venture — substance over form analysis is required
- Failing to reassess classification when facts change
- Incorrectly computing the share of profits where output arrangements exist
- Not eliminating unrealised gains on transactions with joint arrangements

- Testing for unanimous consent without first establishing collective control (IFRS 11.8 before IFRS 11.9)
- Stopping at legal form. IFRS 11.B15 makes "other facts and circumstances" the fourth step, and B30 lets it convert an apparent joint venture into a joint operation
- Treating design, purpose or business need as sufficient in the other-facts analysis — the IFRS IC (March 2015) requires those factors to create **enforceable** rights and obligations
- A joint operator recognising both its share of the joint operation's assets and liabilities **and** its shareholding in the separate vehicle in its separate financial statements
- Recognising only a proportionate share of a liability for which the operator has **primary responsibility** to a third party
- Remeasuring a previously held interest on acquiring an additional interest in a joint operation while retaining joint control (IFRS 11.B33C prohibits it), or applying IFRS 3 principles to a common-control acquisition of an interest (IFRS 11.B33D scopes it out)

**Related standards:** IAS 28 (Equity Method), IFRS 10 (Consolidated Financial Statements), IFRS 12 (Disclosure), IFRS 3 (Business Combinations — for acquisitions of interests in joint operations)

---

## IFRS 12 — Disclosure of Interests in Other Entities

**Scope:** Applies to entities that have interests in subsidiaries, joint arrangements, associates, and unconsolidated structured entities. Disclosure requirements supplement (do not replace) those in IFRS 10, IFRS 11, and IAS 28.

**Status and amendments:** Issued May 2011; effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2013 (EU effective 1 January 2014; endorsed 11 December 2012, OJ 29 December 2012). Amended by *Transition Guidance* (June 2012); *Investment Entities* (October 2012, adding **IFRS 12.9A–9B, 19A–19G, 21A and 25A**); *Investment Entities: Applying the Consolidation Exception* (December 2014); *Annual Improvements 2014–2016 Cycle* (December 2016); and consequential amendments from **IFRS 18** (issued 9 April 2024, effective 1 January 2027). **Amendments to the IFRS 12 disclosure requirements are proposed in the Equity Method exposure draft and were still in redeliberation at 28 August 2026** — in July 2026 the IASB settled a disclosure package that would **expand the IFRS 12.20 disclosure objective**, add a reconciliation of the opening to closing carrying amount of investments in associates, and require disclosure of the accounting policy choice for gains and losses on transactions with associates. Nothing is final.

**Core principle:** An entity must disclose information that enables users of financial statements to evaluate the nature of, and risks associated with, its interests in other entities, and the effects of those interests on its financial position, performance, and cash flows.

**Key rules:**
- **Significant judgements and assumptions (IFRS 12.7, 9)** must be disclosed wherever they affect classification or consolidation conclusions. IFRS 12.7 covers judgements in determining control, joint control or significant influence, and the **type** of joint arrangement where structured through a separate vehicle. **IFRS 12.9 gives the examples, and the negative conclusions are the ones routinely omitted:**
  - (a) it does **not** control an entity despite holding more than half the voting rights
  - (b) it **does** control an entity despite holding less than half
  - (c) it is a **principal or an agent** (IFRS 10.B58–B72)
  - (d) it does **not** have significant influence despite holding 20% or more
  - (e) it **has** significant influence despite holding less than 20%
- **Scope (IFRS 12.5):** interests in subsidiaries, joint arrangements (**both** joint operations and joint ventures), associates, and unconsolidated structured entities. **IFRS 12.B4 requires information to be presented separately for each of those five categories** — aggregating associates with joint ventures, or joint operations with joint ventures, breaches this.
- **"Interest in another entity" (Appendix A)** is broader than an equity holding: contractual *and non-contractual* involvement that exposes the entity to variability of returns from the other entity's performance — funding, liquidity support, credit enhancement, guarantees. A typical customer–supplier relationship is not, on its own, an interest.
- **IFRS 12.12 — subsidiaries with material NCI.** For each subsidiary with NCI **material to the reporting entity**: name; principal place of business (and country of incorporation if different); proportion of ownership interests held by NCI; proportion of voting rights if different; profit or loss allocated to NCI in the period; **accumulated NCI at period end**; and summarised financial information per IFRS 12.B10. Per the IFRS IC (January 2015), materiality is assessed by the **reporting entity on the basis of its consolidated financial statements**, considering both the size and the nature of the subsidiary.
- **IFRS 12.13 — restrictions.** Significant statutory, contractual and regulatory restrictions on accessing or using group assets and settling group liabilities, including restrictions on transferring cash within the group, guarantees restricting dividends or intra-group lending, **the extent to which protective rights of NCI can significantly restrict access**, and the carrying amounts of the assets and liabilities to which the restrictions apply.
- **IFRS 12.20–23 — joint arrangements and associates.** IFRS 12.20 is the disclosure objective and expressly reaches **the nature and effects of the contractual relationship with the other investors** holding joint control or significant influence. IFRS 12.21(a) applies to each **material joint arrangement and associate** (name; nature of the relationship, **including whether its activities are strategic to the entity**; principal place of business; proportion of ownership interest or participating share and, if different, voting rights). IFRS 12.21(b) applies to each material **joint venture and associate** (measurement basis; summarised financial information per B12–B13; and, where equity-accounted, the **fair value of the investment if quoted**). IFRS 12.21(c) requires aggregate information per B16, **separately** for individually immaterial joint ventures and for individually immaterial associates.
  - **IFRS 12.22 — three requirements commonly missed:** (a) significant restrictions on a joint venture's or associate's ability to transfer funds to the entity as dividends or to repay loans or advances; (b) where the investee's financial statements used are as of a **different date or period, the investee's reporting date and the reason** for using it; and (c) the **unrecognised share of losses, for the period and cumulatively**, where the entity has stopped recognising losses under IAS 28.38–39
  - **IFRS 12.23:** commitments relating to joint ventures separately from other commitments (B18–B20), and, unless the probability of loss is remote, contingent liabilities relating to interests in joint ventures or associates — including the share of contingent liabilities incurred jointly — separately from other contingent liabilities
- **Investment entity disclosures:** **IFRS 12.9A** — the judgements in concluding it is an investment entity, and, where one or more of the IFRS 10.28 typical characteristics is absent, the reasons for concluding it is nevertheless an investment entity. **IFRS 12.9B** — change of status, the reasons, and, on becoming an investment entity, the total fair value of the deconsolidated subsidiaries, the total gain or loss under IFRS 10.B101, and the line item containing it. **IFRS 12.19A** — an investment entity applying the consolidation exception discloses that fact. IFRS 12.21A and 25A relieve investment entities from IFRS 12.21(b)–(c) and, in specified circumstances, IFRS 12.24.
- **Unconsolidated structured entities (IFRS 12.24–31).** A "structured entity" (Appendix A) is one designed so that voting or similar rights are **not the dominant factor** in deciding who controls it. IFRS 12.26 requires qualitative and quantitative information on nature, purpose, size, activities and financing. **IFRS 12.27 is the sponsorship trap:** where the entity has sponsored an unconsolidated structured entity but has **no interest in it at the reporting date**, it must still disclose how it determined which entities it sponsored, the income from them during the period (with a description of the types of income), and **the carrying amount at the time of transfer of all assets transferred to them during the period**. IFRS 12.29 requires a summary, **in tabular format unless another format is more appropriate**, of the carrying amounts of related assets and liabilities and the line items containing them. IFRS 12.31 requires disclosure of any **current intentions** to provide financial or other support; IFRS 12.14 and 17 impose the parallel requirements for **consolidated** structured entities.
- **Subsidiaries:**
  - Composition of the group; restrictions on access to assets; NCI information
  - Changes in ownership interests not resulting in loss of control
  - Consequences of losing control
- **Unconsolidated subsidiaries (investment entity exception):** Summarised financial information and restrictions
- **Joint ventures (material):** Summarised balance sheet and P&L, reconciliation to carrying amount, fair value if quoted
- **Associates (material):** Same requirements as joint ventures
- **Non-material interests:** Aggregate disclosure by category (joint ventures/associates)
- **Unconsolidated structured entities (sponsored but not consolidated):** Nature, purpose, size, activities, financing; maximum exposure to loss
- **Quantitative and qualitative risk information** for all interests

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Objectives, policies, and processes for managing risks from interests in other entities
- For NCI: name, principal place of business, proportion held, summarised financial information, dividends paid to NCI
- For unconsolidated structured entities: how the entity determined it does not control the entity; contractual obligations to provide financial support; financial or other support provided during the period

**Common pitfalls:**
- Treating IFRS 12 as a standalone standard — it requires cross-referencing with IFRS 10, 11, and IAS 28
- Providing boilerplate disclosures rather than entity-specific judgements and risks
- Omitting disclosures for non-material interests in aggregate
- Failing to disclose for sponsored structured entities even when they are not consolidated
- Inadequate disclosure of restrictions on the parent's ability to access subsidiary assets

- Disclosing judgements only where control was concluded. IFRS 12.9(a) and (d) require the **negative** conclusions — no control despite >50%, no significant influence despite ≥20%
- Omitting IFRS 12.22(b) and (c) — the investee's non-coterminous reporting date and the reason for it, and the unrecognised share of losses for the period and cumulatively
- Aggregating across the IFRS 12.B4 categories, or presenting one combined "associates and joint ventures" note where both are material
- Assuming no disclosure is needed for a structured entity the group sponsored but no longer has an interest in — IFRS 12.27 requires income and transferred-asset disclosures regardless
- Omitting the fair value of a quoted equity-accounted investment (IFRS 12.21(b)(iii))

**Related standards:** IFRS 10 (Consolidated Financial Statements), IFRS 11 (Joint Arrangements), IAS 28 (Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures), IFRS 9 (Financial Instruments)

---

## IFRS 13 — Fair Value Measurement

**Scope:** Applies when another IFRS requires or permits fair value measurement or disclosure. Does not apply to: share-based payment transactions (IFRS 2); leasing transactions (IFRS 16); measurements that are similar to but not identical to fair value (e.g., net realisable value in IAS 2, value in use in IAS 36).

**Status and amendments:** Issued May 2011; effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2013; earlier application was permitted (IFRS 13.C1). Applied **prospectively** from the beginning of the annual period of initial application, with no requirement to restate comparative disclosures (IFRS 13.C2–C3). Amended by *Annual Improvements 2011–2013* (December 2013, paragraph 52), IFRS 9 (July 2014, paragraph 52), IFRS 16 (January 2016, paragraph 6) and **IFRS 18** (April 2024, paragraph 51 — applied when IFRS 18 is applied, i.e. periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027) (IFRS 13.C4–C7). EU-endorsed 11 December 2012, OJ 29 December 2012. **The standard has never been amended in its recognition or measurement requirements.**

**Post-implementation review: completed December 2018.** The IASB concluded IFRS 13 works as intended and closed the project. The two matters the review singled out as judgement-heavy were left to develop in practice rather than standard-set: (a) assessing whether a market is active and whether an input is significant and observable, which drives inconsistent hierarchy categorisation; and (b) the ***PxQ* issue**, where the unit of account for the measurement differs from the unit for which Level 1 inputs exist. The separate *Fair Value Measurement: Unit of Account (Amendments to IFRS 13)* project was not completed.

**Core principle:** Fair value is the price that would be received to sell an asset or paid to transfer a liability in an orderly transaction between market participants at the measurement date (an exit price).

**Key rules:**
- **Unit of account:** The level at which the asset or liability is aggregated for measurement; determined by the IFRS that requires the measurement
- **Market participants:** Knowledgeable, willing parties acting in their economic best interest; entity's own intentions are irrelevant
- **Principal market:** The market with the greatest volume and level of activity; if no principal market, the most advantageous market
- **Highest and best use:** For non-financial assets — the use that maximises value, physically possible, legally permissible, and financially feasible; in-use vs. in-exchange premise
- **Fair value hierarchy:**
  - **Level 1:** Quoted prices (unadjusted) in active markets for identical assets or liabilities (most reliable)
  - **Level 2:** Inputs other than quoted prices that are observable, either directly or indirectly (comparable assets, yield curves, credit spreads)
  - **Level 3:** Unobservable inputs — entity's own assumptions about market participant assumptions; requires maximum use of observable inputs
- **Valuation techniques:**
  - Market approach: uses prices from market transactions for identical or comparable assets
  - Income approach: discounts future amounts (cash flows, income) to a present value
  - Cost approach: reflects the amount required to replace the service capacity (current replacement cost)
- **Unit of account versus unit of valuation — the structural tension.** **IFRS 13.14:** the unit of account is set by the *other* IFRS requiring the measurement, **not** by IFRS 13, except where IFRS 13 itself provides otherwise. **IFRS 13.32:** a non-financial asset is assumed to be sold **consistently with that unit of account** (which may be an individual asset) *even where* the highest-and-best-use conclusion assumes use in combination with other assets, because the measurement assumes the market participant buyer already holds the complementary assets and associated liabilities. **IFRS 13.69** closes the loop: a premium or discount inconsistent with the unit of account may not be incorporated. The practical consequence is the **PxQ problem** — the unit of account (an investment in a subsidiary or associate) may differ from the level at which Level 1 prices exist (an individual share), and IFRS 13 requires quoted price × quantity.
- **Highest and best use — the mechanics (IFRS 13.27–33).** Applies **only to non-financial assets** (13.27). The three filters are physically possible, legally permissible and financially feasible (13.28); *financially feasible* means the use generates adequate income or cash flows, **net of the cost of converting the asset to that use**, to produce the return market participants would require. **13.29:** determined from the market participant perspective, but **the entity's current use is presumed to be highest and best use** unless market or other factors indicate a different use would maximise value. **13.30:** an entity acquiring an intangible intending to use it *defensively* must still measure it at its highest and best use by market participants. **13.31:** highest and best use sets the **valuation premise** — in-combination (with complementary assets and associated liabilities assumed available to market participants; associated liabilities include working-capital funding but not liabilities funding assets outside the group) or stand-alone. **13.31(a)(iii):** assumptions about highest and best use must be **consistent across all assets of the group** for which highest and best use is relevant.
- **Hierarchy categorisation is driven by inputs, not techniques (IFRS 13.73–75, 82–84).** A present value measurement can be Level 2 or Level 3 depending on which inputs are significant (13.74). A measurement is categorised **in its entirety at the lowest level input that is significant to the entire measurement** (13.73); adjustments made to arrive at a *measurement based on* fair value — costs to sell in fair value less costs to sell — are excluded when determining the level. An observable input adjusted by an **unobservable** input that is significant to the entire measurement lands in **Level 3** (13.75, 13.84); the archetypal case in 13.75 is a Level 2 quoted price adjusted for a sale restriction. Level 2 inputs must be observable **for substantially the full (contractual) term** (13.82), and Level 2 adjustments turn on the asset's condition or location, the comparability of the reference item, and the volume and level of activity in the market where the input was observed (13.83).
- **Third-party and broker prices are not automatically Level 1 or Level 2 (IFRIC agenda decision, January 2015, reproduced at IFRS 13.72).** Classification depends on **the inputs the third party used to derive the price, not on the pricing methodology**. A third-party price qualifies for Level 1 only if the measurement relies solely on unadjusted quoted prices in an active market for an identical instrument the entity can access at the measurement date.
- **The five permitted adjustments to a Level 1 input (IFRS 13.79) are a closed list:** (a) the matrix-pricing practical expedient for a large number of *similar but not identical* assets; (b) where a quoted price does not represent fair value at the measurement date because of a significant post-close event (the entity must establish and consistently apply a policy for identifying such events); and (c) where a liability or own equity instrument is measured using the quoted price of the identical item traded as an asset and that price needs adjustment. **In each case the adjustment itself demotes the measurement to a lower level.**
- **Blockage factors — the precise rule (IFRS 13.80, 69).** For a position in a single asset or liability traded in an active market, fair value is measured **within Level 1 as quoted price × quantity held**, even where normal daily volume could not absorb the position. Premiums or discounts reflecting *size as a characteristic of the entity's holding* are **prohibited**; premiums or discounts reflecting a *characteristic of the asset or liability* — a control premium on a controlling interest — are permitted, but only where consistent with the unit of account in the Standard requiring the measurement.
- **Valuation approaches in substance (IFRS 13.62, B5–B11):**
  - **Market approach (B5–B7):** prices and other information from market transactions in identical or comparable assets, liabilities, or a group of assets and liabilities such as a business. Typically uses market multiples derived from a set of comparables; selecting the multiple within the observed range requires judgement (B6). Includes **matrix pricing** (B7)
  - **Cost approach (B8–B9):** current replacement cost — the cost to a market participant buyer to acquire or construct a substitute asset of **comparable utility**, adjusted for obsolescence. Obsolescence here spans **physical deterioration, functional (technological) obsolescence and economic (external) obsolescence**, and is therefore broader than accounting depreciation
  - **Income approach (B10–B11):** converts future amounts to a single discounted amount reflecting **current market expectations**. Techniques named in B11: present value techniques, option pricing models (Black-Scholes-Merton, binomial/lattice), and the **multi-period excess earnings method** for some intangible assets
  - **13.63:** where multiple techniques are used, the results are evaluated for the reasonableness of the indicated range and fair value is **the point within that range most representative** in the circumstances
  - **13.64 — calibration:** where the transaction price is fair value at initial recognition and a technique using unobservable inputs will be used subsequently, the technique must be **calibrated so that at initial recognition it reproduces the transaction price**. After initial recognition, techniques using unobservable inputs must still reflect observable market data at the measurement date
  - **13.65:** techniques are applied consistently; a change in technique, in its weighting, or in an adjustment is appropriate only where the change produces an equally or more representative measurement
- **Level 3 inputs (IFRS 13.86–89):** used only to the extent relevant observable inputs are not available, but the objective is unchanged — an exit price from the perspective of a market participant holding the asset or owing the liability (13.87). **13.88:** assumptions about risk include **both the risk inherent in the technique (model risk) and the risk inherent in its inputs**; a measurement omitting a risk adjustment market participants would make **is not a fair value measurement**. **13.89:** an entity may start from its own data but must adjust it where reasonably available information shows market participants would use different data, or where something is particular to the entity and not available to others — an **entity-specific synergy** is the example given.
- **Portfolio (net risk exposure) exception (IFRS 13.48–52):** available only where the entity manages a group of financial assets and financial liabilities on the basis of net exposure to a **particular market risk or the credit risk of a particular counterparty**, in accordance with a documented risk management or investment strategy, and reports on that basis to key management personnel as defined in IAS 24 (13.49). It is an **accounting policy decision under IAS 8**, applied consistently period to period for a particular portfolio, including the policy for allocating bid-ask and credit adjustments (13.51). It applies to all contracts **within the scope of IFRS 9**, whether or not they meet the IAS 32 definitions of financial asset or financial liability (13.52).
- **Liabilities and own credit risk:** Assumes liability is transferred, not settled; own credit risk (non-performance risk) must be included
- **Day 1 gains and losses (IFRS 13.57–60) — the deferral rule is in IFRS 9, not here.** IFRS 13.57 distinguishes the transaction price (an **entry** price) from fair value (an **exit** price) and notes that entities do not necessarily sell assets at the prices paid to acquire them. IFRS 13.59 requires the entity to consider factors specific to the transaction and the asset or liability, with IFRS 13.B4 listing the situations in which transaction price may not represent fair value. **IFRS 13.60 then requires any resulting gain or loss to be recognised in profit or loss unless the Standard requiring or permitting the fair value measurement specifies otherwise.** The restriction on recognising a day-one gain sits in **IFRS 9.B5.1.2A**: where fair value at initial recognition of a **financial instrument** differs from the transaction price, the difference is recognised as a gain or loss **only** where that fair value is evidenced by a quoted price in an active market for an identical asset or liability, or by a valuation technique using **only data from observable markets**; **in all other cases the difference is deferred**. Applying the deferral to a **non-financial** item measured at fair value — investment property, a biological asset, an asset acquired in a non-monetary exchange — is wrong: IFRS 13.60 requires immediate profit-or-loss recognition there.
- **Bid-ask spread:** Use the price within the bid-ask spread most representative of fair value
- **Blockage factors:** Not allowed as an adjustment to Level 1 inputs even for large holdings

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Fair value hierarchy level for each class of assets/liabilities
- For Level 3: opening/closing reconciliation, unrealised gains and losses in profit or loss, transfers into and out of Level 3, and the valuation techniques and inputs used
- **Level 3 sensitivity — two distinct requirements, only one of them quantitative:**
  - **IFRS 13.93(h)(i)** applies to **all** recurring Level 3 measurements and is a **narrative** description of sensitivity to changes in unobservable inputs, plus a description of any interrelationships between those inputs and how they magnify or mitigate the effect. It must at a minimum cover the unobservable inputs disclosed under 93(d)
  - **IFRS 13.93(h)(ii)** applies **only to financial assets and financial liabilities** and is **quantitative**: where changing one or more unobservable inputs to reflect reasonably possible alternative assumptions would change fair value significantly, state that fact, disclose the effect, and disclose **how the effect was calculated**. Significance is judged against profit or loss and total assets or total liabilities, or against total equity where fair value changes go through OCI
- **IFRS 13.93(c):** for recurring measurements, transfers **between Level 1 and Level 2**, the reasons for them, and the entity's policy for determining when transfers are deemed to occur; transfers *into* each level are disclosed and discussed **separately from** transfers out
- **IFRS 13.93(a):** for non-recurring measurements, the reasons for the measurement — the Standard's own example is an IFRS 5 asset written down to fair value less costs to sell
- Description of valuation processes used
- Highest and best use (if differs from current use for non-financial assets)
- For non-recurring fair values: reason for measurement

**Common pitfalls:**
- Confusing fair value (exit price) with transaction price or entry price
- Using entity-specific inputs rather than market participant assumptions
- Applying blockage discounts to Level 1 inputs
- Failing to assess whether the market is active before using quoted prices
- Inadequate Level 3 sensitivity disclosures
- Not considering transfer restrictions that market participants would price in
- Incorrectly including entity-specific synergies in valuations

- Categorising a broker or pricing-service quote by reference to the provider's methodology rather than to the observability of the inputs the provider used (IFRIC, January 2015)
- Treating IFRS 13.79 as an open licence to adjust Level 1 inputs; it is a closed list of three circumstances, and each adjustment demotes the measurement
- Omitting a **model-risk** adjustment from a Level 3 measurement — IFRS 13.88 requires a risk adjustment where market participants would include one when pricing the item, covering both the risk in the technique and the risk in its inputs
- Failing to calibrate a Level 3 technique to the transaction price at initial recognition (IFRS 13.64), so that day-two measurements drift without market support
- Giving a quantitative Level 3 sensitivity for non-financial assets in the belief that it is required — 93(h)(ii) is confined to financial instruments — or giving only a narrative for financial instruments where the quantitative disclosure is required
- Assuming IFRS 13 governs deferral of a day-one gain. It does not; the deferral rule is IFRS 9.B5.1.2A and applies only to financial instruments

**Related standards:** IAS 36 (impairment — fair value less costs of disposal is an IFRS 13 measure; value in use is not), IAS 40 (investment property — fair value model), IFRS 9 (financial instruments; the day-one gain deferral at IFRS 9.B5.1.2A), IFRS 3 (business combinations), IFRS 16 (leases — IFRS 13 **excludes leasing transactions from its scope**, but applies to a right-of-use asset presented as investment property and measured under the IAS 40 fair value model), IFRS 17 (insurance contracts — underlying items under the VFA; the transition fair value approach disapplies IFRS 13.47), IAS 24 (key management personnel, for the portfolio exception), IFRS 2 (share-based payment — excluded from IFRS 13's scope)

---

## IFRS 14 — Regulatory Deferral Accounts

**Scope:** Applies only to first-time adopters of IFRS that conduct rate-regulated activities (i.e., supply of goods or services at prices determined by a regulator). Entities already reporting under IFRS cannot apply IFRS 14. It is an interim standard — the IASB has a broader rate regulation project ongoing (IFRS 17 does not replace it).

**Status and amendments:** Issued 30 January 2014; effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2016. **IFRS 14 will be superseded by IFRS 20 *Regulatory Assets and Regulatory Liabilities*** (issued 27 May 2026, effective 1 January 2029) — see the IFRS 20 entry. **IFRS 14 was never EU-endorsed**: the European Commission deliberately decided not to launch the endorsement process for the interim standard and to wait for the final Standard. EU rate-regulated entities therefore have **no existing regulatory deferral account balances to carry forward**, which makes IFRS 20 a first-time recognition event in the EU rather than a change of model.

**Core principle:** A first-time adopter engaged in rate-regulated activities may continue to recognise regulatory deferral account balances in accordance with its previous GAAP, allowing comparability while the IASB completes its comprehensive project on regulatory assets and liabilities.

**Key rules:**
- **Permitted (not required):** The entity may elect to continue its previous GAAP accounting policy for recognising, measuring, impairing, and derecognising regulatory deferral account balances
- **Regulatory deferral account balances:** Amounts of expense/income that would not be recognised under other IFRS but are deferred because rate regulation allows recovery or requires return of amounts to customers in future periods
- **Separate presentation:** Regulatory deferral account debit balances (assets) and credit balances (liabilities) must be presented separately from all other items on the balance sheet; not classified as financial instruments
- **Separate line items:** Movements in regulatory deferral account balances presented as separate line items in the statement of profit or loss and OCI
- **Impairment:** Debit balances must be assessed for recoverability; impaired if the regulatory body will not allow recovery
- **Discontinued election:** If an entity ceases to apply the standard, it must derecognise all balances and cannot re-apply

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Nature of rate regulation and classes of regulatory deferral account balances
- Risks associated with the rate-regulated activities and regulatory balances
- Effects of rate regulation on financial statements (amounts recognised, movements)
- Judgements made in determining whether amounts qualify as regulatory deferral balances
- Rate-regulated activities and the regulatory framework governing them

**Common pitfalls:**
- Misapplying the standard to entities already reporting under IFRS (it is exclusively for first-time adopters)
- Treating regulatory deferral accounts as financial instruments
- Presenting regulatory deferral balances within other asset/liability line items rather than separately
- Not assessing impairment/recoverability of debit balances
- Failing to disclose the regulatory framework and risks adequately

**Related standards:** IFRS 1 (first-time adoption), **IFRS 20** (the permanent Standard that supersedes IFRS 14 from 1 January 2029), IAS 8 (accounting policies), IAS 36 (impairment), IFRIC 12 (service concession arrangements — applied before IFRS 20)

---

## IFRS 15 — Revenue from Contracts with Customers

**Scope:** Applies to all contracts with customers except: leases (IFRS 16), financial instruments and similar items (IFRS 9, 10, 11, IAS 27, 28), insurance contracts (IFRS 17), and non-monetary exchanges between entities in the same line of business.
