A customer is a party that has contracted to obtain goods or services that are the output of the entity's ordinary activities in exchange for consideration.

**Status and amendments:** Issued May 2014; effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2018 (deferred by one year from 2017 in September 2015); earlier application permitted (IFRS 15.C1). *Clarifications to IFRS 15* (April 2016) amended paragraphs 26, 27, 29, B1, B34–B38, B52–B53, B58 and C2, C5, C7, **deleted B57**, and **added B34A, B35A, B35B, B37A, B59A, B63A, B63B, C7A and C8A** — same effective date (IFRS 15.C1B); EU-endorsed 31 October 2017, OJ 9 November 2017. Subsequently amended by IFRS 16 (paragraphs 5, 97, B66, B70), IFRS 17 (paragraph 5) and **IFRS 18** (April 2024, paragraph 43 — applied when IFRS 18 is applied). IFRS 15 itself was EU-endorsed 22 September 2016, OJ 29 October 2016. **No amendment has been made to the five-step model since 2016.**

**Post-implementation review — completed and closed.** Carried out between September 2022 and September 2024; Project Summary and Feedback Statement published **30 September 2024**. The IASB concluded the requirements are **working as intended**: no fatal flaws in the clarity or suitability of the core objectives or principles; benefits to users not significantly lower than expected; costs of applying, auditing and enforcing not significantly greater than expected. Five matters were **deferred to the next agenda consultation**, all low priority: reporting **consideration payable to a customer**; assessing **control over services and intangible assets** in the principal-versus-agent test; applying IFRS 15 with **IFRIC 12**, including obligations to maintain or restore concession infrastructure; IFRS 15 with IFRS 10 (the "**corporate wrapper**" — selling an asset by selling the equity of a single-asset subsidiary); and IFRS 15 with IFRS 11 (**collaborative arrangements**). Whether the transfer of an asset is a **sale** in a sale and leaseback was deferred into the IFRS 16 PIR, which closed it in July 2026 with no action. **No further action** was taken on everything else, including identifying performance obligations, the variable consideration constraint, significant financing components, sales-based taxes, licences, royalties and the disclosure requirements.

**Timing point that matters for planning:** the "next agenda consultation" is no longer imminent. The IASB **suspended its Fourth Agenda Consultation in September 2025** and will instead run a **concurrent agenda consultation with the ISSB, with a Request for Information planned for 2027**. The five deferred matters therefore have no standard-setting timetable before 2027 at the earliest.

**Core principle:** Recognise revenue in a manner that depicts the transfer of promised goods or services to customers in an amount that reflects the consideration to which the entity expects to be entitled in exchange for those goods or services.

**Key rules:**
- **The 5-Step Model:**

  - **Step 1 — Identify the contract(s) with a customer:**
    - Contract must be approved, each party's rights identified, payment terms identifiable, commercial substance, and collection probable
    - **Contract modifications (IFRS 15.18–21).** A modification is a change in scope, price, or both, **approved by the parties** — in writing, orally, or by customary business practice; until approved, the entity continues to apply IFRS 15 to the existing contract (18). **IFRS 15.19 — an unpriced or disputed change order is still a modification:** a modification may exist even where the parties dispute scope or price, or have agreed the scope change but not the price, and where the price is undetermined the entity **estimates** the change to the transaction price applying paragraphs 50–54 **and the constraint in 56–58**. This is the paragraph most often missed in construction and engineering. **Separate contract (20)** only where *both* the scope increases by **distinct** goods or services *and* the price increases by an amount reflecting their **stand-alone selling prices**, with appropriate adjustment for the circumstances. **Not a separate contract (21)** — two mutually exclusive outcomes for the goods or services **not yet transferred**: (a) **prospective**, treated as termination of the old contract and creation of a new one, where the remaining goods or services are **distinct** from those already transferred; or (b) **cumulative catch-up** to revenue at the date of modification, where the remaining goods or services are **not distinct** and form part of a single partially satisfied performance obligation
    - Combining contracts: treat as one contract if negotiated as a package, consideration in one affects the other, or goods/services are a single performance obligation

  - **Step 2 — Identify the performance obligations:**
    - A performance obligation is a promise to transfer a distinct good or service (or a series of distinct goods/services that are substantially the same with the same transfer pattern)
    - **Distinct:** Capable of being distinct (customer can benefit from it on its own or with readily available resources) AND distinct within the context of the contract (separately identifiable — not highly interrelated/integrated, not modification/customisation, not highly interdependent)
    - **The series guidance (IFRS 15.22(b) and 23) is mandatory, not elective.** Where both criteria are met the distinct goods or services **must** be accounted for as a **single** performance obligation. The criteria are that **each** distinct good or service in the series would meet the IFRS 15.35 criteria to be satisfied **over time**, and that under IFRS 15.39–40 the **same method** would be used to measure progress towards satisfying each one. It bites on repetitive service contracts — cleaning, processing, transaction processing, hotel and facility management, and most "as-a-service" arrangements; treating each daily or monthly increment as its own performance obligation is not an available choice. What it changes is **allocation**: a single series obligation means variable consideration can be allocated to a **distinct increment within the series** under IFRS 15.85 rather than spread over everything, and it collapses what would otherwise be a large number of obligations for the remaining-performance-obligation disclosure

  - **Step 3 — Determine the transaction price:**
    - **Variable consideration:** estimate using the expected value method (probability-weighted) or the most likely amount (binary outcomes); include only to the extent it is highly probable a significant reversal will not occur (the constraint)
    - **The constraint (IFRS 15.56–59) — the five factors that increase the risk of reversal (57):** consideration highly susceptible to factors outside the entity's influence (market volatility, the judgement or actions of third parties, weather, obsolescence risk); the uncertainty is not expected to resolve for a long period; the entity's experience with similar contracts is limited or has limited predictive value; the entity has a practice of offering broad price concessions or changing payment terms in similar circumstances; and the contract has a large number and broad range of possible consideration amounts. **IFRS 15.59 requires reassessment at the end of every reporting period**, of both the estimate *and* the constraint. The IASB identified two specific failure patterns in the PIR: constraining to **zero** on initial recognition as a matter of extreme conservatism, and updating the transaction price only when the uncertainty resolves or an invoice is issued rather than when the "highly probable" threshold is met
    - **Significant financing component (IFRS 15.60–65):** the objective is the **cash selling price** (61); the assessment considers the difference between promised consideration and cash selling price, *and* the combined effect of the expected time between transfer and payment and prevailing market interest rates. **IFRS 15.62 — three circumstances in which there is no significant financing component regardless of the paragraph 61 assessment:** (a) the customer **paid in advance** and the timing of transfer is **at the customer's discretion**; (b) a substantial amount of the consideration is **variable** on a future event not substantially within either party's control (a sales-based royalty is the standard's example); (c) the difference arises **for reasons other than financing** and is proportional to that reason — payment terms giving either party protection against the other's non-performance, such as retentions. **IFRS 15.63:** the practical expedient is assessed **at contract inception** on the *expected* period between transfer and payment being one year or less; it is not a look-back. **IFRS 15.64 — the discount rate is the rate in a separate financing transaction between the entity and its customer at contract inception**, reflecting the credit characteristics of the party *receiving* the financing and any collateral, and **it is not updated after inception** for changes in interest rates or in the customer's credit risk; the IASB confirmed in the PIR that it will not require re-evaluation, including for inflation-indexed long-term contracts, and pointed preparers to IFRS 15.20–21 where a modification occurs. **IFRS 15.65:** interest revenue or expense is presented **separately from revenue**
    - **Non-cash consideration (IFRS 15.66–69):** measure at **fair value** (66); only where the entity **cannot reasonably estimate** that fair value does it measure the consideration indirectly by reference to the **stand-alone selling price of the goods or services promised in exchange** (67). **IFRS 15.68 — the variability rule:** where the fair value of the non-cash consideration varies **only because of the form** of the consideration (a movement in a share price), that variability is **not** subject to the constraint; where it varies **for reasons other than form** (because of the entity's performance), the entity applies paragraphs 56–58. **IFRS 15.69:** goods or services contributed by the customer to facilitate fulfilment are accounted for as non-cash consideration **only if the entity obtains control** of them
    - **Consideration payable to customer:** Reduces transaction price unless it is for a distinct good/service
    - **Sales-based and usage-based royalties on licences (IFRS 15.58, B63–B63A) — a carve-out from the constraint, not an application of it.** For a royalty promised in exchange for a **licence of intellectual property**, revenue is recognised at the **later of** (a) the subsequent sale or usage occurring and (b) satisfaction (or partial satisfaction) of the performance obligation to which the royalty has been allocated. **B63A:** the exception applies where the royalty relates only to an IP licence **or** where the IP licence is the **predominant item** to which it relates. The IASB confirmed in the 2024 PIR that it will **not** broaden this exception

  - **Step 4 — Allocate the transaction price:**
    - Allocate based on relative standalone selling prices (SSP) of each performance obligation
    - SSP estimation methods: adjusted market assessment, expected cost plus margin, residual (only if SSP highly variable or uncertain)
    - Discounts allocated to all performance obligations unless evidence that discount relates to only some
    - Variable consideration may be allocated entirely to one performance obligation if: the variable consideration relates specifically to that obligation AND allocation is consistent with the allocation objective

  - **Step 5 — Recognise revenue when (or as) performance obligations are satisfied:**
    - **Over time** if any one of three criteria is met:
      - Customer simultaneously receives and consumes benefits as the entity performs
      - Entity's performance creates or enhances an asset the customer controls as it is created
      - Entity's performance does not create an asset with an alternative use AND entity has an enforceable right to payment for performance completed to date
    - **The "no alternative use + enforceable right to payment" criterion (IFRS 15.35(c), 36, 37, B9) — the hardest test in the model.** *No alternative use (36):* the asset has no alternative use where the entity is either **contractually restricted** from readily directing it to another use during creation or enhancement, or **practically limited** from doing so once complete. **IFRS 15.37 requires the entity to consider the terms of the contract *and any laws that apply to it***, so the same contractual wording can give different answers in different jurisdictions. **IFRS 15.37 also sets the standard as continuous:** to have an enforceable right to payment, **at all times throughout the duration of the contract** the entity must be entitled to an amount that at least compensates it for performance completed to date **if the contract is terminated by the customer or another party for reasons other than the entity's failure to perform**. A right that crystallises only at completion, or only on certain termination events, fails. **IFRS 15.B9 decides most cases:** the compensating amount must **approximate the selling price of the goods or services transferred to date** — **not** compensation for only the entity's **potential loss of profit** on termination. A liquidated-damages or lost-margin clause is therefore *not* an enforceable right to payment for performance completed to date. The **March 2018** IFRIC agenda decision *Right to payment for performance completed to date* adds that only **the payment the entity is entitled to receive under the contract with the customer, relating to performance under that contract**, is relevant — rights against third parties, or amounts recoverable outside the contract, do not create the right. **For off-plan real estate and bespoke construction, over-time recognition cannot be assumed from "the asset is customised":** both limbs must hold, and the right-to-payment limb turns on enforceability under local law at every point in the contract
    - **At a point in time** if none of the over-time criteria are met; consider: right to payment, legal title, physical possession, risks and rewards, customer acceptance
    - **Measure of progress (over time):** Output methods (milestones, units delivered) or input methods (costs incurred, labour hours); must be consistent and applied to performance obligations of similar nature

- **Contract costs:**
  - **Costs to obtain a contract (IFRS 15.91–94):** capitalise incremental costs expected to be recovered (sales commissions). **IFRS 15.93:** costs that would have been incurred **regardless** of whether the contract was won are expensed as incurred, **unless explicitly chargeable to the customer regardless of whether the contract is obtained** — a carve-out routinely overlooked for bid and tender costs. **IFRS 15.94:** the practical expedient tests **the amortisation period the asset would have had**, not the contract term, so a commission on a one-year contract that is expected to be renewed, and that therefore amortises over the anticipated customer relationship, does **not** qualify
  - **Costs to fulfil a contract (IFRS 15.95–98):** capitalise if directly related to a specific contract, generating or enhancing resources used to satisfy future obligations, and expected to be recovered. **95(a)** expressly extends to an **anticipated** contract the entity can specifically identify, **including services under the renewal of an existing contract**. **97** — costs relating directly to a contract include direct labour, direct materials, allocations of contract management, supervision, insurance and **depreciation of tools, equipment and right-of-use assets used in fulfilling the contract**, and costs explicitly chargeable to the customer. **98 — mandatory expensing:** general and administrative costs (unless explicitly chargeable to the customer, in which case assessed under 97); **wasted** materials, labour or resources not reflected in the contract price; costs relating to **satisfied or partially satisfied** performance obligations, i.e. past performance; and costs the entity cannot attribute between satisfied and unsatisfied obligations
  - **Amortisation (IFRS 15.99–100):** on a systematic basis **consistent with the transfer of the goods or services to which the asset relates**, which may include goods or services under an anticipated contract under 95(a) — so the amortisation period can exceed the current contract term. **100:** the amortisation **must be updated** for a significant change in the expected timing of transfer, as a **change in estimate under IAS 8** (prospective, never a prior period adjustment)
  - **Impairment (IFRS 15.101–104):** a loss arises where the carrying amount exceeds the remaining consideration expected less the directly related costs not yet expensed. **102:** the remaining consideration is determined using the transaction price principles **but ignoring the constraint in 56–58**, then adjusted for the customer's credit risk. **103 — the ordering rule:** impair assets under other Standards (IAS 2, IAS 16, IAS 38) **first**, then apply the IFRS 15.101 test, then include the resulting carrying amount in the CGU for IAS 36. **104 — reversal is required** where the impairment conditions no longer exist or have improved, capped at the carrying amount that would have arisen net of amortisation had no impairment been recognised. This is a rare instance of mandatory reversal in IFRS
  - **Two agenda decisions that close off the common workarounds.** *Training Costs to Fulfil a Contract* (**March 2020**): training costs incurred so employees can service a contract are **within the scope of IAS 38** (IAS 38.5 lists training expenditure explicitly), so IFRS 15.95 is never reached, and IAS 38.69(b) requires them to be **expensed as incurred** — **the fact that the contract permits the entity to charge the training costs to the customer does not change that conclusion**. *Costs to fulfil a contract* (**June 2019**): where revenue is recognised over time using an **output** method, construction costs incurred relate to the **partially satisfied** obligation — past performance — so they are expensed under IFRS 15.98(c) and fail the 95(b) criterion. **They cannot be capitalised to smooth the mismatch between an output-based revenue measure and a cost-based spending profile.**

- **Licensing (IFRS 15.B58–B63B):**
  - **Right to access — three cumulative criteria (B58):** (a) the contract requires, or the customer **reasonably expects**, that the entity will undertake activities that significantly affect the IP; (b) the rights granted **directly expose the customer** to the positive or negative effects of those activities; and (c) those activities **do not themselves transfer a good or service** to the customer as they occur. Revenue is recognised over time.
  - **B59 — what creates a reasonable expectation:** the entity's customary business practices, published policies or specific statements. A **shared economic interest** (a sales-based royalty) may also indicate it, though it is **not determinative**.
  - **B59A is the decisive paragraph and is widely misapplied.** Activities significantly affect the IP only where either (a) they are expected to **significantly change the form or the functionality** of the IP, or (b) the customer's ability to obtain benefit is **substantially derived from or dependent upon** those activities (brand support and maintenance is the standard's example). It then states the corollary: where the IP has **significant stand-alone functionality**, a substantial portion of its benefit comes from that functionality, so the customer's ability to benefit is **not** significantly affected by the entity's activities unless those activities change form or functionality. **The types of IP named as having significant stand-alone functionality are software, biological compounds or drug formulas, and completed media content (films, television, music recordings)** — these are therefore presumptively **right-to-use**, recognised at a point in time.
  - **Right to use (B61):** a performance obligation satisfied at a point in time under IFRS 15.38 — **but revenue cannot be recognised before the beginning of the period during which the customer is able to use and benefit from the licence.** The standard's own example: where a software licence period begins before the activation code is provided or made available, no revenue is recognised until the code is provided.
  - **B62 — two factors to disregard** when classifying: restrictions of **time, geography or use** (they define the attributes of the licence, not the timing of satisfaction); and **guarantees that the entity holds a valid patent and will defend it** (defending a patent protects the entity's own asset and is not a performance obligation).
  - **Distinct licence:** assess separately; if not distinct, combine with the other goods or services.
  - **PIR outcome (September 2024):** the IASB found the licensing requirements working as intended and **no evidence of widespread diversity** in accounting for licence **renewals**. It **declined** to follow the FASB (ASU 2016-10), which requires revenue from a licence renewal to be recognised no earlier than the beginning of the renewal period. **Under IFRS this remains a judgement**, and an entity may reach a different answer from a US GAAP peer on the same renewal — the divergence is deliberate and permanent. The IASB also declined to define "a licence" or to clarify when the licensing guidance applies versus the general model (the SaaS-versus-licence question).

- **Principal versus agent — control is the principle; the indicators are subordinate:**
  - The assessment is made **for each specified good or service**, and an entity can be principal for some and agent for others in the same contract (IFRS 15.B34)
  - **Two-step framework (B34A):** (a) identify the specified goods or services — a specified good or service is a **distinct** good or service (or distinct bundle), which may be a *right* to a good or service to be provided by another party (paragraph 26); then (b) assess whether the entity **controls** each one, as defined in IFRS 15.33, **before transfer to the customer**
  - **The three ways a principal obtains control (B35A):** (a) a good or other asset from the other party that it then transfers; (b) **a right to a service to be performed by the other party**, giving the entity the ability to direct that party to provide the service to the customer on the entity's behalf; (c) a good or service from the other party that it **combines** with others — for example where the entity provides a significant integration service (paragraph 29(a))
  - **B35 — momentary legal title is not control.** An entity does not necessarily control a good where it obtains legal title only momentarily before that title passes to the customer. A principal may nevertheless engage a subcontractor to satisfy the obligation on its behalf
  - **Indicators (B37) are a tie-breaker, not a scorecard:** primary responsibility for fulfilling the promise; inventory risk before transfer *or after* transfer of control; and discretion in establishing the price. **B37A** — the indicators may be more or less relevant depending on the nature of the specified good or service and the contract terms, and different indicators may be more persuasive in different contracts. **They are consulted only where the control analysis is unclear**
  - Gross versus net then follows mechanically: a principal recognises the **gross** consideration to which it expects to be entitled (B35B); an agent recognises the fee or commission, which **might be** the net amount retained after paying the other party (B36)
  - **Where preparers get it wrong (IASB findings, PIR of IFRS 15, September 2024):** the most commonly reported failures are **overlooking the concept of control entirely** and going straight to the indicators, and struggling when indicators point in different directions. The difficulty is concentrated in **online e-commerce platforms, internet advertising, consumer goods and retail, fintech and technology** — where there is no inventory to reason from. The IASB expressly **declined** to expand the indicator list for services, on the basis that B34A, B37 and B37A already establish the primacy of control. This is settled: there will be no additional guidance
  - **IFRIC agenda decision, April 2022 — *Principal versus Agent: Software Reseller*.** A reseller of standard software licences gives pre-sales advice, negotiates price with discretion, orders from the manufacturer, and is invoiced by and invoices in its own name; the manufacturer issues the licences in the customer's name and warrants functionality. The Committee held that **the pre-sales advice is not a promise in the contract with the customer** — it has already been delivered at contract inception — so the **specified goods are the licences alone**. The outcome then turns on control and is fact-specific; the Committee pointedly did **not** reach a principal/agent answer. It noted that pricing discretion **may be less relevant where the market leaves the reseller little real flexibility** (B37(c)). Disclosure of the judgement is required under IFRS 15.123

- **Bill-and-hold arrangements:** Revenue recognised when customer has the good (control transferred), it was requested by the customer, it is identified as belonging to the customer, and it is ready for physical transfer

- **Repurchase agreements (IFRS 15.B64–B76)** — three forms: a **forward** (obligation to repurchase), a **call option** (right to repurchase) and a **put option** (obligation to repurchase at the customer's request) (B65).
  - **Forward or call option (B66):** the customer never obtains control, even with physical possession. Account for the contract as **a lease under IFRS 16** where the repurchase amount is **less than** the original selling price, or as **a financing arrangement** under B68 where it is **equal to or more than** the original selling price — **unless the contract is part of a sale and leaseback**, in which case the entity continues to recognise the asset and recognises a financial liability for the consideration received (IFRS 16.103). **B67:** compare repurchase price with selling price **taking account of the time value of money**. **B69:** if the option lapses unexercised, derecognise the liability and recognise revenue
  - **Put option below the original selling price (B70–B72):** assess **at contract inception** whether the customer has a **significant economic incentive** to exercise. If yes → account for the arrangement as **a lease under IFRS 16** (again, unless part of a sale and leaseback). If no → account for it as **a sale with a right of return** under B20–B27. **B71:** indicators of significant economic incentive include the relationship of the repurchase price to the **expected market value** at the repurchase date and the time until the right expires
  - **Put option at or above the original selling price (B73–B74):** if the repurchase price is **also more than** the expected market value, the contract is **in effect a financing arrangement**. If it is less than or equal to expected market value and the customer has no significant economic incentive, it is **a sale with a right of return**
  - Note the two errors this replaces: a below-market-price put with a significant economic incentive is a **lease**, not a financing arrangement; and financing treatment for a call option depends on the relationship of the repurchase price to the **original selling price**, which is the test that decides between lease and financing

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Disaggregation of revenue into categories that depict the nature, amount, timing, and uncertainty of revenue and cash flows
- Contract balances (contract assets, contract liabilities, receivables) — opening/closing balances, significant movements, revenue recognised from contract liabilities
- Remaining performance obligations (transaction price allocated to unsatisfied or partially satisfied obligations), with quantitative disclosures
- Significant judgements: timing of satisfaction of performance obligations, determining transaction price (variable consideration constraint, significant financing component), allocating transaction price
- Assets recognised from costs to obtain/fulfil contracts: amount, amortisation, impairment losses

**Common pitfalls:**
- Bundling distinct goods/services into a single performance obligation
- Failing to apply the variable consideration constraint, leading to premature revenue recognition
- Incorrectly assessing over-time vs. point-in-time criteria — especially the alternative use and enforceable right to payment test
- Misclassifying licensing as right-of-use vs. right-of-access
- Principal/agent misclassification, especially in digital platform or intermediary businesses
- Omitting the significant financing component adjustment
- Not assessing whether contract modifications create a new contract or modify the existing one
- Inadequate disaggregation of revenue disclosures

- Reaching for the B37 indicators before performing the B34A control analysis — the failure the IASB identified as most common in the PIR
- Treating the series guidance in IFRS 15.22(b) as an accounting policy choice
- Failing to estimate the transaction price for an approved-but-unpriced change order (IFRS 15.19)
- Applying the one-year practical expedient in IFRS 15.94 to the contract term instead of to the **amortisation period**
- Capitalising costs of past performance to align cost recognition with an output-based revenue measure (IFRIC, June 2019), or capitalising training costs because they are recharged to the customer (IFRIC, March 2020)
- Classifying a software or completed-media licence as right-to-access on the strength of ongoing support and updates, without asking whether those activities change the **form or functionality** of the IP (IFRS 15.B59A)
- Recognising revenue on a right-to-use software licence at contract signature rather than at code delivery (IFRS 15.B61)
- Updating the significant-financing discount rate for interest rate or credit changes after inception (prohibited by IFRS 15.64)
- Treating a below-market-price put option with a significant economic incentive as a financing arrangement; IFRS 15.B70–B72 make it a **lease**
- Constraining variable consideration to zero as a default, or failing to reassess the constraint at each reporting date (IFRS 15.59)

**Related standards:** IFRS 9 (contract assets within the ECL scope), IFRS 16 (leases; the repurchase-agreement and sale-and-leaseback interfaces at IFRS 15.B66, B70 and IFRS 16.99, 103), IFRS 17 (insurance contracts; the IFRS 17.8 fixed-fee service election), IAS 2 / IAS 16 / IAS 38 (impaired **first** under the IFRS 15.103 ordering rule; IAS 38.5 captures training costs), IAS 37 (provisions — onerous contracts), IAS 8 (judgements and estimates), IFRS 3 (contract assets and liabilities acquired), IFRS 18 (presentation of revenue and of interest under a significant financing component), **IFRS 20** (applied *after* IFRS 15: regulatory income and expense are presented immediately below IFRS 15 revenue), IFRIC 12 (service concession arrangements — construction and operation services revenue)

Note: **IFRIC 13** *Customer Loyalty Programmes*, **IFRIC 15** *Agreements for the Construction of Real Estate*, **IFRIC 18** *Transfers of Assets from Customers* and **SIC-31** *Revenue—Barter Transactions Involving Advertising Services* were all superseded by IFRS 15 (IFRS 15.C10). Do not cite them as live.

---

## IFRS 16 — Leases

**Scope:** Applies to all leases, including leases of right-of-use assets in subleases. Excludes: leases to explore for or use minerals and similar resources (IFRS 6); leases of biological assets (IAS 41); service concession arrangements (IFRIC 12); licences of intellectual property granted by a lessor (IFRS 15); and rights held by lessees under licensing agreements for intangibles (IAS 38). Practical expedients: short-term leases (12 months or less) and leases for which the underlying asset is of low value may be excluded from lessee balance sheet recognition.

**Status and amendments:** Issued January 2016; effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2019; earlier application was permitted only for entities also applying IFRS 15 at or before the date of initial application (IFRS 16.C1). EU-endorsed 31 October 2017, OJ 9 November 2017.

| Amendment | Paragraphs added / amended | IASB effective date |
|---|---|---|
| *Covid-19-Related Rent Concessions* (May 2020) | added 46A, 46B, 60A, C20A–C20B | periods beginning on/after 1 Jun 2020 (C1A) |
| *Interest Rate Benchmark Reform — Phase 2* (Aug 2020) | added 104–106, C20C–C20D | 1 Jan 2021 (C1B) |
| *Covid-19-Related Rent Concessions beyond 30 June 2021* (Mar 2021) | amended 46B, added C20BA–C20BC | 1 Apr 2021 (C1C) |
| ***Lease Liability in a Sale and Leaseback*** (22 Sep 2022) | amended C2, added **102A** and C20E | **1 Jan 2024**, earlier application permitted (C1D). EU-endorsed 20 Nov 2023, OJ 21 Nov 2023; UK adopted 11 May 2023 |
| *IFRS 18* (Apr 2024) | amended paragraph 49 | when IFRS 18 is applied — 1 Jan 2027 (C1E) |

The covid-19 practical expedient in IFRS 16.46A–46B is **spent**: 46B(b) confines it to reductions in payments originally due **on or before 30 June 2022**. Transition for the sale-and-leaseback amendment is **retrospective under IAS 8, but only to sale and leaseback transactions entered into after the date of initial application of IFRS 16** (IFRS 16.C20E) — transactions predating IFRS 16 adoption are not reopened.

**Post-implementation review — substantially complete.** Request for Information published **17 June 2025**, comment deadline **15 October 2025**. At its meeting on **21 July 2026** the IASB concluded **unanimously (12 of 12)** that IFRS 16 is **overall working as intended**, and: added to its **research pipeline** a project to mitigate higher-than-expected ongoing lessee costs, focused on **simplifying lease liability remeasurements and the discount rate requirements**; **tentatively decided** to add a **narrow-scope project** on how a lessee applies IFRS 16 and IFRS 9 to a **rent concession where the only change is the lessor's forgiveness of lease payments** (the lessee-side counterpart of the September 2022 lessor agenda decision); confirmed it will explore, within its *Statement of Cash Flows and Related Matters* project, requiring lessees to disclose the **components of total cash outflow for leases**; **removed the single-asset-entity sale-and-leaseback matter from the maintenance pipeline**; and decided to take **no action** on all other matters raised. The **Project Summary and Feedback Statement is expected in Q4 2026**, subject to Due Process Oversight Committee approval. Expect **no change to IFRS 16 measurement before then**, and any simplification to arrive via a research project — i.e. years away.

**Core principle:** A lessee must recognise a right-of-use (ROU) asset and a lease liability for all leases (subject to exemptions). A lessor classifies leases as either finance leases or operating leases.

**Key rules:**
- **Identifying a lease:**
  - A contract contains a lease if it conveys the right to control the use of an identified asset for a period of time in exchange for consideration
  - **Identified asset:** Specified explicitly or implicitly; substantive substitution rights by supplier preclude identification
  - **Right to obtain substantially all economic benefits** from use of the asset throughout the period of use
  - **Right to direct the use** of the asset: how and for what purpose the asset is used; or relevant decisions are predetermined and customer operates the asset (or designed it to dictate how it is used)
  - **Substitution rights set a high hurdle (IFRS 16.B14–B19).** A substitution right is substantive **only if both** (a) the supplier has the **practical ability** to substitute throughout the period of use — the customer cannot prevent it, and alternatives are readily available or could be sourced within a reasonable period — **and** (b) the supplier **would benefit economically** from exercising it. **B15:** a right exercisable only on or after a particular date or event is **not** substantive. **B16:** the evaluation is made at **inception** and **excludes future events not considered likely to occur at inception** (four named examples: a future customer agreeing to pay above-market rates; introduction of new technology not substantially developed at inception; a substantial difference between actual and expected use or performance; a substantial difference between actual and expected market price). **B17:** where the asset sits at the customer's premises, substitution costs are generally higher and more likely to exceed the benefits. **B18:** rights or obligations to substitute for **repairs, maintenance, malfunction or technical upgrade** do not preclude an identified asset. **B19: where the customer cannot readily determine whether the right is substantive, it must presume it is not.**
    - **IFRIC agenda decision, March 2023 — *Definition of a Lease: Substitution Rights*.** Two holdings. First, the assessment is made **per potential separate lease component (IFRS 16.B12 and B32)**, not across the contract as a whole — on the facts, 100 similar electric-bus batteries under one 10-year contract are assessed battery by battery. Second, **"throughout the period of use" is a real constraint**: where the supplier would not benefit economically from substituting a battery used for less than three years, the B14(b) condition **does not exist throughout the period of use**, so the substitution right is **not substantive** even though the supplier has the practical ability to substitute at any time. The Committee's headline observation: **IFRS 16.B13–B19 "set a high hurdle for a customer to conclude that there is no identified asset when an asset is explicitly or implicitly specified."** Contracts contain leases far more often than preparers assume
  - **Economic benefits from use (IFRS 16.B21) — two agenda decisions that mark opposite sides of the same line.** B21: economic benefits from use may be obtained directly or indirectly, by using, holding or sub-leasing, and include the asset's **primary output and by-products**, including potential cash flows derived from them.
    - ***Economic Benefits from Use of a Windfarm* (November 2021) — no lease.** A retailer and a windfarm generator are registered participants in a market where all electricity goes via the grid at a spot price. Their agreement swaps the spot price per megawatt for a fixed price, **settled net in cash**, and transfers all renewable energy credits to the retailer. The economic benefits from use are the **electricity produced (the primary output)** and the credits. The agreement "conveys neither the right nor the obligation for the retailer to obtain any of the electricity" — so although the retailer obtains the credits, a *portion* of the benefits, it does **not** obtain **substantially all** of them. **A virtual PPA or contract-for-difference over generation output is on the "no lease" side of this line**
    - ***Economic Benefits from Use of a Battery under an Offtake Arrangement* (April 2026) — lease.** The battery owner retains custody but is contractually obliged to operate the battery on the retailer's instructions, covering **100% of its capacity**; the battery cannot be substituted; the retailer pays a **fixed amount** for the right to use it regardless of charging or discharging, and the owner passes all market cash flows to or from the retailer, settling net. The Committee's key observation: the economic benefits from use are derived from the battery's **storage capability and capacity** — **the battery stores and releases electricity, it does not produce it** — and the retailer has the **exclusive right** to the entire capacity throughout the period of use and to direct whether, when and by how much to charge and discharge. **Legal and settlement form does not determine who obtains the economic benefits where the counterparty has an exclusive right to the asset's entire capacity, directs its operation, and the cash flows are passed through net.** The reasoning reaches tolling arrangements, energy storage offtakes and capacity contracts wherever a gross-pool market structure separates the operator of record from the economic beneficiary
  - **Right to direct the use — IFRIC agenda decision, January 2020, *Definition of a Lease: Decision-making Rights*.** A five-year ship contract where **many but not all** decisions about how and for what purpose the ship is used are predetermined in the contract, and the supplier operates and maintains the ship. **IFRS 16.B24(b) applies only where the relevant decisions are predetermined**; because not all were, the customer applies B24(a). Two holdings that change practice: **predetermination defines the *scope* of the right of use, it does not defeat control** — within that scope the customer has the right to make the decisions most relevant to changing how and for what purpose the ship is used; and **IFRS 16.B27 — operating and maintaining the asset is not directing its use**. Although operation and maintenance were "essential to its efficient use", the supplier's decisions in that regard did **not** give it the right to direct use. The contract contains a lease. Read with the April 2026 battery decision, this means **a supplier retaining physical custody and operational responsibility is largely irrelevant to lease identification** where the customer sets the operating instructions
  - **Lease term and the enforceable period (IFRS 16.18–21, B34–B41).** IFRS 16.18 defines the lease term as the non-cancellable period plus optional extension periods the lessee is **reasonably certain** to exercise plus periods covered by termination options the lessee is reasonably certain **not** to exercise. **IFRS 16.B34 is the gate:** determining the non-cancellable period requires determining the period for which the contract is **enforceable**, and "a lease is no longer enforceable when the lessee and the lessor **each** has the right to terminate the lease without permission from the other party with no more than an **insignificant penalty**."
    - **IFRIC agenda decision, November 2019 — *Lease Term and Useful Life of Leasehold Improvements*.** Two determinations that changed practice for month-to-month and evergreen leases. **"Penalty" means the broader economics of the contract, not only contractual termination payments** — where either party has an economic incentive not to terminate such that it would incur a more-than-insignificant penalty, **the contract is enforceable beyond the date it can be terminated**; the cost of abandoning or dismantling leasehold improvements is a penalty for this purpose. And **both parties must have the right**: "a lease is no longer enforceable **only when both parties** have such a right", so **if only one party can terminate without more than an insignificant penalty, the contract remains enforceable beyond that date**. IFRS 16.B35 completes the point: a lessee-only termination right is an **option** feeding the lease-term assessment; a **lessor-only** termination right means the period covered by that option is part of the **non-cancellable period**
    - **Leasehold improvements work in both directions.** Where an entity expects to use non-removable leasehold improvements beyond the date the contract can be terminated, their existence **indicates a more-than-insignificant termination penalty**, so under B34 the entity considers whether the contract is enforceable **for at least the period of expected utility of the improvements**; separately, IFRS 16.B37(b) makes significant leasehold improvements a factor in the reasonably-certain assessment. Under IAS 16.56(d) and 57 the improvements' useful life is in turn capped by the lease term. **The two must be assessed together, not sequentially.** A "rolling monthly" lease with material fit-out is rarely a short-term lease
    - **IFRS 16.B37 — the five factor groups:** contractual terms for the optional period against market rates (including variable or contingent payments such as termination penalties and residual value guarantees, and the terms of options exercisable after the initial optional period); significant leasehold improvements undertaken or expected; termination costs (negotiation, relocation, identifying and integrating a replacement asset, and returning the asset in a specified condition or to a specified location); the **importance of the asset to the lessee's operations** (specialised nature, location, availability of alternatives); and conditionality of exercise
    - **IFRS 16.B38 — the anti-avoidance rule:** where an option is combined with another feature (a residual value guarantee) such that the lessee guarantees a minimum or fixed cash return that is substantially the same whether or not the option is exercised, the entity **shall assume the lessee is reasonably certain** to exercise the extension (or not to terminate)
    - **IFRS 16.20 and B41 — reassessment is triggered only by a significant event or change in circumstances *within the lessee's control*.** B41 lists: unanticipated significant leasehold improvements; a significant unanticipated modification or customisation of the asset; and the inception of a **sublease beyond the previously determined lease term**. IFRS 16.21 separately requires the term to be revised whenever the **non-cancellable period itself changes** — on actual exercise or non-exercise of an option. Market rent movements and a change in the lessee's business plan are not, of themselves, triggers

- **Lessee accounting:**
  - **Initial recognition:**
    - **Lease liability:** Present value of lease payments not paid at commencement, discounted at the rate implicit in the lease (or incremental borrowing rate if implicit rate cannot be readily determined)
    - **The discount rate — IFRS 16.26 and Appendix A.** The lessee uses its incremental borrowing rate only where the interest rate implicit in the lease **cannot be readily determined**. Appendix A defines the IBR as "the rate of interest that a lessee would have to pay to borrow **over a similar term, and with a similar security, the funds necessary to obtain an asset of a similar value to the right-of-use asset in a similar economic environment**." The **September 2019 IFRIC agenda decision** restated those four limbs explicitly and added that the definition **does not require** the rate to reflect a loan with a similar payment profile — but that it would be **consistent with the IASB's objective** for a lessee to use, as a starting point, a readily observable rate for a loan with a payment profile similar to the lease, adjusted as needed. **A single group-wide borrowing rate applied to every lease is not defensible:** the rate must be differentiated at least by term, currency and economic environment, security and asset value. This is one of the two cost drivers the IASB put into its July 2026 research pipeline
    - **Lease payments included:** Fixed payments (including in-substance fixed), variable payments based on an index or rate (using rate at commencement), exercise price of purchase option if reasonably certain, payments for penalties for terminating if the lease term reflects exercise
    - **ROU asset:** Initial measurement of lease liability + lease payments made at or before commencement + initial direct costs + estimated dismantlement/restoration costs (per IAS 37)
  - **Subsequent measurement:**
    - **Lease liability:** Amortised cost — increased by interest (unwinding of discount at effective interest rate), decreased by lease payments; remeasured on reassessment
    - **ROU asset:** Cost model (less accumulated depreciation and impairment) unless:
      - Investment property (IAS 40 fair value model if adopted)
      - Underlying asset is of a class to which entity applies revaluation model (IAS 16)
    - Depreciation: over shorter of useful life and lease term (unless lease transfers ownership or exercise of purchase option is reasonably certain — then over useful life)
  - **Remeasurement — when the discount rate changes and when it does not.** This is the single most common lessee measurement error.
    - **Revised discount rate (IFRS 16.40–41):** required for (a) a change in the **lease term** under paragraphs 20–21, or (b) a change in the assessment of a **purchase option**. The revised rate is the interest rate implicit in the lease for the remainder of the term if readily determinable, otherwise the lessee's **IBR at the date of reassessment**
    - **Unchanged discount rate (IFRS 16.42–43):** for (a) a change in amounts expected to be payable under a **residual value guarantee**, and (b) **a change in future lease payments resulting from a change in an index or a rate**, including a market rent review. IFRS 16.43 gives the sole exception: where the change results from a change in **floating interest rates**, a revised rate reflecting that change is used
    - **Index/rate-linked timing rule (IFRS 16.42(b)):** the liability is remeasured **only when the change in cash flows takes effect** — not when the index is published, and not in anticipation. A CPI-linked lease with an annual uplift effective 1 July is therefore **not** remeasured at a 31 December year end for a CPI movement whose contractual effect falls in the following July
    - **IFRS 16.39 — the floor:** the remeasurement adjusts the ROU asset, but where the ROU asset has been reduced to zero and the liability reduces further, the **remaining amount goes to profit or loss**
  - **Lease modifications — the increase/decrease asymmetry (IFRS 16.44–46):**
    - **Separate lease (44)** only where **both** the modification **increases the scope** by adding the right to use one or more underlying assets **and** the consideration increases by an amount **commensurate with the stand-alone price** for that increase, with appropriate adjustments for the circumstances of the contract
    - **Not a separate lease (45)** — the lessee (a) reallocates the consideration in the modified contract under paragraphs 13–16, (b) redetermines the lease term under paragraphs 18–19, and (c) remeasures the liability at a **revised discount rate**: the rate implicit in the lease for the remainder of the term if readily determinable, otherwise the **IBR at the effective date of the modification**. Note the contrast with paragraphs 42–43: a **modification always revises the rate**; a **reassessment sometimes does not**
    - **IFRS 16.46 — the asymmetry:** for a modification that **decreases the scope**, the lessee **decreases the carrying amount of the ROU asset** to reflect the partial or full termination and recognises the resulting **gain or loss in profit or loss** (46(a)). For **all other** modifications — including price-only changes and scope increases not treated as separate leases — there is **no profit-or-loss effect**: the remeasurement is a **corresponding adjustment to the ROU asset** (46(b)). **Partial terminations hit earnings; rent renegotiations do not**

- **Lessor accounting:**
  - **Finance lease** (transfers substantially all risks and rewards of ownership):
    - Derecognise the underlying asset
    - Recognise net investment in lease (PV of lease payments + unguaranteed residual value, discounted at rate implicit in lease)
    - Recognise finance income over lease term using effective interest method
    - Indicators: ownership transfers; purchase option reasonably certain; lease term is major part of economic life; PV of lease payments substantially equals fair value; specialised nature such that only lessee can use without major modification
    - Manufacturer/dealer lessors: recognise selling profit/loss (same as outright sale) and finance income
  - **Operating lease** (does not transfer substantially all risks and rewards):
    - Continue to recognise underlying asset
    - Recognise lease income on straight-line basis (or another systematic basis if more representative)
    - Initial direct costs added to carrying amount of underlying asset and expensed over lease term
  - **Subleases:** the intermediate lessor classifies based on the **ROU asset**, not the underlying asset; if the head lease uses the short-term exemption, the sublease is an operating lease
  - **Lessor forgiveness of lease payments — IFRIC agenda decision, September 2022.** Where a lessor legally releases a lessee from specifically identified **operating** lease payments and nothing else in the contract changes, the accounting **splits**. **Amounts already recognised as an operating lease receivable** — contractually due but unpaid — fall under **IFRS 9**: IAS 32.AG9 makes individual payments currently due and payable a financial instrument, and IFRS 16.81 means the lessor has already recognised them as income, so under IFRS 9.2.1(b)(i) they are subject to IFRS 9 derecognition. The legal release means those contractual rights **expire** (IFRS 9.3.2.3(a)), so the lessor **remeasures ECL on the receivable, recognises the change in profit or loss, then derecognises the receivable and its allowance**. **Amounts not yet contractually due** are a **lease modification** under **IFRS 16.87**, accounted for as a **new lease from the date the concession is granted**. **The trap:** IFRS 16.87 requires prepaid or accrued lease payments from the original lease to be treated as part of the lease payments for the new lease, but the Committee held that **amounts recognised as an operating lease receivable are not "accrued lease payments"** — so neither those payments nor their forgiveness enter the new lease's payment stream. Rolling forgiven arrears into the straight-lined income of the new lease is wrong. **The lessee-side equivalent is unresolved** — it is the matter the IASB tentatively added to its pipeline on 21 July 2026

- **Sale and leaseback (IFRS 16.98–103, 102A):**
  - **IFRS 16.99:** whether the transfer is a **sale** is determined by applying the IFRS 15 requirements for when a performance obligation is satisfied
  - **Transfer is a sale (IFRS 16.100):** the seller-lessee measures the ROU asset at **the proportion of the previous carrying amount of the asset that relates to the right of use retained**, and consequently recognises **only the gain or loss relating to the rights transferred** to the buyer-lessor. The buyer-lessor accounts for the purchase under the applicable Standards and for the lease under the lessor requirements
  - **Off-market terms (IFRS 16.101–102):** where the sale consideration is not at fair value or the lease payments are not at market rates, adjust to measure sale proceeds at fair value — below-market terms as a **prepayment of lease payments**, above-market terms as **additional financing** from the buyer-lessor. Measure the adjustment on the **more readily determinable** of (a) the difference between the fair value of the consideration and the fair value of the asset and (b) the difference between the present value of contractual lease payments and the present value of payments at market rates. *(Illustrative Example 24: a building sold for CU2,000,000 against a fair value of CU1,800,000 gives CU200,000 of additional financing recognised as a separate financial liability, with the annual CU120,000 payment split into CU103,553 of lease payments and CU16,447 of financial-liability service.)*
  - **Transfer is not a sale (IFRS 16.103):** the seller-lessee **continues to recognise the transferred asset** and recognises a **financial liability** equal to the transfer proceeds, accounted for under IFRS 9; the buyer-lessor recognises a **financial asset**, not the asset
  - **Where all payments are variable — IFRIC agenda decision, June 2020, *Sale and Leaseback with Variable Payments*.** IFRS 16 does not prescribe a method for determining the retained proportion; an acceptable method is to compare **the present value of expected payments for the lease (including the variable ones)** with **the fair value of the asset** at the transaction date. Two conclusions that surprise preparers: **the seller-lessee recognises a lease liability at the date of the transaction even where every lease payment is variable and does not depend on an index or rate**, because the liability is a consequence of how the ROU asset is measured under 100(a); and **the ROU asset is not measured at zero**, because zero would not reflect the proportion of the previous carrying amount relating to the retained right of use. Worked figures from the decision: carrying amount CU1,000,000, sale proceeds CU1,800,000 (= fair value), present value of expected variable payments CU450,000 → retained proportion **25%**, transferred proportion **75%**; ROU asset CU250,000; gain CU600,000 (CU800,000 total gain × 75%); liability CU450,000
  - **Subsequent measurement — IFRS 16.102A (the September 2022 amendment, effective 1 January 2024).** After the commencement date the seller-lessee applies paragraphs 29–35 to the ROU asset and 36–46 to the lease liability, **but must determine "lease payments" or "revised lease payments" in a way that it would not recognise any amount of the gain or loss relating to the right of use it retains**. Applying that constraint **does not prevent** recognition in profit or loss of a gain or loss on **partial or full termination** under IFRS 16.46(a).
    - **This is the mechanical point most often got wrong.** For an ordinary lease, variable payments that do not depend on an index or rate are **excluded** from the lease liability and expensed as incurred. In a sale and leaseback, **IFRS 16.102A overrides that**: the seller-lessee builds a notional stream of "lease payments" that amortises the liability recognised at the transaction date, so that no part of the retained gain leaks into profit or loss over the lease term
    - **Illustrative Example 25 sets out two acceptable approaches**, either of which may meet 102A depending on the circumstances: **Approach 1 — expected lease payments at the commencement date**, producing an **uneven** stream across the term; and **Approach 2 — equal lease payments over the lease term**. **The difference between the cash actually paid and the notional "lease payment" that reduces the liability is recognised in profit or loss.** In the example — opening liability CU450,000, five-year term, IBR 3% — a Year 2 cash payment of CU99,321 produces a profit-or-loss charge of **CU1,197** under Approach 1 and **CU1,061** under Approach 2. The ROU asset is depreciated straight-line over the five years regardless (CU50,000 a year on CU250,000). **The choice of approach is an accounting policy** the seller-lessee develops, applies consistently and discloses


**Consequential effects of IFRS 18 on IFRS 16 presentation** *(annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027, when IFRS 18 is applied; IFRS 16.C1E)*

- **Statement of profit or loss — IFRS 16.49 as amended.** The pre-IFRS 18 text required interest expense on the lease liability to be presented separately from ROU asset depreciation, and treated that interest as a finance cost under IAS 1.82(b). **IFRS 18 replaces that:** interest expense on the lease liability, identified applying **IFRS 16.36(a)**, is classified in the **financing category** under **IFRS 18.61**. IFRS 18.B53(c) explains why — a lease liability arises from a transaction that does **not** involve only the raising of finance, because the entity receives a **right-of-use asset**, not finance — and IFRS 18.B54(c) names interest expense on a lease liability as an example of the income and expenses IFRS 18.61 requires to be classified as financing.
- **ROU asset depreciation stays in the operating category**, and for an entity presenting operating expenses **by function**, **IFRS 18.83(a)(i)** requires the single note total for depreciation to comprise the amounts disclosed under IAS 16.73(e)(vii), IAS 40.79(d)(iv) **and IFRS 16.53(a)**. Lease depreciation cannot be omitted from that note.
- **Statement of cash flows — the change most likely to be missed.** IFRS 16.50 is unamended: 50(a) puts principal repayments in **financing**, 50(b) applies "the requirements in IAS 7 for interest paid", and 50(c) puts short-term, low-value and unrecognised variable lease payments in **operating**. But **IFRS 18 amended IAS 7**: the old free choice in IAS 7.33 is **deleted**, and **IAS 7.34A(a) now requires an entity (other than one described in IAS 7.34B) to classify interest paid as cash flows from financing activities.** For most non-financial entities **the interest component of lease payments moves from operating to financing** on adoption of IFRS 18. IAS 7.34B–34D carve out entities that **invest in assets or provide financing to customers as a main business activity**. Practical effect: reported **operating cash flow rises** for most lessees on IFRS 18 adoption, with a matching reduction in financing cash flow — check covenant definitions, guidance and prior-year comparatives before the 2027 transition.
- **IFRS 16.47–48 are unchanged.** ROU assets are presented separately from other assets or, if not, included in the line item in which the underlying assets would be presented if owned, with disclosure of which line items include them (47(a)); ROU assets meeting the definition of investment property are presented as **investment property** (48).

**Disclosure requirements:**
- **Lessee:** ROU assets by class, depreciation charges, additions; lease liabilities (maturity analysis of undiscounted payments), interest expense on lease liabilities; short-term and low-value lease expenses; variable lease payments not in liability; subleases; sale and leaseback transactions; commitments for leases not yet commenced
- **Lessor:** Finance leases — net investment (maturity analysis, unearned finance income, unguaranteed residual values, allowance for credit losses); operating leases — maturity analysis of future lease payments, depreciation of underlying assets; significant assumptions and judgements

**Common pitfalls:**
- Incorrectly identifying whether a contract contains a lease — particularly in service agreements with dedicated assets and substitution clauses
- Using the incremental borrowing rate without rigorous estimation (especially for entities without direct borrowing)
- Forgetting to include extension option payments in the lease liability when reasonably certain to exercise
- Not reassessing the lease term when a significant event or change occurs
- Lessee applying operating lease accounting (income statement only) — not permitted under IFRS 16 except for short-term and low-value exemptions
- Lessors incorrectly classifying leases — applying operating lease treatment when risks and rewards have been substantially transferred
- Sale and leaseback: recognising the full gain on a sale and leaseback rather than only the portion relating to the buyer-lessor's retained interest
- Intermediate lessors classifying subleases based on the underlying asset rather than the ROU asset

- Revising the discount rate on a CPI or market-rent uplift. IFRS 16.43 requires an **unchanged** rate for index or rate changes; only a **floating interest rate** change revises it
- Remeasuring for an index change when the index moves rather than when **the change in cash flows takes effect** (IFRS 16.42(b))
- Recognising a gain or loss on a scope **increase** or a price-only modification — only scope **decreases** produce a profit-or-loss gain or loss (IFRS 16.46(a) versus 46(b))
- Treating an evergreen or rolling monthly lease as short-term where only the **lessee** can terminate cheaply, or where material non-removable leasehold improvements exist (IFRIC, November 2019; IFRS 16.B34–B35)
- Concluding there is no identified asset because the supplier holds a substitution right, without testing the **economic benefit** limb throughout the period of use, and without the B19 presumption that an undeterminable right is **not** substantive (IFRIC, March 2023)
- Using a single entity-wide or group-wide incremental borrowing rate across leases of different term, currency, security and asset value (IFRIC, September 2019)
- Measuring the ROU asset at zero, or omitting the lease liability, in a sale and leaseback where all payments are variable (IFRIC, June 2020)
- Excluding non-index-linked variable payments from the sale-and-leaseback lease liability after commencement and expensing them as incurred — **IFRS 16.102A requires the opposite**, so that no part of the retained gain is recognised
- Treating a supplier's retention of physical custody and operational responsibility as defeating lease identification; IFRS 16.B27 says operating and maintaining an asset is not directing its use
- Leaving lease interest cash outflows in operating activities after adopting IFRS 18 (IAS 7.34A(a) as amended)

**Related standards:** IAS 17 and IFRIC 4, SIC-15 and SIC-27 (all superseded by IFRS 16.C21 — a legacy IFRIC 4 reference in a transition note is correct in context and should not be edited away), IFRS 9 (lessor's net investment and its impairment; lessee derecognition of lease liabilities; the lessor-forgiveness split), IFRS 15 (the sale-and-leaseback transfer assessment at IFRS 16.99 and the repurchase-agreement interface), IAS 36 (impairment of ROU assets), IAS 37 (lease reinstatement and dismantling costs), IFRS 13 (fair value — applies to an ROU asset presented as **investment property** and measured under the IAS 40 fair value model; **IFRS 13 otherwise excludes leasing transactions from its scope**), IAS 40 (ROU assets meeting the investment property definition — IFRS 16.48), IFRS 3 (lease assets and liabilities acquired), IFRS 18 and IAS 7 (the 2027 classification of lease interest, in profit or loss and in the cash flow statement), IAS 16 (useful life of leasehold improvements, capped by the lease term)

---

## IFRS 17 — Insurance Contracts

**Scope:** Applies to insurance contracts issued (including reinsurance contracts issued), reinsurance contracts held, and investment contracts with discretionary participation features (DPF) issued by an entity that also issues insurance contracts (IFRS 17.3). References to "insurance contracts issued" also capture contracts acquired in a portfolio transfer or business combination, other than reinsurance held (IFRS 17.5). **Excluded** (IFRS 17.7): manufacturer/dealer/retailer product warranties (IFRS 15); employers' assets and liabilities under employee benefit plans (IAS 19, IFRS 2) and defined benefit plan obligations (IAS 26); rights or obligations contingent on the future use of, or right to use, a non-financial item (e.g. some licence fees, royalties, variable lease payments — IFRS 15, IAS 38, IFRS 16); manufacturer/dealer/retailer residual value guarantees and a lessee's residual value guarantee embedded in a lease; contingent consideration in a business combination (IFRS 3); insurance contracts in which the entity is the policyholder, unless they are reinsurance contracts held; and credit card and similar credit/payment contracts that meet the insurance contract definition where the entity does not reflect an assessment of the individual customer's insurance risk in pricing (though an insurance coverage component that IFRS 9 requires to be separated is brought into IFRS 17) (IFRS 17.7(h)).

Two irrevocable elections sit at the scope boundary and are frequently missed:
- **Financial guarantee contracts** are outside IFRS 17 *unless* the issuer has previously **asserted explicitly** that it regards such contracts as insurance contracts and has used insurance accounting for them. Only then may the issuer choose IFRS 17 instead of IAS 32/IFRS 7/IFRS 9 — contract by contract, irrevocably (IFRS 17.7(e)).
- **Fixed-fee service contracts** that meet the insurance contract definition may be accounted for under IFRS 15 instead, contract by contract and irrevocably, if all three conditions are met: pricing does not reflect an assessment of the individual customer's risk; compensation is by providing services rather than cash; and the insurance risk arises primarily from the customer's *use* of services rather than from uncertainty over the cost of those services (IFRS 17.8). Typical candidates: roadside assistance, maintenance and breakdown cover.
- **Any guarantee an entity issues** must be tested in a fixed order — financial guarantee contract first, then insurance contract, then other Standards — and the answer turns on the scoping requirements, **not** on the nature of the entity's business. See the March 2025 IFRIC agenda decision summarised under *IFRIC agenda decisions in force* below; it is the controlling guidance for corporates and banks that write guarantees over the obligations of joint ventures, associates or third parties.
- **Loans with death waivers** and similar contracts that cap compensation at the amount needed to settle the policyholder's obligation created by the contract: an irrevocable choice between IFRS 17 and IFRS 9, made **per portfolio** (not per contract) (IFRS 17.8A).

**Separation of components** (IFRS 17.10–13): apply IFRS 9 first to determine whether an embedded derivative must be separated; then separate a **distinct** investment component; then separate any promise to transfer distinct goods or non-insurance services under IFRS 15, attributing cash inflows under IFRS 15 and cash outflows directly where they relate to a component and otherwise on a systematic and rational basis (IFRS 17.12(b)). Everything remaining stays in the host insurance contract. Note the asymmetry: non-distinct investment components remain *inside* the IFRS 17 measurement but are excluded from insurance revenue and insurance service expenses (IFRS 17.85).

**Status and amendments:** *Issued May 2017; effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2023; earlier application permitted only for entities applying IFRS 9 on or before the date of initial application of IFRS 17* (IFRS 17.C1). Replaced IFRS 4 (issued March 2004), which had been an interim standard permitting grandfathered local practice.
- **Amendments to IFRS 17** — issued June 2020; deferred the effective date to 2023 and made targeted changes (acquisition cash flow allocation and asset, reinsurance loss-recovery, PAA and presentation).
- **Amendments to IFRS 9, IAS 39 and IFRS 17: Interest Rate Benchmark Reform** — issued 2019; EU effective 1 January 2020 (EFRAG, 23 Jul 2026).
- **Initial Application of IFRS 17 and IFRS 9 — Comparative Information** — issued December 2021; added ¶C28A–C28E and C33A (the classification overlay) (IFRS 17.C2A). EU-endorsed 8 September 2022, OJ 9 September 2022, EU effective 1 January 2023 (EFRAG, 23 Jul 2026).
- **IFRS 18 Presentation and Disclosure in Financial Statements** (issued April 2024, effective 1 January 2027) amended IFRS 17 ¶91, 96, 103 and B129; an entity applies those amendments when it applies IFRS 18 (IFRS 17.C2B). See "IFRS 18 interaction" below.
- **IFRS 19 Subsidiaries without Public Accountability: Disclosures** (issued May 2024) made consequential amendments; ¶C3(a) now refers to IFRS 19.178(f) for entities applying IFRS 19.
- **No amendments to IFRS 17 were issued in 2025 or 2026** (ifrs.org IFRS 17 landing page, checked 28 August 2026).
- **EU:** IFRS 17 (with the June 2020 amendments) endorsed 19 November 2021, OJ 23 November 2021, EU effective 1 January 2023 (EFRAG, 23 Jul 2026) — subject to the annual-cohorts carve-out described under *Level of aggregation*.
- **Post-implementation Review:** as at 28 August 2026 the PIR of IFRS 17 **has not started**. It sits on the IASB's *pipeline* projects list, not the active work plan, with the stated position "the IASB plans to discuss when to start the PIR of IFRS 17 in the second half of 2026". There is no Request for Information, no comment deadline and **no tentative decisions**. (The active PIRs are IFRS 16 *Leases* and IFRS 9 *Hedge Accounting*.) Do not confuse the IFRS 9 or IFRS 16 PIR timetables with IFRS 17's.

**Core principle:** Measure groups of insurance contracts at current fulfilment value — the expected present value of the cash flows required to fulfil the contracts, plus an explicit risk adjustment for non-financial risk — with the unearned profit held as a contractual service margin (CSM) that is released to profit or loss as insurance contract services are provided. Revenue reflects services provided, not premiums received.

**Key rules:**

- **Level of aggregation** (IFRS 17.14–24) — determined **once, at initial recognition, and never reassessed** (IFRS 17.24):
  - **Portfolio:** contracts subject to similar risks **and** managed together (IFRS 17.14). Contracts within a product line would be expected to share similar risks and so fall in one portfolio if managed together; contracts in different product lines (the standard's example: single premium fixed annuities versus regular term life assurance) would not, and belong in different portfolios.
  - **Profitability buckets:** each portfolio is divided into a *minimum* of three groups (IFRS 17.16): (a) contracts onerous at initial recognition; (b) contracts with **no significant possibility of becoming onerous** subsequently; (c) the remaining contracts. An entity may subdivide further if internal reporting distinguishes finer levels of profitability or degrees of onerousness (IFRS 17.21).
  - **Annual cohorts:** contracts issued **more than one year apart** cannot be in the same group; groups are subdivided as necessary to achieve this (IFRS 17.22). A group may end up as a single contract (IFRS 17.23).
  - **Set-level assessment:** if the entity has reasonable and supportable information to conclude a *set* of contracts will all fall in the same group, it may measure and assess the set rather than each contract; otherwise it must go contract by contract (IFRS 17.17, ¶47).
  - **PAA presumption:** for PAA groups, assume no contracts are onerous at initial recognition unless facts and circumstances indicate otherwise; assess the "no significant possibility of becoming onerous" bucket by the likelihood of changes in applicable facts and circumstances (IFRS 17.18). For non-PAA groups, that assessment is based on the likelihood of assumption changes and **must use** information from the entity's internal reporting — the entity cannot disregard it, but is not required to gather more (IFRS 17.19).
  - **Law-or-regulation relief:** where law or regulation specifically constrains the entity's practical ability to price differently for policyholders with different characteristics, contracts that would otherwise fall in different groups may be kept in one group. This may **not** be applied by analogy to anything else (IFRS 17.20).
  - **Onerous test at inception** (IFRS 17.47): a contract is onerous if the fulfilment cash flows allocated to it, plus any previously recognised insurance acquisition cash flows, plus any cash flows arising at initial recognition, are in total a **net outflow**. The loss is recognised immediately in P&L, the CSM is nil, and the liability equals the fulfilment cash flows.
  - **EU carve-out — annual cohorts (the one live EU carve-out; still in force at 28 August 2026):** Commission Regulation (EU) 2021/2036 of 19 November 2021, which endorsed IFRS 17 for EU use, includes an **optional exemption from the annual cohort requirement for intergenerationally-mutualised and cash-flow-matched contracts**. The Commission's rationale was that annual cohorts do not always reflect the business model or the legal and contractual features of such contracts, which represent more than 70% of total EU life insurance liabilities. Consequences of using it: the entity **must not** make an explicit and unreserved statement that its consolidated financial statements comply with IFRS Accounting Standards as issued by the IASB, and it must disclose that it has used the exemption. The Commission is required to review the exemption by **31 December 2027**, taking the IASB's IFRS 17 PIR into account, and to propose amending or ending it where appropriate — which is why the PIR timing above matters commercially.

- **Recognition** (IFRS 17.25–28F): a group is recognised from the **earliest** of (a) the beginning of the coverage period; (b) the date the first payment from a policyholder becomes due (deemed to be when received if there is no contractual due date (IFRS 17.26)); and (c) for onerous groups, when the group becomes onerous (IFRS 17.25). Contracts may be added to a group after a reporting period end, which can change the initial-recognition discount rate; the revised rate is applied from the start of the reporting period in which the contracts are added (IFRS 17.28, ¶B73).
  - **Asset for insurance acquisition cash flows** (IFRS 17.28A–28F): acquisition cash flows are allocated to groups on a systematic and rational basis, including to groups of expected *future* renewals; amounts paid before the related group is recognised are held as an asset (IFRS 17.28B) and derecognised into the group's measurement when it is recognised (IFRS 17.28C). The asset is tested for **recoverability** at each reporting date where facts and circumstances indicate impairment, with impairment and reversals through P&L (IFRS 17.28E–28F).

- **General Measurement Model (GMM / building block approach) — the default** (IFRS 17.29–52):
  - **Fulfilment cash flows (FCF)** = estimates of future cash flows + adjustment for time value of money and financial risk + risk adjustment for non-financial risk (IFRS 17.32(a)).
    - **Estimates of future cash flows** must be: *unbiased* and probability-weighted — the expected value of the full range of outcomes, using all reasonable and supportable information available without undue cost or effort; from the *entity's own perspective*, but consistent with observable market prices for market variables; *current*, reflecting conditions at the measurement date; and *explicit*, with the non-financial risk adjustment estimated separately (IFRS 17.33).
    - **Contract boundary** (IFRS 17.34): cash flows are inside the boundary while the entity can compel the policyholder to pay premiums, or has a substantive obligation to provide insurance contract services. That substantive obligation ends when either (a) the entity has the practical ability to reprice the **individual policyholder's** risk to fully reflect it, or (b) both: it has the practical ability to reprice the **portfolio's** risk to fully reflect it, **and** premiums up to the reassessment date do not take account of risks relating to periods after that date. Amounts outside the boundary are not recognised as assets or liabilities (IFRS 17.35).
    - **Discount rates** (IFRS 17.36): must reflect the time value of money, the characteristics of the cash flows and the **liquidity characteristics of the insurance contracts**; be consistent with observable current market prices for instruments with consistent cash-flow characteristics (timing, currency, liquidity); and exclude factors that affect those market prices but not the insurance cash flows. Two permitted constructions:
