In assessing whether it meets the objective, consider whether **several observable rates exist** (multiplicity suggests rates set to encourage or deter particular purposes, embedding an incentive or penalty); the **purpose** for which the currency is exchangeable (a rate available only for narrow purposes such as emergency supplies may not reflect prevailing conditions); the **nature** of the rate (a free-floating rate is more likely to qualify than one set by regular official intervention); and the **frequency of updating** (a rate unchanged over time is less likely to qualify than one updated daily or more often). *The first subsequent exchange rate*, where exchangeability is later restored — considering the **time between the measurement date and restoration** (shorter is better) and **inflation rates**, since in a high-inflation or hyperinflationary economy prices can change several times a day
  - **Another estimation technique.** The entity may use **any observable exchange rate, including rates from markets or exchange mechanisms that do not create enforceable rights and obligations** — parallel-market rates — **adjusted as necessary** to meet the 19A objective. **Note the asymmetry:** unenforceable-market rates are excluded from the *exchangeability assessment* but are permitted as an *input to estimation*
- **Translation to a hyperinflationary presentation currency — the November 2025 amendments, effective 1 January 2027:**
  - **The gap being filled.** IAS 21.39 covered a non-hyperinflationary functional currency translated into a non-hyperinflationary presentation currency, and IAS 21.42–43 covered a hyperinflationary functional currency. **Nothing addressed a non-hyperinflationary functional currency presented in a hyperinflationary presentation currency**, and practice diverged
  - **41A — the new method:** where the functional currency is that of a **non-hyperinflationary** economy but the presentation currency is that of a **hyperinflationary** economy, translate **all amounts — assets, liabilities, equity items, income and expenses, including comparatives — at the closing rate at the date of the most recent statement of financial position**. Comparatives are therefore **retranslated each period at the latest closing rate** rather than frozen. This mirrors the mechanic already in 42(a)
  - **41B — ceasing:** when the economy whose currency is the presentation currency **ceases to be hyperinflationary** while the functional currency remains non-hyperinflationary, stop applying 41A and apply 39 instead, **prospectively from the beginning of the reporting period in which the economy ceases to be hyperinflationary — do not retranslate amounts that arose before that date**
  - **47A — the carve-out for comparatives:** where the entity's **functional and presentation currencies are both hyperinflationary** (or are the currencies of different hyperinflationary economies) and it translates a **non-hyperinflationary foreign operation**, it shall **not** apply 41A to that foreign operation's comparatives. Instead it **restates the comparatives already included in its previously issued financial statements by applying the general price index it applies to corresponding figures under IAS 29.34**
  - **Paragraph 55 as amended:** financial statements presented in a currency other than the functional currency may be described as IFRS-compliant **only if they comply with the translation method in paragraphs 39, 41A, 42 and 47A**
  - **Transition (60P):** entities in the 47A situation apply the amendments **from the beginning of the annual period in which they first apply them**, restate the comparatives of their foreign operations using the IAS 29.34 index, and give the labelled comparative summarised information. **All other entities apply the amendments retrospectively under IAS 8.** No entity is required to give the IAS 8.28(f) quantitative-effect disclosure
- **Intragroup monetary items — classifying the exchange difference under IFRS 18 (IFRIC agenda decision, April 2026).** IAS 21.45 requires that an intragroup monetary asset or liability **cannot be eliminated against the corresponding intragroup liability or asset without showing the results of currency fluctuations**, because the monetary item is a commitment to convert one currency into another. IFRS 18.B65 requires exchange differences to be classified in the **same category** as the income and expenses from the item that gave rise to them — but where the loan's income and expenses have been eliminated on consolidation there is no "same" category. The Committee concluded **two readings are both reasonable** and the entity must choose one as an **IAS 8 accounting policy**: **View 1** — classify in the **operating** category as the IFRS 18.52 default; **View 2** — classify in the category in which the loan's income and expenses **would have been** classified before elimination, falling back to operating where that involves undue cost or effort (assessed **per item** under IFRS 18.B68), the classification being made **from the consolidated group's perspective**, which may differ from the subsidiary's own. Two readings were rejected as **not reasonable**: financing (on the basis that the transaction involves only the raising of finance) and investing (on the basis that cash conversion caused the difference).
- **Goodwill and fair value adjustments on acquisition:** Treated as assets/liabilities of the foreign operation and translated at the closing rate
- **Highly inflationary economies (IAS 29 interaction):** The financial statements of a foreign operation that reports in the currency of a hyperinflationary economy should be restated under IAS 29 before being translated at the closing rate

**Disclosure requirements:**
- The amount of exchange differences recognised in profit or loss (except for those arising on financial instruments measured at fair value through P&L)
- Net exchange differences recognised in OCI and classified in a separate component of equity, and a reconciliation of the opening and closing balance of that component
- When the presentation currency is different from the functional currency: that fact, the functional currency, and the reason for using a different presentation currency
- When there is a change in functional currency: that fact, the reason for the change, and the date of the change
- The entity's policy for translating goodwill and fair value adjustments
- When an entity disposes of a foreign operation: amount of exchange differences reclassified to P&L

- **Lack of exchangeability (IAS 21.57A–57B and Appendix A).** The objective (57A) is to enable users to understand how the currency not being exchangeable **affects, or is expected to affect**, the entity's financial performance, financial position and cash flows. The specified items are: (a) the currency and a **description of the restrictions**; (b) a description of **affected transactions**; (c) the **carrying amount of affected assets and liabilities**; (d) the **spot rates used**, stating whether they are observable rates used without adjustment or estimated using another technique; (e) a description of any estimation technique with **qualitative and quantitative information about the inputs and assumptions**; and (f) qualitative information about **each type of risk** to which the entity is exposed, **with the nature and carrying amount of the assets and liabilities exposed to each type of risk**. Where a **foreign operation's** functional currency is not exchangeable into the presentation currency (or vice versa), also disclose the **name** of the foreign operation, whether it is a subsidiary, joint operation, joint venture, associate or branch, and its **principal place of business**; **summarised financial information** about it; and the **nature and terms of any contractual arrangements that could require the entity to provide financial support**, including events or circumstances that could expose the entity to a loss
- **Translation to a hyperinflationary presentation currency (IAS 21.53A–53B, 54A, from 2027):** where 41A is applied, disclose the fact that all amounts — assets, liabilities, equity items, income and expenses and, where 47A is not applied, comparatives — have been translated at the closing rate at the date of the most recent statement of financial position (53A). Where a hyperinflationary-functional-currency entity applies 41A and 47A to foreign operations, disclose **summarised financial information about those foreign operations**, and **label the comparative summarised information** to identify that it was prepared using the IAS 29.34 general price index (53B). Where 41B is applied, disclose the fact that the presentation currency has ceased to be that of a hyperinflationary economy (54A)

**Common pitfalls:**
- Treating functional currency as a free choice (it is determined by economic facts, not management preference)
- Retranslating non-monetary items carried at historical cost at the closing rate
- Failing to retranslate monetary items at the closing rate and recognising the exchange difference
- Recognising the translation reserve recycling on partial disposals that do not result in loss of control
- Applying average rates to translate items where exchange rates fluctuated significantly (approximation is permitted only when rates do not fluctuate significantly)
- Not translating goodwill and fair value adjustments at the closing rate (treating them as parent-entity items)
- Forgetting to recycle the FCTR on full disposal of a foreign operation, or recycling on partial disposals that do not involve a loss of control/influence

- Using a parallel-market rate as the estimated spot rate **without testing it against the IAS 21.19A objective** and without adjustment — the guidance permits such a rate only as an **adjusted input**, and excludes it from the exchangeability assessment entirely
- Treating exchangeability as symmetric, or as a single entity-wide conclusion, rather than assessing it **per measurement date and per purpose** (IAS 21.8A)
- Concluding a currency is exchangeable on the strength of a black-market mechanism that would not create **enforceable** rights and obligations
- Giving the IAS 21.57A narrative without the **carrying amounts of affected assets and liabilities**, or without the risk-by-risk carrying amounts
- Omitting the foreign-operation disclosures — name, type, principal place of business, summarised financial information, support arrangements — where the restriction sits at a subsidiary
- Continuing to **freeze comparatives at historical rates** where the presentation currency is hyperinflationary and IAS 21.41A applies from 2027
- After a presentation currency ceases to be hyperinflationary, retranslating pre-transition amounts instead of applying IAS 21.41B **prospectively**
- Conflating the 2023 *Lack of Exchangeability* amendments with the 2025 *Translation to a Hyperinflationary Presentation Currency* amendments; they have different effective dates and different endorsement status

**Key interpretations:**
- **IFRIC 22 (Foreign Currency Transactions and Advance Consideration):** When advance consideration is paid or received in a foreign currency, the date of the transaction for the resulting non-monetary prepayment asset or liability is the date of initial recognition of the prepayment — not the date the related asset, expense, or income is subsequently recognised.

**Related standards:** IFRS 9 (hedge accounting for foreign currency risk), IAS 7 (translation of cash flows), **IAS 29** (hyperinflationary economies — IAS 29.34 corresponding figures now cross-refer to IAS 21.41A, 42(b), 43 and 47A), IFRS 3 (translation of goodwill and fair value adjustments), IFRS 10 (translation of foreign subsidiaries; IAS 21.45 survives elimination), IAS 28 (translation of associates and joint ventures), **IFRS 18** (classification of exchange differences, B65–B69), IFRS 17 (an insurance contract group, **including the CSM**, is a monetary item — IFRS 17.30), IFRS 19 (the reduced-disclosure equivalents at IFRS 19.219A–219B, 220A and 221–224), **IFRIC 16** (net investment hedges), **IFRIC 22** (foreign currency transactions and advance consideration), **SIC-7** (introduction of the euro)

---

## IAS 23 — Borrowing Costs

**Scope:** Applies to borrowing costs directly attributable to the acquisition, construction, or production of a qualifying asset. Excludes borrowing costs related to assets measured at fair value, inventories manufactured in large quantities on a repetitive basis, and biological assets within the scope of IAS 41.

**Status and amendments:** IAS 23 (2007 revision) is effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2009 (IAS 23.29). The most recent substantive amendment is ***Annual Improvements to IFRS Standards 2015–2017 Cycle*** (issued December 2017), which amended paragraph **14** and added **28A**, effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after **1 January 2019**, applied to borrowing costs incurred on or after the beginning of the annual period of first application. IFRS 16 (January 2016) and IFRS 9 (July 2014) amended paragraph 6. **No amendment since.**

**Core principle:** Borrowing costs that are directly attributable to the acquisition, construction, or production of a qualifying asset must be capitalized as part of the cost of that asset. All other borrowing costs are expensed in the period incurred.

**Key rules:**
- **Qualifying asset:** An asset that necessarily takes a substantial period of time to get ready for its intended use or sale (e.g., self-constructed buildings, power plants, large inventories, intangible assets under development)
  - **No bright-line threshold for "substantial period"** — judgement is required. There is no 12-month rule in IAS 23, and treating one as authoritative is not supportable
- **Directly attributable borrowing costs:**
  - **Specific borrowings (IAS 23.12):** the eligible amount is the **actual borrowing costs incurred on that borrowing during the period, less any investment income on the temporary investment of those borrowings**. The netting is required, not optional
  - General borrowings: apply a capitalization rate (weighted average cost of general borrowings outstanding) to expenditure on the asset
- **Capitalisation rate (IAS 23.14, as amended 2017):** the **weighted average of the borrowing costs applicable to all borrowings of the entity outstanding during the period**, excluding borrowing costs applicable to borrowings made specifically to obtain a qualifying asset — **but, per the 2017 amendment, only until substantially all the activities necessary to prepare that asset for its intended use or sale are complete.** Once a specifically financed asset is ready, **the related borrowing joins the general pool.** This is a common oversight on long-dated project finance that stays outstanding after commissioning. The rate is applied to **the expenditures on the asset** — the weighted average carrying amount of the qualifying asset during the period — not to total project cost.
- **Commencement (IAS 23.17):** all three conditions must be met simultaneously — the entity incurs expenditures for the asset, incurs borrowing costs, **and** undertakes activities necessary to prepare the asset for its intended use or sale. **"Activities" is broader than physical construction:** it includes technical and administrative work before construction begins, such as obtaining permits.
- **Suspension (IAS 23.20):** suspend capitalisation during **extended periods in which active development is suspended**. Do **not** suspend for periods in which substantial technical and administrative work is being carried on, or for temporary delays that are a **necessary part** of the process — the standard's example is high water levels delaying bridge construction in a region where such flooding is common.
- **Cessation (IAS 23.22, 24):** cease when **substantially all** the activities necessary to prepare the asset for its intended use or sale are complete. Physical construction being finished is normally enough even if routine administrative work continues, and minor modifications outstanding indicate substantial completion. **IAS 23.24 — part-by-part cessation:** where an asset is completed **in parts** and each part is capable of being used while construction continues on other parts, cease capitalising on each part as it is substantially complete. A business park of separately usable buildings ceases part by part; an industrial plant with sequential process stages on one site does not.
- **Excess over recoverable amount:** Capitalized amount reduced to the asset's recoverable amount under IAS 36

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Amount of borrowing costs capitalized during the period
- Capitalization rate used (when general borrowings are used)

**Common pitfalls:**
- Continuing to capitalize during genuine suspension of active development
- Failing to move a **specific borrowing into the general pool** once its qualifying asset is substantially complete (IAS 23.14, 2017 amendment)
- Failing to deduct investment income on the temporary investment of specific borrowings from the eligible amount, which IAS 23.12 requires
- Missing **part-by-part cessation** on a multi-component development (IAS 23.24)
- Treating "activities necessary to prepare the asset" as physical construction only, and so starting capitalisation late (IAS 23.17)
- Applying a capitalization rate to total asset expenditure rather than to the weighted average carrying amount of the qualifying asset during the period
- Failing to cease capitalization when the asset is ready for use even if not yet in use

**Related standards:** IAS 2 (inventories — qualifying assets, but inventories manufactured in large quantities on a repetitive basis are excluded), IAS 16 (PP&E — qualifying assets), IAS 36 (recoverable amount cap), IAS 38 (intangible assets under development), IAS 40 (investment property under construction), IFRIC 12 (borrowing costs are expensed as incurred **unless** the operator has a contractual right to an intangible asset — IFRIC 12.22), **IFRIC 1** (the unwinding of a decommissioning discount is a finance cost and **may not** be capitalised under IAS 23), IAS 7 (interest paid includes amounts capitalised — IAS 7.32)

---

## IAS 24 — Related Party Disclosures

**Scope:** Applies to all entities presenting IFRS financial statements. Requires identification and disclosure of related party relationships and transactions, including outstanding balances and commitments. The government-related entity partial exemption applies to entities controlled, jointly controlled, or significantly influenced by a government.

**Status and amendments:** IAS 24 (2009 revision) applies retrospectively for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2011 (IAS 24.28). Amendments in force: **IFRS 18** (April 2024, amending paragraph 20, applied when IFRS 18 is applied — IAS 24.28D); *Annual Improvements 2010–2012 Cycle* (December 2013, adding **paragraphs 17A and 18A on management entities**, effective 1 July 2014); *Investment Entities* (October 2012); and IFRS 10/11/12 (May 2011). **No amendment since April 2024.**

**Core principle:** Financial statements must disclose information about related party relationships, transactions, and outstanding balances — including commitments — that are necessary for users to understand the potential effect on the financial statements.

**Key rules:**
- **Related party definition — persons:** A person (or close family member) is related to the reporting entity if they:
  - Control or jointly control the entity
  - Have significant influence over the entity
  - Are a member of key management personnel (KMP) of the entity or its parent
- **Related party definition — entities:** An entity is related to the reporting entity if:
  - It is a parent, subsidiary, fellow subsidiary, or associate
  - It is a joint venture of the entity or of a group member
  - It is controlled or jointly controlled by a person identified above
  - A person with significant influence over the entity (or close family member) controls it
  - It has a post-employment benefit plan for employees of either entity
  - Note: Two associates of the same investor are NOT related to each other solely for that reason
- **Who is *not* a related party (IAS 24.11) — the negative list matters as much as the positive one.** Two entities are **not** related simply because they have a **director or other member of key management personnel in common**, or because a member of KMP of one has **significant influence** over the other. Two **venturers** are not related simply because they share joint control of a joint venture. And providers of finance, trade unions, public utilities, and government departments and agencies **that do not control, jointly control or significantly influence** the entity are not related parties merely by virtue of their normal dealings with it — **nor is a customer, supplier, franchisor, distributor or general agent with whom the entity transacts a significant volume of business, merely because of the resulting economic dependence**. In practice the most frequent related-party error is **over-inclusion**, not under-inclusion.
- **Management entities (IAS 24.17A, 18A, added 2013):** where key management personnel services are provided by a **separate management entity**, the reporting entity discloses **the amounts it incurred for those services** (18A) and is **not** required to give the paragraph 17 category-by-category analysis of that management entity's own employee compensation. The management entity is itself a related party of the reporting entity.
- **Key management personnel (KMP):** Persons having authority and responsibility for planning, directing, and controlling the activities of the entity, including any director (executive or non-executive)
- **Government-related entities — the partial exemption (IAS 24.25–27).** A reporting entity is **exempt from the paragraph 18 disclosure requirements** in respect of transactions and outstanding balances, including commitments, with **(a) a government that has control, joint control or significant influence over it**, and **(b) another entity that is a related party because the same government has control, joint control or significant influence over both**. Where the exemption is used, the entity **must instead disclose** (IAS 24.26): the **name of the government and the nature of its relationship** with the entity; and **the nature and amount of each individually significant transaction**, plus, for other transactions that are collectively but not individually significant, a **qualitative or quantitative indication of their extent**. **IAS 24.27:** in judging the level of detail, consider the closeness of the relationship and other factors relevant to significance — whether the transaction is significant in size, carried out on non-market terms, outside normal day-to-day business operations, disclosed to regulatory or supervisory authorities, reported to senior management, or subject to shareholder approval. **The exemption removes the counterparty-by-counterparty detail, not the obligation to describe significant transactions** — which is the practical crux for state-owned enterprises.
- **Arm's length assertion (IAS 24.23):** disclosures that related party transactions were made on terms equivalent to those prevailing in arm's length transactions are made **only if such terms can be substantiated**

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Name of parent and ultimate controlling party (even if no transactions occurred)
- Compensation of KMP in total and split by category (short-term employee benefits, post-employment, other long-term, termination, share-based payment)
- For each category of related party: nature of the relationship, amounts of transactions, outstanding balances and their terms, provisions for doubtful debts, bad debt expense
- Guarantees given or received
- Commitments with related parties

**Common pitfalls:**
- Omitting transactions with entities under common control of KMP's family members
- Disclosing only net amounts rather than gross transaction amounts
- Asserting arm's length terms without substantiation
- Failing to identify post-employment benefit plans as related parties
- Missing the ultimate controlling party disclosure when it differs from the immediate parent

- Assuming a **common director** makes two entities related parties. IAS 24.11 says it does not
- Treating **economic dependence** on a major customer or supplier as a related party relationship
- Applying the government-related exemption and then giving **no** transaction information — IAS 24.26 still requires the government's name, the nature of the relationship, and each individually significant transaction
- Missing the **IAS 24.18A management-entity disclosure** where key management personnel are supplied under a management contract

**Related standards:** IFRS 10 (consolidated financial statements — control definition), IFRS 11 (joint arrangements), IAS 27 (separate financial statements), IAS 28 (associates)

---

## IAS 26 — Accounting and Reporting by Retirement Benefit Plans

**Scope:** Applies to the financial statements of retirement benefit plans themselves (i.e., the plan as a reporting entity), not to the employer entity's financial statements. Covers both defined contribution and defined benefit plans. Does not apply to employee benefit costs as reported by employers (that is IAS 19).

**Status and amendments:** IAS 26 became operative for periods beginning on or after 1 January 1988 (IAS 26.37). The **only** subsequent amendment in force is from *Disclosure of Accounting Policies* (issued February 2021), which amended paragraph 34, effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2023 (IAS 26.38). Note that IFRS 18 carried the material-accounting-policy-information requirements from IAS 1 to **IAS 8 *Basis of Preparation of Financial Statements***, so the cross-reference behind IAS 26.34 runs to IAS 8 from 2027. **IAS 26 is effectively unmaintained: it has had no substantive revision since 1994.**

**Core principle:** Financial statements of a retirement benefit plan must provide information about the plan's resources and activities that is useful to assess the plan's ability to meet its obligations to current and future beneficiaries.

**Key rules:**
- **Defined contribution plans:**
  - Report a statement of net assets available for benefits
  - Report a description of the funding policy
- **Measurement applies to all plans, DC and DB alike (IAS 26.32): retirement benefit plan investments shall be carried at fair value.** For marketable securities, fair value is market value. Where an estimate of fair value is **not possible**, **disclose the reason** why fair value is not used. (A May 2010 agenda decision on the valuation of plan assets is annotated to this paragraph.)
- **IAS 26.13 (defined contribution plans):** the financial statements shall contain a **statement of net assets available for benefits** and a **description of the funding policy**.
- **IAS 26.17 (defined benefit plans):** the financial statements shall contain **either** (a) a statement showing net assets available for benefits, the actuarial present value of promised retirement benefits **distinguishing vested from non-vested**, and the resulting excess or deficit; **or** (b) a statement of net assets available for benefits **including either** a note disclosing the actuarial present value distinguishing vested from non-vested, **or** a reference to that information in an accompanying actuarial report.
- **IAS 26.18 and 23:** the actuarial present value is based on the benefits promised under the plan's terms **on service rendered to date**, using either **current** or **projected** salary levels **with disclosure of the basis used**; the effect of any changes in actuarial assumptions that had a significant effect must also be disclosed. Both bases are permitted — the current-salary basis measures the obligation on wind-up; the projected-salary basis reflects the going-concern reality that benefits will be based on final pay.
- **IAS 26.28:** for defined benefit plans, information is presented in one of several formats reflecting different practices in the disclosure and presentation of actuarial information.
- **IAS 26.34–36:** the financial statements of any plan must also contain a statement of changes in net assets available for benefits, a summary of significant accounting policies, and a description of the plan and the effect of any changes during the period. Paragraph 36 lists what the plan's **report** (as distinct from its financial statements) typically contains — names of employers and employee groups covered, number of participants, type of plan, whether participants contribute, a description of the benefits promised, the plan termination terms, and changes in any of these during the period.
- **Defined benefit plans:**
  - Report a statement of net assets available for benefits
  - Report a statement of changes in net assets available for benefits
  - Actuarial present value of promised retirement benefits (distinguishing between vested and non-vested) must be disclosed, either:
    - In the financial statements, or
    - In an accompanying actuarial report cross-referenced to the financial statements
  - If actuarial valuation not obtained at reporting date, must use most recent valuation as a basis and disclose the date
  - Investments: reported at fair value; for plan assets, market value is used; if fair value cannot be estimated, disclosure of reason and range in which fair value likely falls
- **Actuarial present value of promised benefits:** Based on benefits promised under the plan's terms using either:
  - Current salary levels, or
  - Projected salary levels — method chosen must be disclosed
- **Relationship between net assets and actuarial present value:** If actuarial present value exceeds net assets available, the plan is in deficit — this relationship must be transparent in the financial statements

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Statement of changes in net assets available for benefits (contributions, investment income, benefits paid, expenses, gains/losses on investments)
- Summary of significant accounting policies, particularly investment valuation basis
- Description of the plan and any changes during the period
- Description of any investment policies
- For defined benefit: actuarial assumptions, method used, date of most recent actuarial valuation

**Common pitfalls:**
- Confusing IAS 26 (plan's own financial statements) with IAS 19 (employer accounting)
- Using cost rather than fair value for plan investments
- Omitting the actuarial present value disclosure or failing to cross-reference an actuarial report
- Not disclosing whether projected or current salaries were used in actuarial valuations

- Applying **IAS 19** measurement — projected unit credit, the asset ceiling, OCI remeasurements — to the **plan's own** financial statements. IAS 26 uses actuarial present value and fair value, and **there is no asset ceiling**
- Carrying plan investments at cost, or omitting the IAS 26.32 explanation where fair value cannot be estimated
- Assuming IAS 26 has been modernised. It has not; the only change since 1994 is the accounting policy wording in paragraph 34

**Related standards:** IAS 19 (employee benefits — the **employer's** perspective; IAS 26 is the **plan's own** financial statements), IFRS 13 (fair value measurement of plan assets), IAS 8 (the destination of the IAS 26.34 accounting policy cross-reference from 2027), IFRS 17 (excluded — IFRS 17.7(b) scopes out defined benefit plan obligations under IAS 26)

---

## IAS 27 — Separate Financial Statements

**Scope:** Applies when an entity elects to or is required by local regulation to present separate financial statements. Separate financial statements are those presented in addition to (or instead of, for certain investment entities) consolidated financial statements. Does not apply to consolidated financial statements (IFRS 10) or equity method accounting in consolidated statements (IAS 28).

**Status and amendments:** Reissued May 2011 (the consolidation requirements having moved to IFRS 10); effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2013 (EU effective 1 January 2014; endorsed 11 December 2012, OJ 29 December 2012). Amended by *Investment Entities* (October 2012, adding **IAS 27.8A, 11A–11B and 16A**) and ***Equity Method in Separate Financial Statements*** (issued 12 August 2014, adding the equity method as the third option in **IAS 27.10(c)**; effective 1 January 2016, earlier application permitted; EU-endorsed 18 December 2015, OJ 23 December 2015). **Amendments to the IAS 27 disclosure requirements are proposed in the Equity Method exposure draft and remained in redeliberation at 28 August 2026.**

**Core principle:** In separate financial statements, investments in subsidiaries, associates, and joint ventures must be accounted for either at cost, in accordance with IFRS 9 (fair value), or using the equity method — with the same method applied to each category of investment.

**Key rules:**
- **What separate financial statements are — and are not:**
  - **IAS 27.6:** statements presented **in addition to** consolidated financial statements, or in addition to the statements of an investor with no subsidiaries but with equity-accounted associates or joint ventures — except in the IAS 27.8–8A cases
  - **IAS 27.7: the financial statements of an entity that has no subsidiary, associate, or joint venturer's interest in a joint venture are NOT separate financial statements.** They are simply its financial statements, and IAS 27 does not apply
  - **IAS 27.8:** a parent exempt from consolidation under IFRS 10.4(a), or an entity exempt from the equity method under IAS 28.17, **may** present separate financial statements as its only financial statements
  - **IAS 27.8A:** an investment entity required to apply the IFRS 10.31 exception to **all** its subsidiaries throughout the current and all comparative periods **presents separate financial statements as its only financial statements**. They **are** separate financial statements — there simply are no consolidated ones
- **Three permitted measurement methods for investments:**
  - Cost method: dividends recognized as income when the right to receive is established; no adjustment for the investee's post-acquisition profits
  - IFRS 9 (fair value): changes in fair value through profit or loss or OCI (for equity instruments designated at FVOCI)
  - Equity method (per IAS 28): share of investee's profit/loss and OCI recognized; permitted but not required
- **Consistency requirement:** Once a method is chosen for a category (e.g., subsidiaries), it must be applied consistently to all investments within that category
- **Dividends (IAS 27.12):** recognised when the entity's right to receive is established, **in profit or loss unless the equity method is used**, in which case the dividend reduces the carrying amount of the investment.
  - **The impairment trigger is IAS 36.12(h), not IAS 27, and it has two limbs.** Recognising a dividend from a subsidiary, joint venture or associate is an impairment indicator where evidence is available that **(i)** the carrying amount of the investment in the separate financial statements **exceeds the carrying amounts in the consolidated financial statements of the investee's net assets, including associated goodwill**; or **(ii)** the dividend **exceeds the total comprehensive income** of the investee in the period the dividend is declared. **Limb (i) is the one commonly overlooked**
- **Forced consistency with the group position (IAS 27.11, 11A):**
  - **IAS 27.11:** if the entity elects the **IAS 28.18 fair value option** for an associate or joint venture, it **must** account for that investment the same way in its separate financial statements. The IAS 28 election is not free-standing
  - **IAS 27.11A:** if a parent is required by IFRS 10.31 to measure a subsidiary at FVTPL, it must account for that subsidiary the same way in its separate financial statements. An investment entity does **not** consolidate its subsidiaries (IFRS 10.31); where it consolidates an IFRS 10.32 service subsidiary it presents consolidated financial statements and may in addition present separate financial statements
- **Change of investment entity status (IAS 27.11B):** on **ceasing** to be an investment entity, account for the investment under IAS 27.10 with the date of the change of status as the deemed acquisition date and the fair value at that date as deemed consideration. On **becoming** an investment entity, measure at FVTPL under IFRS 9, recognise the difference between the previous carrying amount and fair value at that date **in profit or loss**, and treat any cumulative OCI on those subsidiaries as if they had been disposed of at that date.
- **Group reorganisations (IAS 27.13):** where a new parent is inserted above an existing parent by issuing equity in exchange for existing equity, the assets and liabilities of the new and original groups are the same immediately before and after, and the owners retain the same absolute and relative interests, **and the new parent accounts for its investment at cost under IAS 27.10(a)**, it measures **cost at the carrying amount of *its share of* the equity items shown in the separate financial statements of the original parent** at the date of the reorganisation. Per the IFRS IC (September 2011), **IAS 27.13–14 apply only where the assets and liabilities of the new and original groups are identical** — they do **not** apply, even by analogy, to a reorganisation that results in a new intermediate parent with **more than one direct subsidiary**.
- **IFRS 18 interaction — the April 2026 IFRIC agenda decision on a parent's separate financial statements.** This is the most consequential recent development for holding companies.
  - IFRS 18.53(a) classifies income and expenses from investments in **unconsolidated subsidiaries** in the **investing** category unless the entity invests in that type of asset as a **specified main business activity**, in which case IFRS 18.55 requires the **operating** category (equity-accounted amounts excepted — IFRS 18.B42). **IFRS 18.B44(c)** confirms that "investments in unconsolidated subsidiaries" **includes** subsidiaries measured at cost in separate financial statements applying **IAS 27.10(a)**
  - The assessment is made **for the reporting entity as a whole** (IFRS 18.B37), so **the parent's conclusion for its separate financial statements can differ from the group's**. Whether investing is a main business activity is "a matter of fact and not merely an assertion" and must be evidenced (IFRS 18.B33); IFRS 18.B34–B36 give example factors (an important operating-performance subtotal; an IFRS 8 reportable segment comprising a single business activity)
  - **The Committee's conclusion:** a non-investment-entity ultimate parent whose only activities are holding investments in subsidiaries, deciding on their management, acquisition and disposal, and distributing returns, and which provides **no** segment analysis or operating subtotals in its separate financial statements, **does** have a specified main business activity of investing in unconsolidated subsidiaries. **The absence of the B34–B36 factors is not determinative**, nor is the absence of a parent from the B31 examples, which are not exhaustive. Applying IFRS 18.55(b), the parent classifies the income and expenses from those investments in the **operating** category
  - **Practical effect:** many pure holding companies expecting dividend income in the investing category must classify it as **operating** from their first IFRS 18 period. Where the parent has more than one business activity the conclusion turns on facts and needs documented judgement
- **Mergers into the parent (IFRS IC, November 2023):** where a parent carrying an investment under IAS 27.10 merges with a subsidiary that contains a business, so that the subsidiary's business becomes part of the parent, the Committee found little diversity in whether the acquisition method applies in the parent's separate financial statements. Treat this as an area to **document** rather than assume.
- **Pending changes.** The Equity Method exposure draft's proposals apply where the equity method is used in separate financial statements. The IASB tentatively decided (June 2026) that an investor applying the equity method to associates or joint ventures in its separate financial statements **may choose independently** whether to restrict or fully recognise gains and losses on transactions with associates — it need **not** apply the same policy in its consolidated and separate statements; a parent applying the equity method to *subsidiaries* in separate financial statements has the same choice. The IASB also decided **not** to add to the project's scope questions on measuring cost, step acquisitions, or loss of control of a subsidiary carried **at cost** in separate financial statements — those gaps remain open.
- **Identification:** Separate financial statements must be clearly identified as such and distinguished from consolidated financial statements

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Fact that the financial statements are separate financial statements
- List of significant investments (subsidiaries, associates, joint ventures): name, registered office, country of incorporation, proportion of ownership and voting rights held
- Accounting method used for each category of investments
- If an entity is exempt from consolidation (IFRS 10), the name and principal place of business of the entity that produces consolidated financial statements
- For investment entities: description of nature of the entity, significant judgments in applying the investment entity definition
- **IAS 27.16** — where a parent uses the IFRS 10.4(a) consolidation exemption: state that the statements are separate financial statements and that the exemption was used; name the entity whose IFRS consolidated financial statements are publicly available, its principal place of business (and country of incorporation if different), **and the address where those statements are obtainable**; list significant investments with names, principal places of business, and proportions of ownership and voting rights; and describe the method used for each
- **IAS 27.16A** — where an investment entity presents separate financial statements as its only financial statements under IAS 27.8A: disclose that fact and give the IFRS 12 investment-entity disclosures
- **IAS 27.17** — for everyone else: identify the IFRS 10 / IFRS 11 / IAS 28 financial statements to which the separate statements relate, state that they are separate financial statements **and the reasons for preparing them if not required by law**, and give the same investment list and method description

**Common pitfalls:**
- Using different measurement methods for different subsidiaries within the same category
- Recognizing dividends from subsidiaries as income under cost method without assessing whether they indicate impairment
- Confusing cost method with equity method treatment
- Failing to identify the financial statements clearly as "separate financial statements"

- Calling a standalone entity's financial statements "separate financial statements" when it has no subsidiary, associate or joint venture — **IAS 27.7 says they are not**
- Treating the IAS 28.18 fair value option or the IFRS 10.31 investment entity measurement as free choices at the separate-financial-statements level. **IAS 27.11 and 11A force the same treatment through**
- Applying only the IAS 36.12(h)(ii) "dividend exceeds total comprehensive income" test and missing limb (i) — carrying amount in the separate statements exceeding the investee's consolidated net assets including goodwill
- Applying IAS 27.13 by analogy to a reorganisation creating an intermediate parent with **more than one direct subsidiary** (IFRS IC, September 2011 — it does not apply)
- Omitting the IAS 27.16(a) **address at which the consolidated financial statements are obtainable**, or the IAS 27.17(a) **reasons for preparing separate statements where not required by law**
- Carrying the group's IFRS 18 main-business-activity conclusion into the parent's separate financial statements. IFRS 18.B37 requires a **per-reporting-entity** assessment

**Related standards:** IFRS 10 (consolidated financial statements; the IFRS 10.4(a) exemption and the IFRS 10.31 investment entity measurement), IFRS 11 (joint arrangements — a joint operator uses the **same** IFRS 11.20–22 accounting in its separate statements), IAS 28 (equity method, available in separate statements since the August 2014 amendments; the IAS 28.18 fair value option), IFRS 9 (fair value measurement of investments), IFRS 12 (investment entity disclosures), **IAS 36.12(h)** (the two-limb dividend impairment indicator), IFRS 13 (fair value), **IFRS 18** (the specified-main-business-activity assessment for a parent's separate statements)

---

## IAS 28 — Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures

**Scope:** Applies to investments in associates and investments in joint ventures. Does not apply to investments held by venture capital organizations, mutual funds, unit trusts, and similar entities that are measured at FVTPL under IFRS 9 (these entities may elect exemption). Also applies to how the equity method is applied in both consolidated and separate financial statements.

**Status and amendments:** Reissued May 2011; effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2013 (IAS 28.45; EU effective 1 January 2014; endorsed 11 December 2012, OJ 29 December 2012). Subsequently:
- *Sale or Contribution of Assets between an Investor and its Associate or Joint Venture* (September 2014 — amended IAS 28.28 and 30 and added **31A–31B**). Per **IAS 28.45C** these apply prospectively to sales or contributions occurring in annual periods beginning on or after **a date to be determined by the IASB** — i.e. **deferred indefinitely since December 2015**; earlier application is permitted with disclosure of that fact; **never EU-endorsed**, so the early-application option is not available to an entity reporting under EU-adopted IFRS
- *Equity Method in Separate Financial Statements* (August 2014, amending IAS 28.25; effective 1 January 2016)
- *Investment Entities: Applying the Consolidation Exception* (December 2014, amending IAS 28.17, 27 and 36 and adding **36A**; effective 1 January 2016)
- *Annual Improvements 2014–2016 Cycle* (December 2016, amending IAS 28.18 and 36A; effective 1 January 2018)
- ***Long-term Interests in Associates and Joint Ventures*** (October 2017, adding **IAS 28.14A** and deleting IAS 28.41; retrospective, effective 1 January 2019; EU-endorsed 8 February 2019, OJ 11 February 2019)
- IFRS 18 consequential amendment to IAS 28.10 (April 2024)
- ***Amendments to the Fair Value Option for Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures* — issued 26 June 2026.** These have **no fixed effective date of their own**: they **take effect when the entity first applies IFRS 18** — 1 January 2027 on IFRS 18's mandatory date, or earlier where IFRS 18 is adopted early. Early application is likewise tied to IFRS 18 adoption rather than free-standing. In substance this is an **IFRS 18 consequential amendment**, which is why its trigger is an event rather than a date. **Not EU-endorsed**, with EFRAG final endorsement advice issued 23 July 2026; ARC vote and Commission regulation outstanding. They post-date the 2026 annotated edition, whose IAS 28 effective-date paragraphs stop at 45L, so the final paragraph number is `[para-unconfirmed]` (the exposure draft proposed 45M)
- **A separate, far broader project — *Equity Method of Accounting—IAS 28 (revised 202x)* — was still in redeliberation at 28 August 2026; no amendments from it have been issued.** The revised IAS 28 would **re-order the standard by topic**, so existing paragraph cross-references in group manuals will break on adoption

**Printing warning.** The annual edition **prints the deferred 2014 *Sale or Contribution* text** in IAS 28.28, 30 and 31A–31B (and in IFRS 10.25(b), 26 and B99A), with the deferral carried only in the effective-date paragraphs. Anyone reading the standard text alone will conclude these requirements are in force. **They are not.**

**Core principle:** An investment in an associate or joint venture must be accounted for using the equity method, unless an exemption applies, because the investor has significant influence (or joint control) that creates a relationship closer than a simple financial asset.

**Key rules:**
- **Significant influence (IAS 28.5–6) — the presumption runs both ways.** Holding **20% or more** of the voting power presumes significant influence **unless clearly demonstrated otherwise**; holding **less than 20% presumes there is none unless significant influence can be clearly demonstrated** — a rebuttal burden, not merely "possible with evidence". A substantial or majority holding by another investor **does not preclude** significant influence. The upper bound is control, not 50% of votes. **IAS 28.6 evidence:** board representation; participation in policy-making **including decisions about dividends or other distributions**; material transactions; interchange of managerial personnel; provision of essential technical information.
- **Potential voting rights are treated differently from IFRS 10 (IAS 28.12–14).** The **interest** in an associate or joint venture is determined **solely on existing ownership interests** and does **not** reflect possible exercise or conversion (IAS 28.12) — unless IAS 28.13 applies, i.e. the entity has in substance an existing ownership because a transaction currently gives it access to the returns of an ownership interest, in which case eventual exercise is taken into account in determining the proportion allocated. **IAS 28.14:** instruments containing potential voting rights that in substance currently give access to ownership returns are **not** subject to IFRS 9; in all other cases they are. **So potential voting rights can create significant influence while not affecting the share of profit.**
- **Equity method mechanics:**
  - Initial recognition at cost
  - Subsequently: carrying amount adjusted for the investor's share of profit or loss (recognized in P&L) and other comprehensive income (recognized in OCI)
  - Distributions received reduce the carrying amount
  - **Upstream and downstream transactions (IAS 28.28–31, as printed — note the deferral).** **IAS 28.28** as printed applies the restriction to gains and losses from upstream and downstream transactions **involving assets that do not constitute a business, as defined in IFRS 3**, between the entity (including its consolidated subsidiaries) and its associate or joint venture: recognised only to the extent of unrelated investors' interests, with the entity's share eliminated. **The words "that do not constitute a business" were inserted by the deferred 2014 amendments** — an entity that has not early-applied them applies the restriction to *all* such transactions, businesses included. Upstream = sales from the associate to the investor; downstream = sales or contributions from the investor to the associate
  - **IAS 28.29 is not deferred and is often missed:** where a **downstream** transaction evidences a **reduction in net realisable value or an impairment** of the assets to be sold or contributed, the investor recognises those losses **in full**; where an **upstream** transaction gives such evidence, the investor recognises **its share** of those losses
  - **IAS 28.30 (as printed):** a gain or loss on contributing non-monetary assets that do not constitute a business in exchange for an equity interest is accounted for under IAS 28.28, **except where the contribution lacks commercial substance**, in which case the gain or loss is unrealised and not recognised unless IAS 28.31 applies. Such unrealised amounts are **eliminated against the equity-accounted investment** and must **not** be presented as deferred gains or losses in the statement of financial position
  - **IAS 28.31:** where the entity receives monetary or non-monetary assets **in addition to** the equity interest ("boot"), it recognises **in full in profit or loss** the portion of the gain or loss relating to the assets received
  - **IAS 28.31A and 31B (deferred, printed but not in force):** a gain or loss on a **downstream** transaction involving assets that **constitute a business** would be recognised **in full** (31A); and in determining whether the assets constitute a business, the entity would consider whether the transaction is part of multiple arrangements to be accounted for as a single transaction under IFRS 10.B97 (31B)
  - **Initial measurement and the acquisition difference (IAS 28.32):** equity-account from the date the investment becomes an associate or joint venture. Any difference between cost and the share of the net fair value of identifiable assets and liabilities: **goodwill is included in the carrying amount and is not amortised** (32(a)); any **excess of the share of net fair value over cost is income in the period of acquisition**, included in the share of profit or loss (32(b)). Post-acquisition, adjust the share of profit or loss for the fair value uplifts — additional depreciation on depreciable assets, and impairment of assets within the investee
  - **Group share and multi-tier structures (IAS 28.27):** the group's share aggregates the holdings of the parent and its **subsidiaries** only; holdings by the group's *other* associates or joint ventures are ignored. Where the associate itself has subsidiaries, associates or joint ventures, take the amounts recognised in the associate's own financial statements (including its share of its associates and joint ventures), adjusted for uniform accounting policies
- **Losses in excess of the interest — apply IAS 28.14A, 37, 38, 39 in that order.** The most common error in the whole standard is applying IAS 28.38 to the equity-accounted carrying amount alone.
  1. **Apply IFRS 9 first (IAS 28.14A).** IFRS 9 applies to other financial instruments in the investee to which the equity method is not applied, **including long-term interests that in substance form part of the net investment**. Apply IFRS 9 to those **before** applying IAS 28.38 and IAS 28.40–43. In applying IFRS 9, **do not** take account of any adjustments to the carrying amount of long-term interests arising from IAS 28 — so effective-interest income on a shareholder loan is computed on the **IFRS 9** carrying amount
  2. **Adjust for the investee's cumulative preference shares (IAS 28.37):** where held by parties other than the entity and classified as equity, compute the share of profit or loss after adjusting for those dividends, **whether or not declared**
  3. **Apply IAS 28.38.** The threshold is the **interest** — the equity-method carrying amount **together with any long-term interests that in substance form part of the net investment**. An item whose settlement is neither planned nor likely in the foreseeable future is in substance an extension of the investment. It **includes** preference shares and long-term receivables or loans; it **excludes** trade receivables, trade payables, and long-term receivables for which adequate collateral exists, such as secured loans. **Losses in excess of the investment in ordinary shares are applied to the other components in the *reverse* order of their seniority** — least senior absorbing first
  4. **Stop at nil and track (IAS 28.39):** beyond that, recognise additional losses and a liability **only** to the extent of legal or constructive obligations or payments made on behalf of the investee. On the investee returning to profit, resume recognising the share of profits **only after** the cumulative share of profits equals the share of losses not recognised. **IFRS 12.22(c) requires disclosure of unrecognised losses for the period and cumulatively**
  5. **Subsequent-period mechanics** (from the IASB's own worked example accompanying the 2017 amendments): if applying IFRS 9 first would drive an interest's carrying amount **negative**, reverse the portion of previously allocated IAS 28 losses attributed to that interest; where subsequent IFRS 9 measurement restores a positive carrying amount, allocate previously unrecognised losses against it **before** recognising any share of profits; on recovery, restore the interests **in order of seniority**
- **Goodwill:** Any goodwill relating to an associate or joint venture is included in the carrying amount and is not separately tested for impairment (but the entire carrying amount is tested under IAS 36)
- **Uniform accounting policies (IAS 28.35–36A):** the entity's financial statements use uniform accounting policies for like transactions in similar circumstances, and the associate's policies are adjusted to conform (IAS 28.36) — **except as IAS 28.36A allows**. **IAS 28.36A** permits an entity that is **not** itself an investment entity, holding an interest in an associate or joint venture that **is** an investment entity, to elect to **retain the investment entity's fair value measurement** of that investee's interests in its own subsidiaries. Elected **separately for each such investee**, at the later of initial recognition, the investee becoming an investment entity, and the investee first becoming a parent. This is the IAS 28 counterpart to IFRS 10.33 and is the **only** fair-value look-through relief available to a non-investment-entity investor.
- **Reporting dates (IAS 28.33–34):** use the most recent available financial statements; where the reporting dates differ, **the associate prepares statements as at the entity's reporting date unless impracticable**. Only then may a different date be used, adjusted for significant transactions and events in between, with a gap of **no more than three months**, and with period lengths and the gap consistent from period to period. Same structure as IFRS 10.B92–B93 — the three-month window is a fallback conditional on impracticability, not the default. **IFRS 12.22(b)** requires disclosure of the investee's date and the reason.
- **Three different things that are routinely conflated — an elective measurement basis, a scope exemption, and a held-for-sale rule:**
  - **(a) Election to measure at FVTPL (IAS 28.18–19).** An entity may elect, **separately for each associate or joint venture, at initial recognition**, to measure an investment held by, or held indirectly through, a **venture capital organisation, mutual fund, unit trust or similar entity** (including investment-linked insurance funds) at **FVTPL under IFRS 9**. IAS 28.19 extends the election to the **portion** of an investment held indirectly through such an entity, regardless of whether that entity itself has significant influence over the portion, with the equity method applied to the remaining portion. **IAS 27.11 forces the same treatment in separate financial statements.**
    - **What the June 2026 amendments change:** they clarify that "similar entities" **includes entities that have a main business activity of investing in particular types of assets, as set out in IFRS 18.49(a)**. Why it matters: IFRS 18 requires income and expenses from all **equity-accounted** investments to sit in the investing category, but permits income and expenses from associates and joint ventures held under the **IAS 28 fair value option** to sit in the **operating** category where the entity invests in those assets as a main business activity. Insurers were reading IAS 28.18 either narrowly (investment-linked funds only) or broadly (anything related to insurance contracts issued); tying eligibility to IFRS 18.49(a) resolves it. **Transition:** apply at the same time and on the same basis as IFRS 18 — and because the option is otherwise available only at initial recognition, **IFRS 18's transition provisions open a one-off election window on first applying IFRS 18** (IFRS 18.C7). Issued **without re-exposure**; three IASB members had opposed publishing the exposure draft. The IASB separately decided to explore an **unrestricted** fair value option as a future work-plan priority — this is a stop-gap
  - **(b) Exemption from applying the equity method at all (IAS 28.17).** Available where the entity is a parent exempt from consolidation under IFRS 10.4(a), **or** where all of the following hold: it is a wholly-owned subsidiary, or a partially-owned subsidiary whose other owners (including non-voting ones) have been informed and do not object; its debt or equity instruments are not publicly traded; it has not filed and is not filing statements with a securities commission for a public issue; and its ultimate or an intermediate parent produces publicly available IFRS financial statements in which subsidiaries are consolidated **or measured at FVTPL** under IFRS 10
  - **(c) Held for sale — IFRS 5, not IFRS 9 (IAS 28.20–21).** Apply **IFRS 5** to an investment, or portion of an investment, meeting the held-for-sale criteria; any retained portion not so classified continues to be equity-accounted until the disposal occurs. After disposal, the retained interest goes to IFRS 9 unless it remains an associate or joint venture. **IAS 28.21: if the held-for-sale criteria cease to be met, apply the equity method retrospectively from the date of the original classification and amend the intervening financial statements.** Otherwise the investment is a **non-current asset** (IAS 28.15)
- **Discontinuing the equity method (IAS 28.22–25):**
  - Becomes a subsidiary → IFRS 3 and IFRS 10 (22(a))
  - Retained interest is a financial asset → measure at fair value, which is its IFRS 9 initial-recognition fair value; recognise in profit or loss the difference between (fair value of the retained interest plus any proceeds) and the carrying amount at the date the equity method was discontinued (22(b))
  - **22(c) and 23:** account for all amounts previously in OCI **on the same basis as if the investee had directly disposed of the related assets or liabilities** — so cumulative exchange differences on a foreign operation of the associate are reclassified to profit or loss
  - **24:** an associate becoming a joint venture, or vice versa, continues under the equity method with **no remeasurement** of the retained interest
  - **25:** where the ownership interest is merely **reduced** but the investment remains an associate or joint venture, reclassify to profit or loss the **proportionate** part of previously recognised OCI relating to that reduction, to the extent it would recycle on disposal. Per the IFRS IC (July 2009), **IAS 28.25 applies to all reductions in ownership interest regardless of cause, including dilution from a share issue by the associate**
- **Impairment (IAS 28.40, 41A–41C, 42–43) — note the gate and the negatives:**
  - **IAS 28.40 sets the order:** apply the equity method **including recognising losses under IAS 28.38 first**, then apply IAS 28.41A–41C to determine whether there is objective evidence of impairment of the **net investment**. (IAS 28.41 was deleted by the 2017 long-term interests amendments.)
  - **IAS 28.41A is an incurred-loss trigger, not an expected-loss one:** impairment exists **if and only if** there is objective evidence of one or more **loss events after initial recognition** that have a reliably estimable impact on estimated future cash flows. **Losses expected from future events, however likely, are not recognised.** The listed loss events: significant financial difficulty of the investee; breach of contract such as default or delinquency; the entity granting a concession it would not otherwise consider because of the investee's financial difficulty; probable bankruptcy or financial reorganisation; disappearance of an active market for the net investment **because of the investee's financial difficulties**
  - **IAS 28.41B — two important negatives:** the disappearance of an active market **because the investee's instruments are no longer publicly traded is not evidence of impairment**; and a **credit rating downgrade or a decline in fair value is not of itself evidence of impairment**, though it may be when considered with other information
  - **IAS 28.41C** adds, for a net investment in equity instruments: significant adverse changes in the technological, market, economic or legal environment indicating the **cost** of the investment may not be recovered; and a **"significant or prolonged" decline in fair value below cost**. Per the IFRS IC (June 2005 and July 2009), **"significant" is evaluated against original cost at initial recognition and "prolonged" against the period the fair value has been below that original cost — a prior impairment does not reset the cost basis**
  - **IAS 28.42:** goodwill within the carrying amount is **not separately recognised** and so is **not** tested separately under IAS 36's goodwill requirements. The **entire carrying amount** is tested as a **single asset** under IAS 36 — recoverable amount (the higher of value in use and fair value less costs of disposal) against carrying amount. Any impairment loss is **not allocated to any asset, including goodwill**, and accordingly it **reverses** under IAS 36 to the extent the recoverable amount subsequently increases. Value in use may be estimated either as the share of the present value of the investee's estimated future cash flows including disposal proceeds, **or** as the present value of expected dividends plus ultimate disposal
  - **IAS 28.43:** assess recoverable amount for **each** associate or joint venture separately, unless it does not generate cash inflows largely independent of the entity's other assets


**The IFRS 10 / IAS 28 conflict — status at 28 August 2026.**
- **The conflict:** IFRS 10.25 and B97–B99 require full recognition of the gain or loss on losing control of a subsidiary, with remeasurement of the retained interest to fair value; IAS 28.28 restricts gains and losses on transactions with an associate to unrelated investors' interests. Selling a subsidiary **to** an associate or joint venture engages both.
- The September 2014 amendments would resolve it by requiring a **full** gain or loss where a **business** is transferred (IAS 28.31A) and a restricted gain where the assets are not a business (IAS 28.28, IFRS 10.B99A). **They are printed in the standards but their effective date is "a date to be determined by the IASB"; deferred since December 2015 and still deferred.**
- **The Equity Method project is now resolving it, but nothing is final.** The September 2024 exposure draft proposed recognising gains and losses **in full** on all upstream and downstream transactions, including loss-of-control transactions, and amending IFRS 10 to **remove** the deferred 2014 requirements. In October 2025 the IASB declined to confirm that and commissioned research into earnings-management concerns. **In May 2026 (the pivotal meeting) it decided unanimously to retain the inconsistency within scope, and tentatively decided (12 of 13) to** (i) introduce an **accounting policy choice** between **full** and **restricted** recognition of gains and losses on all transactions with associates, **except** transfers of **businesses**, which are recognised in full either way; (ii) **confirm the withdrawal** of the 2014 *Sale or Contribution* amendments; and (iii) **amend IFRS 10** so that an investor choosing restricted recognition also restricts the gain or loss on losing control of a subsidiary that is **not a business**. In June 2026 the choice was extended to separate financial statements, independently of the consolidated policy, and the ED's proposals not to remeasure previously held or retained interests while continuing the equity method were **withdrawn**. In July 2026 the disclosure package was settled — disclose the policy choice; a full-recognition investor discloses gains and losses on **downstream** transactions, a restricting investor on **both downstream and upstream**; expand the **IFRS 12.20** disclosure objective; and a narrow exemption only where the information can be attributed to an individual associate **and** the transactions are part of ordinary activities.
- **Net position for a preparer today:** apply IAS 28.28 as printed **without** the deferred carve-out unless the 2014 amendments have been early-applied (and disclose which); IFRS 10.B99A is not in force; and the eventual answer will be a **policy choice with a mandatory full-gain carve-out for business transfers** plus a matching IFRS 10 restriction for non-business subsidiaries. **No effective date has been set and the IASB has not sought permission to ballot.** A parallel, unresolved conflict exists between IFRS 11.B34–B35 and IFRS 10 for loss of control into a **joint operation** (IFRS IC, July 2016).

**Other tentative Equity Method decisions worth planning for** (all tentative at 28 August 2026): the **cost of an associate** would be the fair value of consideration transferred, including the fair value of any previously held interest and of contingent consideration, with deferred tax effects of fair value adjustments included in the carrying amount; on acquiring **additional interests while retaining significant influence**, add the additional share of the fair value of identifiable assets and liabilities including goodwill, recognise any bargain purchase gain in profit or loss, and make **no catch-up** of previously unrecognised losses where the carrying amount had reached nil, with a relief from fair-value measuring the additional share and disclosure of its use; on **partial disposals retaining significant influence**, reduce the carrying amount by the percentage disposed of and recognise a gain or loss in profit or loss; where the **share of profit or loss and the share of OCI are both losses** and together exceed the carrying amount, recognise the **share of profit or loss first**, then the share of OCI; and on **impairment**, replace the reference to the "cost" of the investment in IAS 28.41C with "carrying amount" and **remove** the "significant or prolonged decline" indicator.

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Fair value of investments in associates/joint ventures with quoted prices
- Summarized financial information for each material associate/joint venture (assets, liabilities, revenue, profit/loss, OCI, dividends received)
- Nature and extent of significant restrictions on ability to transfer funds
- Unrecognized losses for the period and cumulatively
- Share of contingent liabilities of associates
- Reasons if 20–50% holding does not give significant influence, or why <20% does

- Cumulative **unrecognised losses**, for the period and cumulatively (IFRS 12.22(c)); the investee's **reporting date and the reason** for a non-coterminous date (IFRS 12.22(b)); and **restrictions** on the investee transferring funds to the entity (IFRS 12.22(a))
- Where the **IAS 28.36A** investment-entity fair value retention election is used, that fact and the basis for it
- Where the deferred 2014 *Sale or Contribution* amendments have been **early-applied**, that fact (IAS 28.45C)
- Once the June 2026 amendments apply, the basis for concluding the entity has a **main business activity of investing in particular types of assets** under IFRS 18.49(a)

**Common pitfalls:**
- Continuing to recognize losses once the carrying amount reaches zero without legal/constructive obligations
- Failing to eliminate unrealized profits on intercompany transactions
- Using the associate's unadjusted financial statements when accounting policies differ
- Treating goodwill embedded in the equity-accounted carrying amount as separately testable
- Missing the impracticability threshold for aligning accounting policies

- Applying IAS 28.38 to the equity-accounted carrying amount alone. The threshold is the **interest**, including long-term interests forming part of the net investment; secured loans and trade balances are excluded from it
- Absorbing losses against long-term interests in order of seniority rather than **reverse** order (IAS 28.38)
- Applying IAS 28 before IFRS 9 to long-term interests, or adjusting effective-interest income on a shareholder loan for IAS 28 loss allocations — **IAS 28.14A prohibits both**
- Treating a decline in fair value or a rating downgrade as impairment evidence on its own (IAS 28.41B), or **resetting the cost basis after a prior impairment** when assessing "significant or prolonged" (IFRS IC, June 2005)
- Testing goodwill within an equity-accounted investment separately, or allocating an impairment loss to it — IAS 28.42 prohibits both, **and that is precisely why such impairments reverse**
- Presenting an eliminated unrealised gain on a contribution lacking commercial substance as a **deferred gain** in the statement of financial position; IAS 28.30 requires elimination **against the investment**
- Ignoring IAS 28.29 and eliminating a proportionate share of a **loss** that evidences impairment or an NRV reduction — downstream losses of that kind are recognised **in full**
- Ignoring IAS 28.31 where consideration includes cash or other assets alongside the equity interest; that portion of the gain is recognised in full
- Failing to reclassify the proportionate share of OCI on a **dilution** caused by the associate issuing shares — IAS 28.25 applies to **all** reductions in ownership interest (IFRS IC, July 2009)
- Treating potential voting rights as increasing the **share of profit**; IAS 28.12 determines the share on existing ownership interests only, unless IAS 28.13 applies
- Measuring a **held-for-sale** associate under IFRS 9 rather than **IFRS 5** (IAS 28.20), or failing to restate retrospectively when the criteria cease to be met (IAS 28.21)
- Treating the 2014 *Sale or Contribution* amendments as effective — they are printed but **deferred**, and are on course to be **withdrawn** rather than activated
- Reading IAS 28.18–19 as an entity-level policy. The election is made **separately for each associate or joint venture, at initial recognition**, with a one-off window on first applying IFRS 18
- Assuming the equity method is unavailable in separate financial statements — IAS 27.10(c) has permitted it since the August 2014 amendments

**Related standards:** IFRS 3 (initial measurement concepts; the definition of a business for IAS 28.28 and 31A), IFRS 5 (**held-for-sale associates are measured under IFRS 5, not IFRS 9** — IAS 28.20–21), **IFRS 9** (the fair value option at IAS 28.18–19, **and** the measurement of long-term interests, which is applied **before** IAS 28.38 and 40–43 under IAS 28.14A), IFRS 10 (the deferred sale-or-contribution conflict; the IFRS 10.33 counterpart to IAS 28.36A), IFRS 11 (joint ventures are equity-accounted; the unresolved IFRS 11.B34–B35 conflict), IFRS 12 (disclosures — IFRS 12.20–23), **IFRS 18** (the IFRS 18.49(a) main-business-activity link that drives the June 2026 fair value option amendments), IAS 27 (separate financial statements; IAS 27.10(c) and 11), IAS 36 (impairment of the whole carrying amount as a single asset), IAS 21 (translation of a foreign associate)

---

## IAS 29 — Financial Reporting in Hyperinflationary Economies

**Scope:** Applies when an entity's functional currency is the currency of a hyperinflationary economy. Applies to the financial statements (including consolidated financial statements) of any entity whose functional currency is hyperinflationary. There is no absolute rate of inflation that establishes hyperinflation; judgment is required based on qualitative and quantitative indicators.

**Status and amendments:** IAS 29 became operative for periods beginning on or after 1 January 1990 (IAS 29.41). The only amendment in force is from **IFRS 18** (April 2024), which amended paragraphs 8 and 25 and is applied when IFRS 18 is applied (IAS 29.42). **IFRIC 7 *Applying the Restatement Approach under IAS 29*** supplements the standard for the period in which an entity first identifies hyperinflation. An IASB **research pipeline project, *High Inflation: Scope of IAS 29***, remains open; **no exposure draft has been published**. From 1 January 2027 the November 2025 amendments to IAS 21 interact with IAS 29.34 — the corresponding-figures paragraph now cross-refers to **IAS 21.41A, 42(b), 43 and 47A**.

**Core principle:** Financial statements of an entity that reports in the currency of a hyperinflationary economy must be stated in terms of the measuring unit current at the balance sheet date — i.e., all amounts restated to the current purchasing power unit — and the gain or loss on the net monetary position recognized in profit or loss.

**Key rules:**
- **Indicators of hyperinflation** (not exhaustive; judgment required):
  - The general population prefers to keep wealth in non-monetary assets or stable foreign currency
  - Prices are quoted in a stable foreign currency
  - Credit transactions use prices that compensate for expected purchasing power loss
  - Interest rates, wages, and prices are linked to a price index
  - Cumulative three-year inflation rate approaches or exceeds 100%
- **Assessing hyperinflation — the June 2025 agenda decision (annotated at IAS 29.3(e)).** The Committee was asked whether all the paragraph 3 indicators must be considered (including once one has been met), whether indicators outside paragraph 3 must be considered, and whether a subsidiary and its parent must reach the same conclusion. The evidence showed **little if any diversity**: stakeholders do **not** conclude that an economy is hyperinflationary on the basis of **one indicator alone**; they **do** consider other relevant indicators; and they do **not** reach different conclusions at different levels of a group preparing financial statements on the same basis. Stakeholders **use judgement and may assign different weights** to the indicators. The Committee concluded the matter does not have widespread effect and added no project. **Practical reading: the three-year 100% cumulative inflation figure in IAS 29.3(e) is one indicator among five, not a trigger.**
- **Timing of first application (IAS 29.4):** it is preferable that all entities reporting in the currency of the same hyperinflationary economy apply the standard from the same date; but the standard applies to an entity **from the beginning of the reporting period in which it identifies the existence of hyperinflation**. **Restatement is therefore applied to the whole of that annual period, not from the date of identification.** IFRIC 7 governs the mechanics of that first period, including a **two-step sequence for opening deferred tax**: remeasure deferred tax under IAS 12 after restating the nominal carrying amounts of non-monetary items at the opening date using the measuring unit at that date, then restate the remeasured deferred tax for the change in the measuring unit from the opening date to the reporting date (IFRIC 7.4).
- **No supplementary presentation (IAS 29.7):** presenting the IAS 29 information as a **supplement to unrestated financial statements is not permitted**, and separate presentation of the pre-restatement financial statements is discouraged.
- **Restatement of historical cost financial statements:**
