  - **IAS 29.11–12:** statement of financial position amounts **not already expressed in the measuring unit current at the end of the reporting period** are restated by applying a general price index. **Monetary items are not restated** because they are already expressed in the current measuring unit; **index-linked assets and liabilities are adjusted in accordance with the agreement**, not by the general index
  - **IAS 29.26:** all items in the statement of comprehensive income are restated by applying the change in the general price index **from the dates the income and expenses were initially recorded**
  - **IAS 29.17 — where no general price index exists** for the periods over which PP&E must be restated, it may be necessary to use **an estimate based, for example, on movements in the exchange rate between the functional currency and a relatively stable foreign currency**
  - **Gain or loss on the net monetary position (IAS 29.9, 27–28).** An entity holding an **excess of monetary assets over monetary liabilities loses purchasing power**; an entity with an excess of monetary liabilities over monetary assets **gains** purchasing power, to the extent those items are not linked to a price level. **IAS 29.27: the amount is normally derived as the balancing figure** — the difference resulting from restating non-monetary assets, owners' equity and items in the statement of comprehensive income, and adjusting index-linked assets and liabilities. It may alternatively be **estimated** by applying the change in the general price index to the weighted average of net monetary items for the period. **IAS 29.9:** it is included in profit or loss and **separately disclosed**
  - Opening equity restated from transaction dates using general price index
  - All items in the income statement and cash flow statement restated to current measuring unit using the price index at the transaction date (or average rate as an approximation if inflation was roughly even)
- **Restatement of current cost financial statements:** Non-monetary items already at current cost are not further restated; other procedures same as above
- **Consolidation (IAS 29.35):** a subsidiary reporting in a hyperinflationary currency is **restated using the general price index of the country in whose currency it reports** before consolidation — even where the parent is not hyperinflationary — and a foreign such subsidiary's restated statements are then **translated at closing rates**. Subsidiaries not reporting in hyperinflationary currencies are dealt with under IAS 21.
- **The index (IAS 29.37):** the general price index used must **reflect changes in general purchasing power**, and it is **preferable that all entities reporting in the currency of the same economy use the same index**.
- **Corresponding figures (IAS 29.34):** comparatives, whether historical cost or current cost based, are **restated by applying a general price index** so that they are presented in the measuring unit current at the **end of the current reporting period**; earlier-period information is likewise expressed in the current measuring unit. **Where comparatives are presented in a different presentation currency, IAS 21.41A, 42(b), 43 and 47A apply** (from 1 January 2027).
- **When hyperinflation ceases (IAS 29.38):** cease applying IAS 29; the amounts expressed in the measuring unit **current at the end of the previous reporting period** become the basis for carrying amounts in subsequent financial statements — restated amounts become the **new deemed cost**. There is no reversion to unrestated historical cost.


**Which economies are hyperinflationary — the source, and its limits.** IAS 29 deliberately sets **no list and no bright line** (IAS 29.3). In practice the reference point used across IFRS reporting is the **International Practices Task Force (IPTF) of the SEC Regulations Committee of the Center for Audit Quality**, which periodically publishes a *Document for Discussion: Monitoring Inflation in Certain Countries*. **Two caveats bind:** the IPTF document is framed under **US GAAP ASC 830**, not IAS 29 — its "highly inflationary" categories are an **input to**, not a substitute for, the IAS 29.3 judgement; and the CAQ states expressly that the document does not represent formal views or conclusions of the IPTF, the CAQ or the AICPA and **is not authoritative**.

The most recent document retrievable at 28 August 2026 is dated **6 November 2025** (published January 2026). Its categorisation:
- **Category 1a — three-year cumulative inflation exceeding 100% in the most recent annual period:** Argentina, Burundi, Ethiopia, Ghana, Haiti, Iran, Lao P.D.R., Lebanon, Malawi, Myanmar, Nigeria, Sierra Leone, South Sudan, Sudan, Suriname, Türkiye, Venezuela, Zimbabwe (18 countries)
- **Category 1b — projected three-year cumulative inflation greater than 100% in the current year:** none
- **Category 2 — exceeded 100% in recent years but between 70% and 100% in the last calendar year:** **Egypt** (moved down from Category 1a)
- **Category 3 — exceeded 100% after a spike in a discrete period:** none
- **Category 4 — between 70% and 100% in the current year, or a 25%-or-more increase in inflation during the last calendar year, or a significant increase in projected inflation, or projected to exceed 100% next year:** Angola, Yemen

The document notes that additional countries may exceed 100% but are absent because IMF data is unavailable — it names **Afghanistan, Eritrea and Syria** — and that non-IMF-member countries are not covered at all. **This list changes and must be re-checked at every reporting date;** the IPTF publishes roughly semi-annually, latterly quarterly, with a publication lag of around two months.

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Fact that financial statements and prior period comparatives have been restated for changes in purchasing power
- Whether the financial statements are based on historical cost or current cost approach
- Identity and level of the price index at the balance sheet date and the movement during the period
- Gain or loss on net monetary position (usually in P&L or as a separate line)

- **IAS 29.39 requires:** (a) the fact that the financial statements **and the corresponding figures for previous periods** have been restated for changes in general purchasing power and are stated in the measuring unit current at the end of the reporting period; (b) whether the statements are based on a **historical cost** or a **current cost** approach; and (c) **the identity and level of the price index at the end of the reporting period and the movement in the index during the current *and the previous* reporting period**

**Common pitfalls:**
- Failing to identify hyperinflation early — waiting for the 100% threshold rather than using qualitative indicators
- Restating monetary items (which are already in current units)
- Using an exchange rate instead of a domestic price index for restatement
- Presenting prior period comparatives in original nominal terms rather than restated current-period units
- After cessation, reverting to historical costs at the original amounts instead of using the restated carrying amounts as the new deemed cost

- Treating the three-year 100% figure as a **trigger** rather than as one of five indicators requiring judgement (IAS 29.3; June 2025 agenda decision)
- Applying IAS 29 from the date hyperinflation is identified rather than from the **beginning of that reporting period** (IAS 29.4)
- Presenting restated figures as a **supplement** to unrestated statements (IAS 29.7 prohibits it)
- Computing the gain or loss on the net monetary position independently and then failing to reconcile it — IAS 29.27 derives it as the **balancing figure** from the restatement
- Restating **index-linked** assets and liabilities by the general index rather than in accordance with the agreement (IAS 29.12)
- Omitting the movement in the index for the **previous** reporting period from the IAS 29.39(c) disclosure
- Treating the IPTF list as **authoritative** for IFRS purposes — it is a US GAAP ASC 830 monitoring tool, expressly non-authoritative, and IAS 29.3 remains a judgement

**Related standards:** **IAS 21** (translation of hyperinflationary subsidiaries; and, from 2027, the IAS 21.41A/47A translation *into* a hyperinflationary presentation currency, which IAS 29.34 now cross-refers to), **IFRIC 7** (applying the restatement approach in the first period, including the two-step opening deferred tax sequence), IAS 12 (deferred tax on restated amounts), IFRS 13 (fair value), IAS 36 (impairment of restated assets), IFRS 18 (the net monetary position gain or loss goes to **operating** unless presented with the associated income and expenses — IFRS 18.B69), IAS 20 (government grants in hyperinflationary economies are outside IAS 20's scope)

---

## IAS 32 — Financial Instruments: Presentation

**Scope:** Applies to the classification of financial instruments as financial liabilities or equity, to the presentation of compound instruments, and to the offsetting of financial assets and liabilities. Does NOT address recognition, measurement, or hedge accounting (those are IFRS 9). Applies from the issuer's perspective.

**Status and amendments:** IAS 32 (as revised) was issued December 2003. As at 28 August 2026 the **most recent amendment is the conforming amendment made by IFRS 18** (issued April 2024), which amended paragraphs **34, 39–41 and AG29** and is applied when IFRS 18 is applied (IAS 32.97U).

**FICE — no standard and no amendments have been issued.** The *Financial Instruments with Characteristics of Equity* project is **active and in redeliberation**, with **Final Amendments expected 30 September 2027** (an IASB estimate, subject to change). The exposure draft was published **November 2023**, comments closing 29 March 2024. The eventual package is tagged against **IAS 32, IFRS 7, IFRS 18, IAS 1 and the Conceptual Framework** — so it reaches presentation and disclosure, not just classification. Tentative decisions taken so far — **present these as tentative, never as requirements**:
- **24 February 2026, derivatives on own equity and the fixed-for-fixed condition (13/13 on each):** clarify that the condition requires a **fixed amount of consideration and either a fixed number of own equity instruments or a fixed exchange ratio**; that where a group entity issues a derivative over another group entity's shares the condition may be met in the consolidated statements if the consideration is denominated in **either entity's functional currency**; rename "preservation adjustments" as **adjustments that compensate the future holders of the equity instruments** and "passage-of-time adjustments" as **adjustments that are solely a function of time**; **withdraw** the proposed requirement to fix present-value calculations at initial recognition for such adjustments; require **each individual adjustment to satisfy the fixed-for-fixed condition independently**, with failure of any one classifying the **entire derivative** as a financial asset or liability; and extend the requirements to **share-for-share exchanges**.
- **20 April 2026, contingent settlement provisions (13/13 on each):** clarify that IAS 32.25 applies to instruments that could require liability settlement **only** as a result of the occurrence or non-occurrence of an uncertain future event beyond the control of both parties; that dividends are recognised as expenses where shares are **"wholly classified"** as liabilities; describe **liquidation** as *a process that has the objective of permanently ceasing an entity's operations*; and require the **"not genuine"** assessment to evaluate **both the probability and the nature** of the contingent event, with a substantive business purpose rendering a term genuine **regardless of remote probability**. The IASB also decided to **remove the measurement of contingent-settlement liabilities from FICE entirely** and address it in the Amortised Cost Measurement project.
- **Still open:** obligations to purchase own equity instruments, including **written non-controlling-interest put options** — taken to the July 2026 ASAF meeting for views on alternative approaches.

**What a preparer should do now:** nothing changes today. But **IAS 32.23** (an obligation to purchase own equity — recognise a financial liability at the present value of the redemption amount, reclassified from equity, **even where the contract itself is an equity instrument**) and **IAS 32.25** remain the two areas of greatest FICE exposure. Entities with written NCI puts, contingently redeemable preference shares, or convertibles with anti-dilution or FX-denominated strikes should expect the classification outcome to move.

**Core principle:** The classification of a financial instrument as a financial liability or equity must be based on the substance of the contractual arrangement and the economic reality, not legal form. A financial instrument is equity only if the issuer has no contractual obligation to deliver cash or another financial asset, and settlement in the issuer's own equity instruments meets specific criteria.

**Key rules:**
- **Liability vs. equity classification — the fundamental test:**
  - Financial liability: instrument gives rise to a contractual obligation to deliver cash or another financial asset, OR to exchange financial instruments under potentially unfavourable conditions
  - Equity: residual interest after deducting all liabilities; NO contractual obligation to deliver cash or other financial assets
  - Preference shares: classified as liabilities if they carry mandatory dividend payments or mandatory redemption (regardless of being called "shares")
  - Perpetual debt: may be equity if there is genuinely no obligation to repay
- **Two separate exceptions, not one — and the reclassification rule is a third paragraph:**
  - **IAS 32.16A–16B — puttable instruments** classified as equity: the instrument entitles the holder to a **pro rata share of net assets on liquidation**; it is in the **most subordinate class**; all instruments in that class have identical features; there is **no other contractual obligation** to deliver cash or another financial asset; and the total expected cash flows are substantially based on profit or loss, the change in net assets, or the change in fair value
  - **IAS 32.16C–16D** — instruments (or components) imposing an obligation to deliver a **pro rata share of net assets *only on liquidation***
  - **IAS 32.16E** — **reclassification is required from the date** the instrument acquires or ceases to have all the features and conditions; **16F** deals with the consequential gain or loss. The reclassification exception is 16E, not 16A–16D
  - **In consolidated financial statements the exception does not travel up:** puttable instruments issued by a **subsidiary** and classified as equity in the subsidiary's own statements are **classified as financial liabilities in the consolidated financial statements** (IAS 32.AG29A; IFRS IC, November 2013)
- **Obligations to purchase own equity instruments (IAS 32.23):** a contract containing an obligation for the entity to purchase its own equity instruments for cash or another financial asset gives rise to a **financial liability at the present value of the redemption amount**, reclassified **from equity** — **even where the contract itself is an equity instrument**. This is the paragraph that catches **written put options over non-controlling interests**.
- **A restriction does not negate an obligation.** A restriction on the entity's ability to satisfy a contractual obligation — lack of access to foreign currency, or the need for regulatory approval to pay — **does not negate** the obligation. And a contractual obligation conditional on the counterparty exercising a right to redeem **is** a financial liability, because the entity does not have the unconditional right to avoid delivering cash. This is directly relevant in hyperinflationary and exchange-restricted jurisdictions and interacts with the IAS 21 *Lack of Exchangeability* amendments.
- **Contingent settlement provisions (IAS 32.25) — there are three exceptions, not one.** Where settlement depends on the occurrence or non-occurrence of uncertain future events beyond the control of both the issuer and the holder, the instrument is a **financial liability unless**:
  - **(a)** the part of the contingent settlement provision that could require settlement in cash or another financial asset is **not genuine**;
  - **(b)** the issuer can be required to settle **only in the event of liquidation of the issuer** — the exception most often overlooked for perpetual instruments; or
  - **(c)** the instrument has all the features and meets the conditions in IAS 32.16A–16B.
  Note what IAS 32.25 lists as examples of triggers beyond both parties' control: a change in a **stock market index, consumer price index, interest rate or taxation requirements**, or the issuer's **future revenues, net income or debt-to-equity ratio**. The debt-to-equity-ratio example is the one that catches covenant-linked redemption clauses.
- **Compound financial instruments (split accounting):**
  - Instruments with both liability and equity components (e.g., convertible bonds) must be split on initial recognition
  - Liability component = present value of contractual cash flows discounted at the market rate for a comparable non-convertible instrument
  - Equity component = residual (total proceeds minus liability component); no subsequent remeasurement of the equity component
  - Transaction costs allocated proportionately between liability and equity components
  - On conversion: derecognize the liability, reclassify equity component — no gain or loss
- **Treasury shares:** Own equity instruments reacquired must be deducted from equity; no gain or loss recognized on purchase, sale, issue, or cancellation of treasury shares; consideration paid/received is recognized directly in equity
- **Interest, dividends, gains and losses:**
  - Payments on instruments classified as financial liabilities: recognized in P&L (interest expense)
  - Payments on instruments classified as equity: distributions from equity, not expense
- **Offsetting financial assets and liabilities:**
  - Present net only when BOTH conditions are met:
    - Legally enforceable right to set off the amounts, AND
    - Intention to settle net OR realize the asset and settle the liability simultaneously
  - Master netting agreements alone do NOT meet the offsetting criteria unless both conditions are satisfied

**Disclosure requirements:**
- The nature and extent of risks arising from financial instruments (detailed disclosures required by IFRS 7, not IAS 32 itself)
- For compound instruments: the carrying amounts of the liability and equity components
- Terms and conditions of equity instruments

**Common pitfalls:**
- Classifying preference shares as equity merely because they are called "shares" — must assess contractual obligations
- Forgetting to split compound instruments at inception and not tracking the unamortized discount on the liability component
- Assuming a master netting agreement permits offsetting — it only does if real-time simultaneous settlement is intended or the legal right is currently enforceable in all circumstances
- Treating the equity component of a convertible bond as a liability when conversion is probable
- Recognizing gain or loss on repurchase of own equity instruments

- Treating IAS 32.16A–16D as one exception. They are **two** (puttable instruments; liquidation-only obligations), with **reclassification governed by 16E**
- Reading IAS 32.25 as having a single "remote / non-genuine" escape. There are **three**, and the **liquidation-only** exception is the one most often overlooked for perpetual instruments
- Concluding that an obligation is negated by **exchange controls or a regulatory approval requirement**. It is not
- Failing to identify a **written put over non-controlling interests** as an IAS 32.23 financial liability at the present value of the redemption amount, reclassified out of equity — and not tracking the FICE redeliberations, which will change how these are measured and presented
- Classifying puttable NCI instruments as equity in the **consolidated** financial statements because they are equity in the subsidiary's own statements (IAS 32.AG29A)
- Presenting FICE tentative decisions as requirements. **No FICE amendments have been issued**; final amendments are expected 30 September 2027 at the earliest

**Related standards:** IFRS 7 (disclosures — including the IFRS 7.19A–19B puttable-instrument disclosures added by IFRS 18, and the IFRS 7.13A–13F offsetting disclosures that reach beyond the IAS 32.42 offset test), IFRS 9 (recognition and measurement; the IAS 32.33A election feeds IFRS 7.8(a)(iii)), IFRS 2 (share-based payment — instruments to deliver own equity), IFRS 10 (IAS 32.AG29A — puttable NCI instruments in consolidated statements), IFRS 13 (the demand-feature floor at IFRS 13.47, which **IFRIC 19.7 disapplies** for an extinguished liability), **IFRIC 2** (members' shares in co-operative entities), **IFRIC 19** (extinguishing financial liabilities with equity instruments), IAS 33 (the classification of preference shares drives the EPS numerator)

---

## IAS 33 — Earnings Per Share

**Scope:** Applies to entities whose ordinary shares (or potential ordinary shares) are publicly traded, and to entities that are in the process of issuing such instruments. Other entities that disclose EPS voluntarily must comply with IAS 33 in full. Applies to individual and consolidated financial statements; where consolidated are presented, EPS is based on consolidated data.

**Status and amendments:** **IFRS 18** (issued 9 April 2024) amended IAS 33 paragraphs 4A, 13 and 67A–68A, **added paragraphs 73B–73C and deleted paragraphs 73–73A**, applied when IFRS 18 is applied — i.e. from **1 January 2027** (IAS 33.74F). The change **materially restricts which additional per-share measures may be disclosed**. Entities applying IFRS 19 before IFRS 18 continue to apply the old paragraphs 73 and 73A (IFRS 19.A3).

**Core principle:** EPS provides a measure of the return attributable to each ordinary share. Basic EPS reflects actual shares outstanding; diluted EPS reflects the maximum potential dilution from convertible instruments, options, and warrants, giving users a "worst-case" picture of per-share earnings.

**Key rules:**
- **Basic EPS:**
  - Numerator: profit or loss attributable to ordinary equity holders of the parent, after deducting preference dividends (including undeclared cumulative preference dividends) and other adjustments for non-ordinary instruments
  - Denominator: weighted average number of ordinary shares outstanding during the period
  - Bonus issues / stock splits: treated as if they occurred at the beginning of the earliest period presented (retrospective adjustment; no time-weighting)
  - Rights issues at below-market price: contains a bonus element; use a theoretical ex-rights price to restate prior periods
- **Diluted EPS:**
  - Numerator: adjusted for after-tax effect of dividends/interest on dilutive potential ordinary shares and other adjustments
  - Denominator: weighted average shares plus weighted average dilutive potential ordinary shares (options, warrants, convertibles, contingently issuable shares)
  - Options/warrants: treasury share method — only the incremental shares (proceeds assumed to buy back shares at average market price) are added; out-of-the-money options are anti-dilutive
  - Convertible instruments: "if converted" method — add back after-tax interest on convertibles; add all shares that would result from conversion from the beginning of the period (or issue date if later)
  - Anti-dilutive instruments: EXCLUDED from diluted EPS (must disclose their existence)
  - Order of dilution: instruments ranked from most dilutive to least dilutive (incremental EPS method); include in sequence until cumulative EPS is no longer declining
- **Contingently issuable shares:** Included in diluted EPS if conditions are currently satisfied; included in basic EPS only when all conditions are met and shares are actually issued
- **Restatement:** If share transactions occur after the reporting date but before financial statements are authorized, EPS is recalculated for all periods presented
- **Presentation (IAS 33.66–68A):** basic and diluted EPS are presented in the statement of comprehensive income, **with equal prominence**, for all periods presented, for profit or loss from continuing operations attributable to ordinary equity holders of the parent **and** for profit or loss attributable to those holders, and for each class of ordinary shares with a different right to share in profit (66). If diluted EPS is reported for one period it is reported for all; where basic and diluted are equal, dual presentation can be one line (67). Discontinued-operation amounts per share are either presented in the statement of comprehensive income or disclosed in the notes (68). **Where the entity uses the two-statement approach described in IFRS 18.12(b)**, the paragraph 66–67 amounts are presented in the **statement of profit or loss** (67A), and the discontinued-operation amounts are presented there or disclosed in the notes (68A).
- **Additional per-share amounts — narrowed from 2027 (IAS 33.73B–73C, replacing the deleted paragraphs 73–73A):**
  - An entity may disclose additional per-share amounts using a different numerator, **but the numerator must be** the amount attributable to ordinary equity holders of the parent of either (a) a **total or subtotal listed in IFRS 18 paragraphs 69, 86 or 118**, or (b) a **management-defined performance measure** as defined in IFRS 18.117. **Free choice of numerator is gone**
  - If such an amount is disclosed, the entity must disclose the additional **basic and diluted** amounts **with equal prominence**; use the IAS 33 weighted average number of ordinary shares; disclose it **in the notes only — it cannot be presented in the primary financial statements**; and, where the numerator is an MPM, give the **full IFRS 18.121–125 MPM disclosures** for that numerator (IAS 33.73C)

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Basic and diluted EPS on the face of the statement of comprehensive income (or income statement)
- Numerators used (reconciled to profit/loss) and denominators (shares)
- Instruments that could potentially dilute basic EPS in the future but were anti-dilutive in the current period
- Description of transactions after the reporting date that would have changed EPS materially

**Common pitfalls:**
- Including anti-dilutive instruments in diluted EPS calculations (this increases EPS, which is not "dilution")
- Failing to adjust the prior period comparatives for bonus issues or share splits
- Incorrect treatment of the bonus element in a rights issue (using full proceeds rather than theoretical ex-rights price)
- Omitting the adjustment for cumulative preference dividends even when not declared
- Using end-of-period shares rather than the weighted average for basic EPS

- Presenting an "adjusted EPS" on the face of the statement of profit or loss from 2027. IAS 33.73C(c) confines additional per-share amounts **to the notes**
- Using a bespoke numerator for an additional per-share amount. From 2027 it must be an IFRS 18 paragraph 69, 86 or 118 total or subtotal, **or an MPM** — and if it is an MPM, the full MPM note requirements including the reconciliation and per-item tax and NCI effects apply
- Disclosing an additional **basic** amount without the corresponding **diluted** amount (IAS 33.73C(a))

**Related standards:** IFRS 5 (discontinued operations — EPS must show the contribution), IAS 32 (classification of preference shares drives the EPS numerator), IFRS 2 (dilution from options), **IFRS 18** (paragraphs 69, 86, 117, 118 and 121–125 — the permitted numerators and the MPM regime; the two-statement approach at IFRS 18.12(b)), **IFRS 19** (IAS 33 disclosures are **not** reduced for entities applying IFRS 19 — IFRS 19.4(b))

---

## IAS 34 — Interim Financial Reporting

**Scope:** Applies when an entity is required or elects to publish interim financial reports. Does not mandate which entities must present interim reports or how frequently — that is determined by securities regulators or other authorities. IAS 34 sets the minimum content and recognition/measurement principles for such reports.

**Status and amendments:** **IFRS 18** (issued 9 April 2024) amended IAS 34 paragraphs 1–5, 7–10, 11A–12, 19 and its heading, 20 and 24, and **added paragraph 16A(m)**; applied when IFRS 18 is applied, from **1 January 2027**. In interim financial statements in the **first year** of applying IFRS 18, an entity applies **IFRS 18 paragraphs C4–C6** (IAS 34.61). Earlier: *Definition of Material* amended IAS 34.24 (effective 1 January 2020) and *Disclosure of Accounting Policies* amended IAS 34.5 (effective 1 January 2023).

**Core principle:** Interim financial reports must provide an update on the most recent annual financial statements, focusing on new activities, events, and circumstances. The same recognition and measurement principles applied in annual financial statements must be applied in interim periods.

**Key rules:**
- **Minimum content (condensed interim financial report):**
  - Condensed statement of financial position
  - Condensed statement of comprehensive income (or separate income statement)
  - Condensed statement of changes in equity
  - Condensed statement of cash flows
  - Selected explanatory notes
  - If a complete set is presented, it must conform to all IFRS requirements
  - **IAS 34.10:** condensed statements must include, at a minimum, **each of the headings and subtotals included in the most recent annual financial statements**, plus the selected explanatory notes. In preparing them the entity also applies **IFRS 18 paragraphs 41–45** (aggregation, disaggregation, labelling, offsetting) and **IAS 8 paragraphs 6A–6N** (basis of preparation). Additional line items or notes are included if their omission would make the condensed statements misleading
  - **IAS 34.9:** if a **complete** set of financial statements is published in the interim report, its form and content must conform to IFRS 18's requirements for a complete set. Publishing a complete set, or exceeding the minimum condensed content, is expressly permitted (IAS 34.7)
- **Comparative periods:**
  - Balance sheet: current period-end vs. most recent annual year-end
  - Income statement: current interim period and YTD vs. comparable periods of preceding year
  - Cash flow: YTD vs. comparable YTD of preceding year
  - Changes in equity: YTD vs. comparable YTD of preceding year
- **First-year IFRS 18 interim requirements (IFRS 18.C4–C6, applied via IAS 34.61):**
  - **C4 overrides IAS 34.10 for the first year:** the entity must present in its condensed interim statements **every heading it expects to use** under IFRS 18 and **the subtotals required by IFRS 18.69–74** — not merely the headings from the last annual statements. It does not revert to the IAS 34.10 rule until it has issued its first IFRS 18 **annual** financial statements
  - **C5:** as part of the IAS 34.16A(a) information, disclose reconciliations for **each line item** in the statement of profit or loss for **the comparative interim period *and* the comparative cumulative year-to-date period**, between the restated IFRS 18 amounts and the amounts previously presented under IAS 1. IFRS 19 entities give this under IFRS 19.246(a)
  - **C6:** the C3 and C5 reconciliations may optionally also be given for the current period or earlier comparatives
- **Discrete vs. integral approach:**
  - IAS 34 requires the discrete approach: each interim period is treated as a discrete accounting period
  - Revenues are recognized when earned; costs are accrued when incurred — NOT deferred/anticipated solely because of the annual accounting period
- **Taxes:** Use the estimated annual effective tax rate to determine the tax expense for the interim period (exception to strict discrete approach — anticipation of full-year tax is required)
- **Year-end bonuses, seasonal revenues:** Recognized on the same basis as annual (do not smooth or defer)
- **Materiality (IAS 34.23–24):** assessed in relation to the **interim period** financial data, recognising that interim measurements may rely on estimates to a greater extent than annual measurements. IFRS 18 defines material information and requires separate disclosure of material items; **neither IFRS 18 nor IAS 8 contains quantified materiality guidance** (IAS 34.24).
- **Changes in accounting policy:** Applied retrospectively; restate all prior period interim data presented
- **Estimates:** If an estimate made in an interim period is revised in a subsequent interim period in the same year, recognize in the period of change; do NOT restate prior interim periods (prospective treatment within the year)
- **Fourth quarter:** No separate fourth-quarter report required, but significant events between the third quarter and year-end must be disclosed in the annual financial statements

**Disclosure requirements:**
- Significant events and transactions (including seasonality/cyclicality, unusual items, changes in estimates, dividends, segment information, acquisitions/disposals, changes in accounting policy)
- Statement that the same accounting policies as the most recent annual report have been applied (or description of any changes)
- Comparative figures as required
- Explanation of seasonality or cyclicality of operations

- **MPM disclosures in interims (IAS 34.16A(m), new from 2027):** the disclosures about management-defined performance measures required by **IFRS 18.121–125**. Note the interaction with IFRS 18.B120 — a subtotal used in interim public communications but not annual ones is an MPM **only in the interim financial statements**
- Disaggregation of revenue from contracts with customers required by IFRS 15.114–115 (IAS 34.16A(l))
- For entities becoming or ceasing to be investment entities, the IFRS 12.9B disclosures (IAS 34.16A(k))
- Fair value disclosures for financial instruments per IFRS 13.91–93(h), 94–96, 98 and 99 and IFRS 7.25, 26 and 28–30 (IAS 34.16A(j))

**Common pitfalls:**
- Deferring costs in early interim periods that are expected to benefit later periods (not permitted — costs expensed when incurred)
- Anticipating revenues not yet earned at interim date to smooth annual results
- Using year-to-date materiality rather than interim period materiality
- Restating prior interim periods for changes in estimates (should be prospective within the year)
- Omitting segment disclosures required by IFRS 8 in condensed interim reports

- In the first IFRS 18 year, carrying forward the prior annual headings into the interim statements. **IFRS 18.C4 requires every heading the entity expects to use plus the paragraphs 69–74 subtotals**, overriding IAS 34.10
- Omitting the C5 interim reconciliations, or giving them only for the year-to-date comparative and not the discrete interim comparative — **C5 requires both**
- Missing IAS 34.16A(m). MPM disclosures are required in interim reports from 2027, and the population of MPMs can legitimately **differ** between interim and annual statements (IFRS 18.B120)
- Applying only IAS 34 when preparing condensed statements. IAS 34.10 also pulls in **IFRS 18.41–45** and **IAS 8.6A–6N**

**Related standards:** IFRS 8 (segment disclosures required in condensed interims), IAS 12 (the annual effective tax rate method), IAS 36 with **IFRIC 10** (a goodwill impairment recognised in a previous interim period **cannot** be reversed), **IFRS 18** (the first-year interim requirements at C4–C6; MPM disclosures via IAS 34.16A(m); aggregation at IFRS 18.41–45), **IAS 8** (paragraphs 6A–6N apply to condensed interims via IAS 8.3A), IFRS 15 (revenue disaggregation), IFRS 13 and IFRS 7 (interim fair value disclosures), IFRS 12 (investment entity status changes), **IFRIC 21** (a levy triggered at a point in time hits a single interim period in full)

---

## IAS 36 — Impairment of Assets

**Scope:** Applies to impairment testing of most non-financial assets, including PP&E, right-of-use assets, investment property carried at cost, intangible assets, goodwill, investments in subsidiaries/associates/JVs, and assets under construction. Key exclusions: financial assets (IFRS 9), investment property at fair value (IAS 40), biological assets at fair value (IAS 41), deferred tax assets (IAS 12), employee benefit plan assets (IAS 19), and most non-current assets held for sale (IFRS 5).

**Status and amendments:** Issued by the IASC June 1998, adopted by the IASB April 2001, revised March 2004 (first phase of the business combinations project) and amended January 2008 (second phase). *Recoverable Amount Disclosures for Non-Financial Assets* (Amendments to IAS 36) was issued May 2013, effective 1 January 2014; EU-endorsed 19 December 2013, OJ 20 December 2013.

**Two live developments at 28 August 2026:**
- ***Business Combinations — Disclosures, Goodwill and Impairment*.** Exposure Draft published **March 2024** (comments closed 15 July 2024). **No final amendments have been issued** — the IASB was still redeliberating at its 21 July 2026 meeting and the next work-plan milestone is "Decide Project Direction". **Do not apply the proposals.** The tentative decisions that would change IAS 36 if finalised: **remove** the IAS 36.33(b)/44 requirement to exclude cash flows from **uncommitted future restructurings and asset enhancements** from value in use (retained 21 April 2026, **9 of 13** — the narrowest margin in the package); **remove** the requirement to calculate value in use on a **pre-tax** basis (unanimous, 13 of 13), with a new disclosure of whether the discount rate used is pre-tax or post-tax; **clarify that the operating-segment ceiling in IAS 36.80(b) is not a default** allocation level, to reduce the "shielding" of goodwill by headroom in the acquirer's pre-existing business; and require disclosure of **which reportable segment** contains a CGU carrying goodwill. **The IASB explicitly declined to reintroduce amortisation of goodwill.** That the text of IAS 36.33(b), 44, 45, 55, 80 and 124 is unchanged in the 2026 edition independently corroborates that nothing has been issued.
- ***Disclosures about Uncertainties in the Financial Statements*** — issued **November 2025**. These are **illustrative examples added to the guidance accompanying** IFRS 7, IFRS 18, IAS 1, IAS 8, **IAS 36** and **IAS 37**. They are **non-mandatory accompanying material, they change no requirement, and they have no effective date**; they illustrate how existing disclosure requirements apply when reporting the effects of uncertainties, using climate-related fact patterns, and were developed jointly with the ISSB so that they interact cleanly with ISSB sustainability disclosures. Treat them as application guidance a regulator will expect you to have considered — not as a new requirement.

**Core principle:** An asset must not be carried at more than its recoverable amount. If the carrying amount exceeds the recoverable amount, an impairment loss must be recognized immediately. Recoverable amount is the higher of fair value less costs of disposal (FVLCOD) and value in use (VIU).

**Key rules:**
- **Indications of impairment** (trigger review required at each reporting date):
  - External: significant decline in market value, adverse changes in technology/market/economy/law, increase in market interest rates, market capitalization below net assets
  - Internal: evidence of obsolescence, physical damage, significant changes in use or performance below expectation, worse-than-expected economic performance
  - Goodwill, indefinite-life intangibles, and intangibles not yet available for use: tested annually regardless of indicators
- **Recoverable amount — you do not always need both measures.** If *either* fair value less costs of disposal or value in use exceeds the carrying amount, the asset is not impaired and the other need not be estimated (IAS 36.19).
- **FVLCOD versus VIU — what each measure may and may not contain. This is the most common technical error in the standard.**

  | Input | Fair value less costs of disposal | Value in use |
  |---|---|---|
  | Perspective | **Market participant** (IFRS 13); entity-specific advantages excluded (IAS 36.53A) | **Entity-specific**; reflects factors specific to the entity and not available to others (IAS 36.53A) |
  | Cash flows from a **future restructuring the entity is not yet committed to** | Included if a market participant would reflect them | **Excluded** (IAS 36.33(b), 44(a), 45(a)) — including the related cost savings |
  | Cash flows from **improving or enhancing** the asset's performance (enhancement capex and the inflows it generates) | Included if market participants would reflect them | **Excluded** (IAS 36.33(b), 44(b), 45(b)) — the asset is valued **in its current condition** (IAS 36.44) |
  | Financing cash flows | Excluded — IFRS 13 measures the asset, not its funding | **Excluded** (IAS 36.50(a)) |
  | Income tax | Not addressed by IAS 36 for FVLCOD — **IFRS 13 governs. Do not assume "tax is excluded"** | **Excluded** (IAS 36.50(b)) — hence the discount rate must also be pre-tax |
  | **Transaction** taxes | **Deducted** as costs of disposal — stamp duty and similar transaction taxes are named in IAS 36.28 | Not applicable |
  | Discount rate | The market-participant rate implicit in the fair value | **Pre-tax** rate reflecting current market assessments of the time value of money and the risks specific to the asset for which the cash flow estimates have not already been adjusted (IAS 36.55) |
  | Costs of disposal | Deducted: legal costs, **stamp duty and similar transaction taxes**, costs of removing the asset, direct incremental costs to bring the asset into condition for sale. **Not** deducted: termination benefits (IAS 19) and costs of reducing or reorganising the business following the disposal (IAS 36.28) | Not applicable |

  - "Committed to a restructuring" is determined by reference to **IAS 37** (IAS 36.46) — the IAS 37 constructive-obligation test governs whether restructuring cash flows may enter value in use
  - **Consistency (IAS 36.51):** because the discount rate is determined pre-tax, the cash flows must also be estimated pre-tax; and because the time value of money is captured by discounting, financing cash flows are excluded. **IAS 36.A20** states expressly that where the basis used to estimate the discount rate is post-tax, **that basis is adjusted to reflect a pre-tax rate** — "gross up the WACC by dividing by (1 − t)" is not the requirement; the adjustment must be derived so that discounting pre-tax cash flows reproduces the correct value
  - **The practical consequence of the asymmetry: an asset can have a higher FVLCOD than VIU purely because of the VIU restrictions**, even on identical underlying business plans. Where an entity has an approved enhancement or restructuring programme, running only a value-in-use model on the board-approved plan **overstates VIU**, and it is one of the most common audit findings in IAS 36
- **Value in use — the cash flow projections (IAS 36.33):** based on reasonable and supportable assumptions, using the most recent approved budgets or forecasts covering a **maximum of five years, unless a longer period can be justified** (33(b)); beyond that, extrapolate using a steady or declining growth rate that **must not exceed the long-term average growth rate** for the products, industries, or country or countries in which the entity operates, or for the market in which the asset is used, **unless a higher rate can be justified** (33(c)). **Both limits carry an express justification carve-out** — stating them as absolutes distorts the models of preparers with genuinely longer investment cycles in infrastructure, pharmaceuticals and mining. Terminal values using a Gordon Growth Model or an exit multiple are common, but the growth rate cap applies.
- **Carry-forward relief (IAS 36.99):** the most recent detailed recoverable-amount calculation for a CGU to which goodwill has been allocated may be **reused** in the current period's annual test if (a) the assets and liabilities making up the unit have not changed significantly, (b) the most recent calculation exceeded the carrying amount by a **substantial margin**, and (c) based on events and circumstances since, the likelihood of a current shortfall is **remote**. The IASB cited this in the March 2024 Exposure Draft as an existing cost relief.
- **Cash-generating units (CGUs):**
  - The smallest identifiable group of assets generating cash **inflows** that are largely independent of the cash inflows from other assets or groups of assets. Identification is a matter of **judgement about where cash *inflows* are largely independent** — it is driven by inflows, **not** by cost structure, shared management, or where costs are pooled (IAS 36.68)
  - An asset is tested at CGU level if its individual recoverable amount cannot be determined; the entity identifies the **lowest aggregation** of assets generating largely independent inflows
  - **Contractual or regulatory constraints matter:** where an entity cannot curtail one loss-making route or site because a contract requires the whole network, **the CGU is the network, not the route** (IAS 36.68, the bus-route example)
  - Cash inflows means inflows **from parties external to the entity**; in judging independence the entity considers how management **monitors** operations (by product line, business, location, district, region) and how it decides whether to continue or dispose of operations (IAS 36.69)
  - **Internal use does not defeat CGU status: if an active market exists for the output, that asset or group *shall* be identified as a CGU even if some or all of the output is used internally (IAS 36.70).** Where inflows are affected by **internal transfer pricing**, the entity must use **management's best estimate of arm's length prices** — both for the inflows of the supplying CGU and for the outflows of every receiving CGU affected. Testing an internally-supplying unit at transfer price rather than market price is a common and material error
  - CGU boundaries must be **consistent period to period** unless a change is justified
  - A CGU must **not be larger than an operating segment as defined in IFRS 8.5, before any aggregation** under IFRS 8.12
- **Goodwill allocation:**
  - **IAS 36.80:** goodwill is allocated to the CGUs or **groups of CGUs expected to benefit from the synergies** of the combination — **irrespective of whether any other assets or liabilities of the acquiree are assigned to those units**. Goodwill therefore routinely lands in units that acquired nothing
  - **The unit or group must simultaneously satisfy both constraints (IAS 36.80(a)–(b)):** (a) it is the **lowest level at which goodwill is monitored for internal management purposes**; and (b) it is **not larger than an operating segment as defined by IFRS 8.5, before aggregation**. Note that the cross-reference is to the IFRS 8 *definition* of an operating segment, i.e. **before** the aggregation criteria are applied — so the reportable segments in the segment note are usually **too large** to be the goodwill-testing level. In the March 2024 Exposure Draft the IASB proposed to clarify that this ceiling is **not a default**
  - If the initial accounting for a business combination is incomplete at the reporting date, IFRS 3's measurement-period rules govern and the goodwill allocation is completed **before the end of the first annual period beginning after the acquisition date** (IAS 36.85)
  - **Ordering of tests (IAS 36.97–98) — bottom-up.** Individual assets with impairment indicators are tested first, then CGUs within a group, then the group of CGUs carrying the goodwill. Doing it the other way absorbs an asset-level loss into headroom and understates the charge
  - **Timing (IAS 36.96):** the annual test may be at any time in the annual period provided it is the **same time each year**, and different CGUs may be tested at different times. **But if goodwill was allocated to a unit from a combination in the current annual period, that unit must be tested before the end of that period.** The same rule applies to intangibles (IAS 36.10(a)): indefinite-life intangibles and intangibles not yet available for use are tested annually at a consistent date, but one **recognised during the current period must be tested before the end of that period**
  - Tested by comparing the carrying amount of the CGU (including goodwill) with its recoverable amount
  - Impairment loss on a CGU: first reduces goodwill to zero, then allocated pro-rata to other assets (but not below the highest of FVLCOD, VIU, or zero)
- **Corporate assets (IAS 36.100):** the defining characteristics are that they do **not** generate independent cash inflows **and** their carrying amount cannot be fully attributed to the CGU under review — head office buildings, EDP equipment, research centres. Allocate them to CGUs on a reasonable and consistent basis; where they cannot be fully allocated, test the **smallest group of CGUs** that includes the corporate asset. Whether an asset is a corporate asset depends on the entity's structure, so the same asset type can be a corporate asset in one group and a CGU asset in another.
- **Recognition of impairment loss:**
  - Single asset: recognized in P&L (or in OCI to the extent of a previous revaluation surplus for revalued assets under IAS 16/IAS 38)
  - CGU: reduce goodwill first, then other assets pro-rata
- **Reversal of impairment:**
  - Assessed at **each reporting date** by looking for indications that a prior impairment loss no longer exists or has decreased (IAS 36.110–111) — a mirror of the impairment-indicator review
  - **Ceiling (IAS 36.117):** the increased carrying amount must not exceed the carrying amount, net of depreciation or amortisation, that **would have been determined had no impairment loss been recognised** in prior periods
  - For a CGU, the reversal is allocated **pro rata to the assets of the unit other than goodwill**, subject to the same asset-level ceiling (IAS 36.122–123)
  - **Goodwill impairment shall not be reversed in a subsequent period (IAS 36.124).** The stated reason (IAS 36.125) is that any recovery in goodwill value after impairment is likely to be **internally generated** goodwill, whose recognition IAS 38 prohibits. The asymmetry is deliberate, not an oversight, and it is why CGU-level impairments are so sticky
  - Note the contrast with an **equity-accounted investment**: because IAS 28.42 does not allocate the impairment to goodwill within the carrying amount, **that impairment does reverse** under IAS 36

**Disclosure requirements:**
- For each class of assets: impairment losses and reversals recognized, their location in the income statement
- For material impairment/reversals: description of the asset/CGU, events leading to the impairment, amount, FVLCOD or VIU approach used
- For CGUs with significant goodwill or indefinite-life intangibles: carrying amount, description of key assumptions, approach to determining values (growth rates, discount rate), sensitivity analysis if reasonable change in assumption would cause further impairment

- **IAS 36.130 drives the per-event disclosure** for individual assets and CGUs; **IAS 36.134 drives the annual CGU-level disclosure** for every unit or group of units whose allocated goodwill or indefinite-life intangibles are **significant in comparison with the entity's total** goodwill or indefinite-life intangibles — carrying amounts, the basis of recoverable amount, key assumptions, the period covered by budgets and forecasts, the growth rate used to extrapolate beyond it, and the discount rate. The IAS 36.134 **sensitivity disclosure** is required where a reasonably possible change in a key assumption would cause the carrying amount to exceed the recoverable amount; **regulators treat a boilerplate "no reasonably possible change" statement as a finding**

**Common pitfalls:**
- Using post-tax cash flows with a post-tax discount rate inconsistently (IAS 36 requires pre-tax; the pre-tax and post-tax approaches should give the same answer only if applied correctly)
- Using a growth rate in the terminal value that exceeds the long-term average
- Failing to test goodwill annually and only testing on indicators
- Reversing goodwill impairment losses (never permitted)
- Allocating impairment losses below an asset's FVLCOD or VIU
- Not splitting a CGU when operations or monitoring has changed

- Building **enhancement capex or an uncommitted restructuring into a value in use model** because it is in the board-approved budget (IAS 36.33(b), 44). The budget is the *starting point*, not the permitted content
- Applying market-participant assumptions to value in use, or entity-specific synergies to fair value less costs of disposal — collapsing the IAS 36.53A distinction
- **Adding back stamp duty and similar transaction taxes in FVLCOD.** IAS 36.28 names them among the costs of disposal, so they are **deducted**; what IAS 36 excludes is **income tax**, and it says so only for **value in use** (IAS 36.50(b))
- Deducting termination benefits or post-disposal reorganisation costs as "costs of disposal" (IAS 36.28 excludes them)
- Treating the reportable segments in the IFRS 8 note as the goodwill-testing level; the ceiling is an **operating segment before aggregation** (IAS 36.80(b))
- Testing the goodwill-carrying group **before** testing the individual assets and units inside it (IAS 36.97–98)
- Testing an internally-supplying CGU at **transfer price** rather than management's best estimate of arm's length prices (IAS 36.70)
- Stating the five-year and long-term-growth-rate limits as **absolutes**; IAS 36.33(b)–(c) each carry an express "unless it can be justified" carve-out
- Failing to test a newly acquired CGU before the end of the acquisition year (IAS 36.96), or a newly recognised indefinite-life intangible before the end of its first year (IAS 36.10(a))
- Applying the March 2024 Exposure Draft proposals — post-tax value in use, enhancement cash flows — as if final. **They are not; no amendment has been issued.**

**Related standards:** IFRS 3 (goodwill recognition; the measurement period governs an incomplete allocation), IFRS 5 (held for sale — **IFRS 5.23 cross-refers to IAS 36.104 and 122 but not to 105**, so the individual-asset floor does not apply to a disposal-group allocation), IFRS 8 (the **operating** segment ceiling, before aggregation), IFRS 6 (the parallel E&E ceiling at IFRS 6.21, and the IFRS 6.20 indicator substitution), IFRS 13 (fair value measurement for FVLCOD), IAS 16 and IAS 38 (revaluation surplus; and IAS 38's prohibition on internally generated goodwill, which is why IAS 36.124 exists), **IAS 28.40–43** (the whole equity-accounted carrying amount is tested as a single asset — and that impairment **does** reverse), **IAS 36.12(h)** (the two-limb dividend indicator for investments in separate financial statements), IFRIC 1 (an upward decommissioning adjustment is an impairment indicator), **IFRIC 10** (a goodwill impairment recognised in an interim period cannot be reversed), IAS 37 (the "committed to a restructuring" test)

---

## IAS 37 — Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets

**Scope:** Applies to provisions (liabilities of uncertain timing or amount), contingent liabilities, and contingent assets. Key exclusions: financial instruments at fair value (IFRS 9), executory contracts (unless onerous), insurance contracts (IFRS 17), items covered by other standards (e.g., income taxes — IAS 12, leases — IFRS 16, employee benefits — IAS 19, construction contracts — IFRS 15).

**Status and amendments:** Issued by the IASC September 1998, adopted by the IASB April 2001.
- ***Onerous Contracts — Cost of Fulfilling a Contract*** — issued **14 May 2020**; effective for annual periods beginning on or after **1 January 2022**; earlier application permitted (IAS 37.105). Added **IAS 37.68A and 94A** and amended 69. EU-endorsed 28 June 2021, OJ 2 July 2021. Approved by all 14 IASB members.
- ***Disclosures about Uncertainties in the Financial Statements*** — issued **November 2025**. Non-mandatory **illustrative examples** added to the guidance accompanying IFRS 7, IFRS 18, IAS 1, IAS 8, IAS 36 and **IAS 37**. **No effective date, no change to any requirement**; they illustrate the application of existing disclosure requirements to uncertainties, using climate-related fact patterns, and were developed with the ISSB.

**Open project — *Provisions: Targeted Improvements* (ED/2024/8).** Exposure Draft issued **November 2024**, comments closed 12 March 2025. **No amendments have been issued as at 28 August 2026**; the IASB discussed a completion plan on 22 June 2026 without taking decisions. Three targeted changes are proposed:
1. **The present obligation recognition criterion**, realigned to the 2018 *Conceptual Framework* definition of a liability. Would accrue some costs — typically levies — **earlier and progressively** where payment depends on two separate actions or on activity exceeding a threshold.
2. **The costs included in measuring a provision**, specified as the costs that **relate directly to the obligation**: incremental costs **plus an allocation of other directly related costs**. This deliberately imports the IAS 37.68A onerous-contract cost basis into **general** provision measurement.
3. **The discount rate**, to reduce diversity between entities using risk-free rates and those using rates reflecting own credit or other risks, and to require disclosure of the rate.

If finalised as proposed, **IFRIC 21 *Levies* and IFRIC 6 would be withdrawn** and replaced by illustrative examples in the Guidance on implementing IAS 37. In early 2026 the IASB tentatively decided (12 of 13) to omit proposed paragraph 14Q. **Nothing here is yet effective, and IFRIC 21 remains in force.**

**Core principle:** A provision must be recognized when, and only when: (1) an entity has a present obligation (legal or constructive) as a result of a past event; (2) it is probable (more likely than not) that an outflow of resources embodying economic benefits will be required; and (3) a reliable estimate can be made of the amount. Provisions are distinguished from other liabilities because there is uncertainty about timing or amount.

**Key rules:**
- **Three recognition criteria — all must be met:**
  - Present obligation (legal or constructive) arising from a past obligating event
  - Probable outflow (more likely than not > 50%)
  - Reliable estimate possible (only in extremely rare circumstances will this not be possible)
- **Legal vs. constructive obligation:**
  - Legal: arising from a contract, legislation, or other operation of law
  - Constructive: entity has created a valid expectation through established pattern of past practice, published policies, or specific statements
- **Measurement:**
  - Best estimate of expenditure required to settle the obligation at the reporting date
  - For a large population of items (e.g., warranty provisions): use expected value (probability-weighted average)
  - For a single obligation: use the most likely outcome, but consider other possible outcomes
  - Reflect risks and uncertainties; do not double-count
  - **Discount rate (IAS 37.47): a pre-tax rate that reflects current market assessments of the time value of money *and the risks specific to the liability*. It is not a risk-free rate.** Where the cash flow estimates have already been adjusted for a risk, the discount rate must not reflect that same risk again — the double-count prohibition. Discounting is required where the effect of the time value of money is **material** (IAS 37.45–46)
    - **Own credit risk is excluded in practice (IFRIC agenda decision, March 2011, annotated to IAS 37.47).** Asked whether "risks specific to the liability" brings in the entity's **own credit risk (performance risk)**, the Committee observed that **IAS 37 does not explicitly say**, but that the **predominant practice is to exclude** it, being generally viewed as a risk *of the entity* rather than a risk *specific to the liability*. It declined to add the issue, considering it better dealt with in the project to replace IAS 37. So the rate is risk-adjusted for **liability-specific** risk but **not** for the entity's own credit standing — the opposite of the IFRS 13 treatment of a liability's fair value. ED/2024/8 revisits precisely this diversity
    - Using a **risk-free** rate systematically **overstates** long-dated provisions — decommissioning, environmental — relative to what IAS 37.47 requires
  - Future events (e.g., new legislation, technological changes): taken into account only when there is sufficient objective evidence they will occur
  - Expected disposal of assets: NOT included in measurement of a provision (even if tied to the obligating event) — account for separately under IFRS 5/IAS 16
- **Reimbursements (IAS 37.53–57):**
  - Recognised as a **separate asset** when, and only when, it is **virtually certain** that reimbursement will be received **if the entity settles the obligation** — a conditional test, not an unconditional one (53)
  - The reimbursement asset is **capped at the amount of the provision** (53); any expected recovery above the provision is not recognised
  - **In the statement of comprehensive income the expense may be presented net of the reimbursement (54); the statement of financial position may not be netted.** Preparers routinely get this the wrong way round
  - **IAS 37.56:** where the entity **remains liable** if the third party fails to pay, the provision is the **full amount** with a separate reimbursement asset. **IAS 37.57:** where the entity would **not** be liable if the third party fails to pay, it has no liability for those costs and they are **not included in the provision at all**. The distinction turns on the legal position on counterparty default, and it is what determines gross versus nil presentation
- **Onerous contracts:**
  - A contract is onerous when the **unavoidable costs** of meeting the obligations exceed the economic benefits expected under it. Unavoidable costs are the **least net cost of exiting**, i.e. the **lower of** the cost of fulfilling the contract and any compensation or penalties for failing to fulfil it (IAS 37.68)
  - **The directly-related-cost approach (IAS 37.68A, the 2020 amendment, effective 1 January 2022):** the cost of fulfilling a contract comprises the costs that **relate directly to the contract**, being **both** (a) the **incremental** costs of fulfilling it — direct labour and materials — **and** (b) an **allocation of other costs that relate directly to fulfilling contracts**, for example an allocation of the depreciation charge on an item of PP&E used in fulfilling that contract and others. **This settled a long-running divergence: the incremental-cost-only view, which produced fewer onerous contracts, is no longer available.** Entities that previously used an incremental-only policy recognise more, and larger, onerous-contract provisions
  - **Sequencing (IAS 37.69):** before a separate onerous-contract provision is established, the entity recognises any **impairment loss on the assets used in fulfilling the contract** under IAS 36. Booking the provision first double-counts the loss
  - **Transition (IAS 37.94A):** applied to contracts for which the entity has **not yet fulfilled all its obligations** at the beginning of the annual period of first application; **comparatives are not restated**; the cumulative effect is an adjustment to opening retained earnings at the date of initial application
- **Restructuring provisions (IAS 37.72, 80–81):** a constructive obligation arises **only** when the entity has a **detailed formal plan** identifying at least the business or part concerned; the principal locations affected; the location, function and **approximate number of employees who will be compensated for terminating their services**; the expenditures that will be undertaken; and when the plan will be implemented — **and** has raised a valid expectation in those affected by starting to implement the plan or announcing its main features.
  - **IAS 37.80:** a restructuring provision includes **only the direct expenditures arising from the restructuring**, being those both **necessarily entailed by** the restructuring **and not associated with the ongoing activities** of the entity. **IAS 37.81** excludes retraining or relocating continuing staff, marketing, and investment in new systems and distribution networks
- **Future operating losses (IAS 37.63–65):** provisions **shall not** be recognised for future operating losses — they fail both the IAS 37.10 liability definition and the IAS 37.14 recognition criteria. An expectation of future operating losses is instead an **impairment indicator** for the assets of that operation, tested under IAS 36 (IAS 37.65).
- **Decommissioning, restoration and environmental rehabilitation:**
  - **Initial recognition:** the IAS 37 provision is the credit; the debit is capitalised into the cost of the PP&E under IAS 16 (or into the right-of-use asset under IFRS 16) — the obligation arising either on acquisition of the item or as a consequence of having used it in the period for purposes **other than producing inventories** in that period. Where the obligation arises from **producing inventories** in the period, the cost is an **inventory** cost, not a PP&E cost (IFRIC 1.1)
  - **Subsequent changes are governed by IFRIC 1** — see the block under IAS 16. In summary: a change in estimate or in the discount rate adjusts the **asset** under the cost model (IFRIC 1.5) or the **revaluation surplus** under the revaluation model (IFRIC 1.6); once the asset's useful life has ended, all further changes go to profit or loss (IFRIC 1.7); and **the unwinding of the discount is a finance cost in profit or loss and may not be capitalised under IAS 23 (IFRIC 1.8)**
  - **IFRIC 5 — decommissioning funds.** Applies where a contributor has an interest in a fund whose assets are **administered separately** (a separate legal entity, or segregated assets within another entity) **and** to which the contributor's right of access is **restricted** — both features are required (IFRIC 5.4). The contributor recognises its **obligation to pay decommissioning costs as a liability** and its **interest in the fund separately** — no netting — unless it is not liable to pay decommissioning costs even if the fund fails to pay (IFRIC 5.7), which is the same gross-versus-nil logic as IAS 37.56–57. It then tests for control, joint control or significant influence over the fund and, if present, consolidates or equity-accounts (IFRIC 5.8). **If not**, it recognises the right to receive reimbursement as an **IAS 37 reimbursement** (IFRIC 5.9), measured at the **lower of** the decommissioning obligation recognised and the contributor's share of the fair value of the fund's net assets attributable to contributors, with movements other than contributions and payments going to **profit or loss**. An obligation to make **additional contributions** — on another contributor's bankruptcy, or if fund assets fall short — is a **contingent liability**, and a liability is recognised only when additional contributions are **probable** (IFRIC 5.10)
  - **IFRIC 6 — waste electrical and electronic equipment.** A narrow scope: the recognition by **producers** of waste-management liabilities under the EU WE&EE Directive for **historical household equipment only** (IFRIC 6.6). The consensus is that the obligating event under **IAS 37.14(a)** is **participation in the market during the measurement period** — **not** the manufacture or sale of the equipment, and not the incurrence of waste-management costs. Consequently no liability arises as products are made or sold, and the timing of the obligating event may be wholly independent of the period in which the waste-management activity is performed (IFRIC 6.9). Where national legislation treats new household waste like historical household waste, the same principles apply via the IAS 8.10–12 hierarchy, which is also relevant to other regulations using a similar cost-attribution model (IFRIC 6.7). **IFRIC 6 has become a general-purpose precedent for cost-attribution environmental levies** — the Committee relied on it in the July 2022 *Negative Low Emission Vehicle Credits* agenda decision
- **Contingent liabilities:** Possible obligation (not probable) or present obligation not probable or cannot be reliably estimated — disclose in notes only; do NOT recognize on balance sheet
- **Contingent assets:** Possible assets from past events; disclose only when inflow is probable (more likely than not); do not recognize unless inflow is virtually certain (at which point it is no longer contingent)
- **Reviews:** Provisions must be reviewed at each reporting date and adjusted to reflect the current best estimate; if outflow is no longer probable, provision is reversed

**Disclosure requirements:**
- For each class of provision: carrying amount at start and end, additions, amounts used, reversals, discounting effect; description of nature, timing, amount uncertainty, and reimbursement rights
- For contingent liabilities: nature, estimate of financial effect, uncertainties, possibility of reimbursement
- For contingent assets: nature and estimate of financial effect (if probable)
- In extremely rare cases, information may be omitted if its disclosure would seriously prejudice the entity in a dispute — must disclose the general nature and reason for omission

**Common pitfalls:**
- Recognizing provisions for future operating losses (not permitted — no obligation until loss is incurred)
- Recognizing provisions based on management intent rather than past obligating events
- Netting reimbursements against provisions on the balance sheet
- Including future operating costs in restructuring provisions
- Failing to discount long-term provisions when the time value of money is material
- Recognizing contingent assets before inflow is virtually certain

- Discounting a provision at a **risk-free** rate. IAS 37.47 requires a rate reflecting the time value of money **and the risks specific to the liability**, without double-counting risks already in the cash flows — and, in predominant practice, **excluding the entity's own credit risk** (IFRIC, March 2011)
- Applying an **incremental-cost-only** test to onerous contracts; superseded by IAS 37.68A from 1 January 2022
- Recognising the onerous-contract provision **before** impairing the dedicated fulfilment assets (IAS 37.69)
- Recognising a reimbursement asset **in excess of the provision**, or netting it in the statement of financial position (IAS 37.53–54)
- Recognising a provision for a third party's costs the entity would **not** be liable for on that party's default (IAS 37.57)
- Including retraining, relocation of continuing staff, or new-system investment in a restructuring provision (IAS 37.80–81)
- Capitalising the unwinding of a decommissioning discount into the asset or into borrowing costs (IFRIC 1.8 prohibits it)
- Applying the ED/2024/8 proposals — including any assumption that **IFRIC 21 has been withdrawn** — before final amendments are issued

**Key interpretations:**
- **IFRIC 21 (Levies):** the obligating event for a levy imposed by a government is **the activity that triggers payment of the levy, as identified by the legislation** (IFRIC 21.8). Where the levy is calculated on a prior period's revenue but triggered by generating revenue in the current period, the current-period revenue generation is the obligating event. **Economic compulsion to continue operating does not create a constructive obligation for a future-period levy (21.9)**, and the going concern assumption does not imply a present obligation for such a levy (21.10). Where the obligating event occurs **over time**, the liability is recognised **progressively** (21.11); where it is the reaching of a **minimum threshold**, the liability is recognised when that threshold is reached (21.12). **The same recognition principles apply in interim reports as in annual financial statements (21.13)** — which is why many annual levies hit a single interim period in full rather than accruing evenly. A levy prepaid before the present obligation arises is recognised as an **asset** (21.14). **ED/2024/8 proposes to withdraw IFRIC 21 and IFRIC 6** and replace them with illustrative examples; **not yet effected — IFRIC 21 remains in force at 28 August 2026**.
- **IFRIC 1 (Changes in Existing Decommissioning, Restoration and Similar Liabilities)** — see the full treatment under IAS 16.
- **IFRIC 5 (Rights to Interests arising from Decommissioning, Restoration and Environmental Rehabilitation Funds)** and **IFRIC 6 (Liabilities arising from Participating in a Specific Market — Waste Electrical and Electronic Equipment)** — see the decommissioning block above.
- **Negative Low Emission Vehicle Credits (IFRIC agenda decision, July 2022)** — the reference analysis for any emissions, fuel-economy or environmental credit scheme settled in **credits rather than cash**. Where an entity producing or importing vehicles receives **negative** credits because average fleet emissions exceed a government target, and must eliminate them by obtaining and surrendering positive credits: there **is** an outflow of resources either way, because positive credits are resources embodying economic benefits; the **obligating event is the production or import, in a calendar year, of vehicles whose average emissions exceed the target** — reached by analogy to IFRIC 6 and IFRIC 21 — and it exists **independently of the entity's future actions**, which determine only the *means* of settlement. **A present obligation can arise at any date within the calendar year**, on the basis of activity to that date, not only at year end: that is the interim reporting trap. The measures **could** give rise to a **legal** obligation, since the obligation derives from an operation of law and the sanctions are the enforcement mechanism; the entity has a legal obligation **if accepting the possible sanctions is not a realistic alternative** for it, and if there is no legal obligation it must then consider whether a **constructive** obligation exists.

**Related standards:** IAS 10 (events after the reporting period — clarifying provisions), IAS 12 (income taxes — excluded; but the IAS 12.22A deferred tax gross-up attaches to decommissioning provisions), IAS 19 (employee benefits — excluded; termination benefits are recognised at the earlier of the IAS 19 and IAS 37 triggers), IFRS 9 (financial guarantee contracts are measured under IFRS 9.4.2.1(c), **not** at an IAS 37 amount), IFRS 3 (a contingent liability assumed in a business combination is recognised at fair value **even if an outflow is not probable** — IFRS 3.23), IFRS 15 (onerous contracts overlap; and IFRS 15.103 impairs contract assets first), IFRS 16 (lease reinstatement costs; onerous lease contracts), IAS 36 (assets impaired **before** an onerous-contract provision; and "committed to a restructuring" governs value in use), IAS 16 (the debit side of a decommissioning provision), **IFRIC 1**, **IFRIC 5**, **IFRIC 6** and **IFRIC 21**

---

## IAS 38 — Intangible Assets

**Scope:** Applies to intangible assets not covered by other standards. Key exclusions: financial assets (IFRS 9), exploration and evaluation assets (IFRS 6), mineral rights and reserves, intangible assets arising from insurance contracts (IFRS 17), deferred acquisition costs and intangible assets in the scope of IFRS 17. Goodwill from business combinations is within IFRS 3, not IAS 38.

**Status and amendments:** Issued March 2004 (replacing the 1998 IAS 38). ***Clarification of Acceptable Methods of Depreciation and Amortisation* (Amendments to IAS 16 and IAS 38)** — issued **12 May 2014**; effective for annual periods beginning on or after **1 January 2016**, applied **prospectively**; earlier application permitted; EU-endorsed 2 December 2015, OJ 3 December 2015. Added **IAS 38.98A–98C**. **IFRS 18** (effective 1 January 2027) makes consequential amendments. **No substantive IASB amendment to IAS 38 is currently open** — the practical development in this area has come through **IFRS Interpretations Committee agenda decisions**, below. An IASB **research** project on *Intangible Assets* was at "Decide Project Direction" with an expected date of 31 October 2026; at its July 2026 meeting the IASB was also developing a model for **customer accounting for intellectual property licensing contracts** alongside possible changes to the definition of an intangible asset. **No exposure draft, scope or timetable has been established for either.**

**Core principle:** An intangible asset is recognized when, and only when, it is probable that expected future economic benefits attributable to the asset will flow to the entity, and the cost can be measured reliably. The intangible asset must also meet the definition: it must be identifiable, non-monetary, and without physical substance.

**Key rules:**
- **Definition criteria:**
  - Identifiable: separable (capable of being separated and sold, transferred, licensed) OR arising from contractual or other legal rights
  - Controlled by the entity (power to obtain benefits and restrict others)
  - Future economic benefits attributable to the asset
- **Separate acquisition:** Presumed that the probability criterion is always satisfied; recognized at cost
- **Business combination:** Recognized at fair value at acquisition date (IFRS 3); probability criterion deemed satisfied; recognized separately from goodwill if definition met
- **Internally generated intangibles — the 6 capitalization criteria (development phase):** ALL must be met to capitalize:
  1. Technical feasibility of completing the intangible asset
  2. Intention to complete and use/sell it
  3. Ability to use or sell it
  4. How the asset will generate probable future economic benefits (e.g., existence of a market)
  5. Availability of adequate technical, financial, and other resources to complete development
  6. Ability to reliably measure expenditure attributable to the asset during development
  - The six criteria are **IAS 38.57(a)–(f)** and are **cumulative** — failure of any one means expensing. The requirement is that the entity can **demonstrate** all six; assertion is not demonstration, and criterion (e), the availability of adequate technical, financial and other resources to complete development, is the one most often asserted without contemporaneous evidence
  - **All research expenditure is expensed as incurred (IAS 38.54)**, because in the research phase an entity cannot demonstrate that an intangible asset exists that will generate probable future economic benefits (IAS 38.55)
  - **Internally generated goodwill shall not be recognised (IAS 38.48)** — it is not an identifiable resource, neither separable nor arising from contractual or legal rights, that can be measured reliably at cost (IAS 38.49)
  - **IAS 38.71 bars re-capitalising an item previously recognised as an expense.** Expenditure once expensed cannot be resurrected as an asset later
